	<item>
		<title>How LendingTree built a multi-agent mortgage assistant on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-lendingtree-built-a-multi-agent-mortgage-assistant-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Eric Hanson]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 05 Aug 2026 18:50:02 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e26b67888914f9285d58eac09de5504ddfbfc66b</guid>

					<description>Learn how LendingTree built a production multi-agent mortgage assistant on Amazon Bedrock. Three coordinated agents use LangGraph, the Model Context Protocol, and Amazon Nova models with built-in guardrails to deliver 24/7 personalized mortgage guidance while meeting strict financial-services compliance.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Buying a home is one of the biggest financial decisions most people face, and &lt;a href="https://www.lendingtree.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LendingTree&lt;/a&gt; built a multi-agent mortgage assistant on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; to make the process more straightforward. The assistant educates borrowers, understands their situation, and provides tailored options in a natural conversation. Borrowers must weigh purchase or refinance, conventional or government-backed, 15-year or 30-year terms, and fixed or adjustable rates. On top of that, there’s jargon like “discount points,” “origination fees,” and “debt-to-income ratio.” It’s no wonder many people feel lost before they even start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;LendingTree has been helping consumers sort through these choices for over 25 years, connecting millions of people with lenders to find competitive mortgage offers. The company was built on a belief that everyone deserves the tools and knowledge to make confident financial decisions. The &lt;a href="https://agent.lendingtree.com/chat" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AI-powered mortgage assistant&lt;/a&gt; is that next step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution had to meet the same standards that have guided LendingTree from day one: accurate information, transparent guidance, and rigorous protection of user data. Operating within the regulatory requirements of the mortgage industry means content filtering, personally identifiable information (PII) protection, and compliance oversight aren’t optional features. They’re non-negotiable. That made Amazon Bedrock and its built-in guardrails the right foundation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Our goal was to be a trusted partner in the home-buying journey, a guide that educates consumers, understands their situation, and matches them with the right offer. The foundation models and built-in guardrails in Amazon Bedrock let us deliver that with security and compliance from the start.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Srinivas Madabushi, SVP Technology, LendingTree&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many companies in the industry have added chatbots for basic questions. LendingTree wanted to go further, answering the hard questions and matching borrowers with competitive offers. That took more than one agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To address these challenges, LendingTree deployed three independent AI agents: a supervisor and two specialized workers (education and matching), coordinated using &lt;a href="https://www.langchain.com/langgraph" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt;, and foundation models powered by Amazon Bedrock. All agents run containerized services on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;LendingTree chose Amazon Bedrock for its multi-model flexibility and inherited AWS governance controls, which their compliance team required. The solution was deployed on Amazon ECS instead of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; because it was already in production when AgentCore reached general availability. AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows the high-level architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21123-1.png" alt="Multi-agent architecture: chat interface, Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, a Supervisor agent routing to Education and Matching workers, and shared services" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;LendingTree multi-agent architecture for mortgage guidance&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(1)&lt;/strong&gt; The consumer-facing chat interface is a React application running as a containerized service on Amazon ECS with Fargate, serving consumers on web and mobile browsers through a public endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(2)&lt;/strong&gt; User inputs and model outputs pass through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; for content filtering (such as hate and profanity detection) and PII redaction. Incoming messages are also screened for prompt threats.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In parallel with Guardrails, a safety classifier based on a large language model (LLM) enforces LendingTree’s conversational policy. The two safety checks run concurrently, so this added assurance doesn’t add latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A business-logic layer handles LendingTree’s operational rules: routing complex issues to human support and redirecting off-topic conversations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(3)&lt;/strong&gt; The Supervisor agent is the orchestrator, built on LangGraph as a state machine that follows a plan-and-execute pattern. It works like a well-organized team: the Supervisor understands the big picture and decides what needs to happen, while the workers handle what they’re each best at.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Supervisor is a graph of nodes and edges. Nodes do the work (intent analysis, execution planning, response composition), and edges decide what happens next based on the result (route to Education, route to Matching, or answer directly). This makes the flow explicit: every path through the system is defined, auditable, and traceable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Supervisor analyzes user intent with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Pro&lt;/a&gt; and produces an execution plan. It then routes to the appropriate worker over MCP using connection pooling for low-latency calls. For model availability by Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Not every task needs the most powerful model. The Supervisor implements multi-model architecture, automatically selecting between Amazon Nova Pro (for complex reasoning and critical classification) and Amazon Nova Lite (for conversational responses and lightweight classification) depending on what the task demands. This balances reliability with cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Planning and execution are separated by design. When a conversation goes wrong, the team can trace exactly which node made which decision.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(4)&lt;/strong&gt; The Education worker is the patient educator. It helps users understand mortgage concepts, explains product differences, and provides guidance through interactive conversation. It runs its own LangGraph workflow and maintains specialized &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, the fully managed retrieval augmented generation (RAG) capability, tailored to its domain, backed by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service&lt;/a&gt; as the vector store. Every response is grounded in real documents rather than relying on model knowledge alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(5)&lt;/strong&gt; The Matching worker is the connector. It gathers user preferences and calls LendingTree’s internal offer, eligibility, and rate APIs. From there, it delivers personalized lending options based on the user’s actual situation. It interprets qualification criteria and helps users compare options side by side.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(6)&lt;/strong&gt; Shared services handle conversation memory and session state through the LangGraph PostgreSQL checkpointer on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS)&lt;/a&gt;. This lets conversations persist across turns, agent handoffs, and service restarts. Users can pause, come back later, or ask follow-ups without losing context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(7)&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock provides a broad set of foundation models suited to a wide range of use cases. These range from complex reasoning and critical classification (Amazon Nova Pro) to cost-efficient conversational responses and lightweight classification (Amazon Nova Lite), all accessible through a single API. Built-in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; deliver configurable safety controls, while Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases connect agents to authoritative content through vector stores such as Amazon OpenSearch Service for hybrid retrieval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(8)&lt;/strong&gt; Internal LendingTree services and APIs provide lender search, rate and product data, eligibility and prequalification checks, offer comparison, and user profile information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(9)&lt;/strong&gt; All services run on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate, where each agent scales independently on its own demand signals. Infrastructure is managed through Terraform and deployed through GitLab continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines with automated testing and health checks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="following-a-conversation-end-to-end"&gt;Following a conversation end to end&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppose a user asks, &lt;em&gt;“What’s the difference between FHA and conventional, and which one fits me?”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The message flows through Amazon Bedrock Guardrails (2) for safety screening. The Supervisor agent (3) then loads conversation history from conversation memory (6) and analyzes intent, recognizing two needs: an explanation and a personalized comparison. It routes the educational question to the Education worker (4) over MCP, and the matching question to the Matching worker (5). Each worker operates independently and returns its result. The Supervisor aggregates both into one coherent reply, running the output back through Guardrails before delivering it. The full exchange is checkpointed to PostgreSQL (6), so the next turn picks up with complete context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To the user, it’s one continuous conversation. Behind the scenes, it’s a coordinated workflow where every decision point is traceable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment-and-operations"&gt;Deployment and operations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the agents communicate through MCP, each can be updated, scaled, and rolled back independently. The Education worker can ship a new knowledge base without touching the Supervisor or the Matching worker. Operationally, the challenge unique to multi-agent systems is tracing a single conversation that spans multiple services. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; logs and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/xray/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS X-Ray&lt;/a&gt; distributed tracing allow the team to follow a single conversation’s journey across all three agents, with per-agent metrics and detailed timing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assistant has been handling real mortgage conversations in production since late 2025. The metrics in this section are based on LendingTree’s internal production analytics from launch through Q1 2026, as reported by the LendingTree engineering team. Across that period, it served roughly 1,960 conversations and 12,100 messages, averaging 6.2 messages per exchange. These aren’t one-shot lookups: engaged users sustain multi-turn sessions averaging 10+ messages over 9 minutes, asking follow-up questions, refining their situation, and exploring options conversationally.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;From education to action.&lt;/strong&gt; The conversation data reveals how consumers actually use an AI mortgage assistant. Early in the rollout, 75% of conversations were educational. Users asked questions like “What is an FHA loan?”, “How does a HELOC work?”, or “What credit score do I need for a conventional loan?” As the system matured and word spread, transactional intent grew: over 50% of recent conversations now involve rate comparisons, lender matching, or prequalification, actions tied directly to conversion.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conversation depth signals trust.&lt;/strong&gt; The average session length of 6.2 messages tells one story. The engaged-user average of 10+ messages over 9 minutes tells a different one. Users don’t sustain that kind of engagement with a system they don’t find useful. The Education worker builds understanding, and users naturally progress to the Matching worker when they’re ready to act. No forced handoff, no “start over” moment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The questions reveal real complexity.&lt;/strong&gt; The most common topics include loan type comparisons (FHA, conventional, and VA), qualification criteria for specific credit profiles, rate negotiation strategies, closing timelines, and down payment trade-offs. These are exactly the situation-dependent questions that static FAQ pages can’t answer well. They require context: what the user said three turns ago, what they qualify for, what they’re optimizing for. This is where conversation memory and the Supervisor’s intent analysis earn their complexity, and the system maintains the thread so each answer builds on the last.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Containment.&lt;/strong&gt; Over 97% of conversations were handled end-to-end without human escalation, and only about 3% of users explicitly requested a live agent. For a regulated financial product where the questions are genuinely hard and getting it wrong has real consequences, that containment rate is significant. It demonstrates the system’s ability to operate as a self-contained advisory service rather than a triage layer in front of a call center.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the data confirms.&lt;/strong&gt; The engagement depth is the signal that matters most. Better-informed users make better decisions. A conversational interface surfaces preferences and constraints that a static form never captures in combination. Details like “I’m a veteran with a 650 credit score looking to buy in Colorado Springs in the next 30 days” make downstream lender matches more relevant. That’s ultimately what serves the consumer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-lendingtree-learned-and-whats-next"&gt;What LendingTree learned, and what’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Getting a multi-agent system to production taught the team as much about architecture as about any single feature.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After shipping the mortgage assistant, LendingTree realized that building one agent is straightforward, but scaling to many agents requires shared foundations. Rather than building every new feature as a one-off, LendingTree is investing in reusable pieces. These include shared context layers for data access, MCP contracts for tool integration, and standardized deployment processes. A registry lets teams discover existing capabilities instead of reinventing them. This lets teams build smaller, domain-focused capabilities that can be composed into broader consumer experiences. Consistent standards for safety, observability, evaluation, and reuse tie it all together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent design learnings:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Semantic chunking for Knowledge Bases.&lt;/strong&gt; Breaking documents into semantically coherent chunks rather than fixed-size chunks improved retrieval quality significantly, because chunk boundaries align with natural topic breaks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge Base conflict resolution.&lt;/strong&gt; With multiple KBs, contradictory information sometimes surfaced. Domain-based filtering and source prioritization solved this: internal LendingTree content takes precedence for product-specific questions, while external resources serve general mortgage education.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inter-agent context passing.&lt;/strong&gt; Worker agents initially lacked awareness of the broader conversation. Passing full conversation history and intent summaries in each MCP request gave workers the context they need to provide relevant responses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Query rewriting.&lt;/strong&gt; Short user responses like “not sure” or “yes” get rewritten into meaningful, searchable queries using conversation history before retrieval. This significantly improved retrieval quality.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrail tuning is ongoing work.&lt;/strong&gt; Early configurations blocked legitimate questions because mortgage terminology tripped content filters. Tuning against realistic conversation data resolved this.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task-based model routing&lt;/strong&gt; kept costs in check: Nova Pro only where reasoning demanded it, Nova Lite everywhere else.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These are agent design problems, not infrastructure problems. They remain important regardless of runtime choice, and represent the lasting knowledge from this implementation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure learnings:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conversation state management.&lt;/strong&gt; Early versions lost context during agent handoffs. A unified PostgreSQL-backed checkpointer with explicit state serialization solved this, allowing conversations to persist across agent transitions and system restarts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Running safety checks in parallel&lt;/strong&gt; preserved latency without weakening protection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Independent agent scaling&lt;/strong&gt; required hand-wiring ECS containers, health checks, and deployment pipelines per agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team is now evaluating re-architecting onto AgentCore to offload undifferentiated infrastructure work. That would avoid hand-wiring PostgreSQL checkpointers, ECS containers, and agent scaling, letting engineering effort shift from “keep the runtime alive” to “keep optimizing the agent logic and domain knowledge.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;LendingTree’s production multi-agent system demonstrates that AI agents can serve consumers in heavily regulated industries, not only as prototypes, but as production systems running 24/7. Amazon Bedrock (Nova Pro and Nova Lite, Knowledge Bases, and Guardrails) provided the model and safety foundation. LangGraph handled agent orchestration, and MCP handled agent-to-agent communication. Together, they deliver personalized mortgage guidance at scale while maintaining strict compliance standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For organizations building multi-agent systems: separate planning from execution so the system is debuggable. Make safety a structural pillar rather than a skippable step. Design from day one for the reusable capabilities that will let you scale the pattern across your organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with multi-agent systems on AWS, explore the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; for managed runtime capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="related-resources"&gt;Related resources&lt;/h2&gt;

		<item>
		<title>How Mobileye transformed support operations using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-mobileye-transformed-support-operations-using-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Adi Jabkowski]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 05 Aug 2026 18:09:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Lambda]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Secrets Manager]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">71e2b371359c204069dc89ff11b182c9b6470b7f</guid>

					<description>In this post, we'll explore how Mobileye deployed an AI support agentic solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore - from the support bottleneck that sparked the idea, through the proof of concept that validated it, to the hybrid architecture that bridges on-premises systems with AWS cloud services. This approach is relevant for enterprises struggling to scale AI Agents while maintaining enterprise grade governance and security standards.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;What if deploying production-grade AI agents required zero infrastructure management, came with enterprise observability built-in, and worked easily with your existing on-premises systems?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.mobileye.com/"&gt;Mobileye&lt;/a&gt;, the autonomous driving pioneer with more than 230 million EyeQ system-on-chips deployed across roughly 1,200 vehicle models worldwide, saw an opportunity to free skilled engineers from routine internal ticket status inquiries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Mobileye’s Data Collection Processing pipeline ingests thousands of drive-recording sessions daily, generating a constant stream of status inquiries from engineers and data teams. Each inquiry previously required manual steps across multiple systems – identifying sessions, cross-referencing visualization tools, validating outputs, and reviewing logs – before composing a response. Using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, Mobileye deployed an AI Support Agent that cut response times by 90% and exceeded 95% accuracy targets, with zero infrastructure overhead. The results were so compelling that Mobileye transformed AgentCore into a self-service platform for teams across the company to deploy their own AI agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we’ll explore how Mobileye deployed an AI support agentic solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore – from the support bottleneck that sparked the idea, through the proof of concept that validated it, to the hybrid architecture that bridges on-premises systems with AWS cloud services. This approach is relevant for enterprises struggling to scale AI Agents while maintaining enterprise grade governance and security standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-manual-bottleneck-to-ai-powered-support"&gt;From manual bottleneck to AI-powered support&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As Mobileye’s Data Collection pipeline scaled, 66% of support tickets became routine status inquiries requiring engineers to manually navigate 15 clicks across multiple backend systems. This time-consuming process diverted skilled engineers from complex issues while internal users faced longer wait times.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Traditional automation approaches – scripting, static workflows, and rule-based decision trees – proved inadequate. While these could handle predictable queries, they lacked the contextual understanding needed to interpret the variability of real-world support requests. The team chose to build an AI agent capable of understanding context and adapting to diverse inquiry patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validating-the-approach-proof-of-concept"&gt;Validating the approach: Proof of concept&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before full production rollout, Mobileye’s team conducted a proof of concept targeting 95% accuracy in ticket classification with sub-2-minute response times.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent uses Anthropic Claude foundation models, accessed through Mobileye’s internal LLM Gateway that provides governed, quota-managed access to foundation models on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/?trk=5d83343f-40c5-4167-8ec4-5b70bac566f7&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;trk=a3a1d5b1-eaa2-4ade-8305-ede5684ce2b6&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=Cj0KCQjwzqXQBhD2ARIsAKrIeU8vkPVSqjltCQs3bC26UGwaCmkOvh3ehg29TjuFN7YkKSN2UkIJpvwaAlyzEALw_wcB:G:s&amp;amp;s_kwcid=AL!4422!3!808746982981!e!!g!!amazon%20bedrock!23846236754!200318271070&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23846236754&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp-iaMgCRiTZQsuvNZVOgOlNe&amp;amp;gclid=Cj0KCQjwzqXQBhD2ARIsAKrIeU8vkPVSqjltCQs3bC26UGwaCmkOvh3ehg29TjuFN7YkKSN2UkIJpvwaAlyzEALw_wcB"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. The critical enabler was the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which gave the agent real-time access to the drive-data processing platform’s APIs -allowing it to query session status, retrieve processing logs, and pull diagnostic information during inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This meant the agent went far beyond simple ticket classification, becoming an intelligent investigator capable of handling inquiries that were previously resolved through time-consuming manual effort.&amp;nbsp; When users inquired about drive-recording sessions, the agent could determine current state and respond with answers: confirming completed sessions with access details, surfacing specific errors with debugging recommendations and log links for failures, or guiding users through submission processes for missing requests – all in under two minutes with no human intervention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="evaluating-the-options"&gt;Evaluating the options&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After successfully proving the concept with their AI agent, Mobileye needed to take it to production. This transition required a solution that could meet enterprise-grade requirements while maintaining the flexibility they had demonstrated in their proof of concept. After careful evaluation, they chose to deploy their AI Agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a fully managed platform to build, connect and optimize AI agents at scale with any framework or model. The decision came down to five key factors:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Serverless infrastructure ready for enterprise workloads&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accelerated time-to-resolution for support inquiries&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Comprehensive built-in observability&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Support for multiple agentic frameworks&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hybrid architecture support that could integrate easily with their existing on-premises ticketing system&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This last point was particularly important. Mobileye’s internal ticketing system operated on-premises and was inaccessible from AWS, so any solution would need to bridge that divide without compromising security or compliance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-production-architecture-bridging-two-worlds"&gt;The production architecture: Bridging two worlds&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Following the successful proof of concept, Mobileye moved to production with a sophisticated hybrid architecture featuring clearly defined component roles across two environments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136019 alignnone" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ml-20254-img1.png" alt="" width="979" height="447"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="on-premises-components"&gt;On-premises components&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table describes the on-premises components that handle local ticketing operations and bridge to cloud-based AI processing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Local Orchestrator&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Handles ticketing operations locally, extracting new tickets and posting completed responses back into the ticketing system.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Internal ticketing system&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The on-premises ticket management system serves as both the source of support tickets and the destination for AI-generated responses. Its inaccessibility from AWS was the primary driver behind the hybrid design.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-cloud-components"&gt;AWS cloud components&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table describes the AWS cloud components that provide serverless compute, security, and observability infrastructure for the AI agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Runtime&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mobileye runs their AI Support Agent on AgentCore’s serverless runtime, eliminating all infrastructure management. The team deploys and iterates without provisioning servers, benefits from automatic scaling to handle spikes in support volume and maintains full flexibility to use any agentic framework. A single API call invokes the agent – keeping integration with their existing systems straightforward.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Observability &lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mobileye’s engineering team uses built-in observability to trace every agent interaction end-to-end — from the initial request, through MCP tool calls to backend systems, to the final response. Detailed logs and tracebacks surface exactly how the agent reasoned, what data it retrieved, and where errors occur, dramatically reducing debugging time and accelerating iteration cycles.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mobileye uses Secrets Manager to enable faceless, secure authentication for their GenAI platform. Teams invoking AI agents never handle credentials directly – secrets required to interact with agents and backend systems are stored, rotated, and retrieved programmatically, enforcing enterprise security standards without adding friction for end users.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mobileye-service"&gt;Mobileye service&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table describes Mobileye’s own services that provide data connectivity and governed model access for the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data Pipeline Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Data Pipeline MCP server enables real-time querying of live production data during inference including driving session warming statuses, request progress, and processing errors – allowing the agent to deliver context-aware responses on the fly.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AI LLM Gateway&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mobileye’s governed, quota-managed access to foundation models on Amazon Bedrock and other LLM providers&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-all-works-together"&gt;How it all works together&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The automated workflow follows a streamlined four-step process:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Local Orchestrator&lt;/strong&gt; extracts new tickets and sends them to the &lt;strong&gt;AI Support Agent&lt;/strong&gt; running on &lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Runtime&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;AI Support Agent&lt;/strong&gt; processes each ticket end-to-end – categorizing the inquiry, querying live production systems via the &lt;strong&gt;MCP Server&lt;/strong&gt; for real-time session data, and accessing Claude through the &lt;strong&gt;AI LLM Gateway&lt;/strong&gt; – to generate a fully formatted response with links, recommendations, and actionable next steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Local Orchestrator&lt;/strong&gt; receives the completed response and posts it back to the &lt;strong&gt;Internal Ticketing System&lt;/strong&gt; with appropriate comments and labels.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/strong&gt; captures agent activity – session metrics, latency, token usage, and traces – for continuous monitoring and debugging&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="measurable-results-that-speak-for-themselves"&gt;Measurable results that speak for themselves&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The production deployment of Mobileye’s AI Support Agent on AgentCore delivered results that consistently met or surpassed initial targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Overall success rate: 98% (surpassing the original 95% target)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Response time: Reduced from hours to ~1 minute (90% improvement)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Support volume automated: 66% of total ticket volume&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Monthly throughput: 100+ tickets processed&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="strategic-benefits"&gt;Strategic benefits&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Support engineers are freed to focus on complex work – and the team didn’t have to build the infrastructure themselves, because AgentCore provides it out of the box.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Amazon Bedrock AgentCore enabled us to build a production-grade AI agent that delivers measurable business value while maintaining the security and operational standards our enterprise requires. The AI Support Agent has transformed our support operations, saving our engineers hundreds of hours previously spent on routine ticket lookups, and freed them to focus on what they do best.” &lt;/em&gt;— Shay Margalit, Principal Engineer, Mobileye&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scaling-the-vision-from-single-agent-to-enterprise-platform"&gt;Scaling the vision: From single agent to enterprise platform&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the AI Support Agent delivering results in production, Mobileye faced a new challenge: most developers across the organization lacked the AWS credentials or infrastructure access to deploy agents on AgentCore independently. To address this, the Mobileye Cloud Infra team built an internal agent deployment platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This internal managed service enables Mobileye developers to deploy production-grade AI agents without AWS expertise or cloud credentials. The process follows these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Teams provide their agent code&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Teams specify needed AgentCore capabilities – Memory, Browser Tool, Code Interpreter, Observability, Gateway.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Cloud Infra team provisions all infrastructure: AWS IAM Roles, Amazon S3 storage, Amazon CloudWatch monitoring, and Amazon Cognito authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Developers receive a pre-configured &lt;code&gt;bedrock_agentcore.yaml&lt;/code&gt; file that integrates directly into their project.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deployment is a single command: &lt;code&gt;agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The provisioned infrastructure aligns with Mobileye’s standards for security, cost governance, and operational excellence. CloudWatch Alarms on code exceptions help verify issues surface immediately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What began as a single proof-of-concept has evolved into an enterprise-wide platform where teams can deploy secure, monitored, cost-tracked agents in minutes rather than weeks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The path from manual processes to intelligent automation is shorter than you think, and the results, as Mobileye has shown, can be extraordinary.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How we built an MCP bridge to give our AgentCore-hosted AI agent access to local MCP tools</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-we-built-an-mcp-bridge-to-give-our-agentcore-hosted-ai-agent-access-to-local-mcp-tools/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rohan Lekhwani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 05 Aug 2026 18:02:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">f2de6d336934f855258e3aa9bbca0a0efc1a8dcd</guid>

					<description>AI agents on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore run in the cloud, but users' tools and files live on their laptops. Learn how to build a secure MCP bridge that lets a cloud-hosted agent call local MCP servers by tunneling signed messages over the existing WebSocket connection through a browser extension and Chrome native messaging, with no open ports or VPN required.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Our agent runs in the cloud, but our users’ spreadsheets live on their laptops. How do you bridge that gap?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt; is an open source standard introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 to standardize how AI models connect to external data and tools. MCP follows a client-server architecture where an MCP host, an AI application like &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; or Claude Code, establishes connections to one or more MCP servers. The MCP protocol supports two transport mechanisms: stdio (standard I/O for communication between local processes on the same machine) and streamable HTTP transport (HTTP-based communication between remote servers and clients). A missing piece is when the MCP server exists locally and the MCP client is remote.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern matters for financial managers and analysts who primarily work with Excel and local files. They can use centrally deployed AI agents to act on those files while also drawing context from their browser. This is the same pattern that powers products like &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/product/claude-cowork" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Cowork&lt;/a&gt;, a cloud agent calling local tools through MCP, but fully self-hosted on AWS with your own model and custom tool servers. Internally, we built a production-grade AI assistant for finance that has seen over 41,000 conversations within a year since launch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we recreate what we built internally in a simplified form. Our agent, deployed on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, uses MCP servers that run on a user’s local machine. We bridge the gap between the remote MCP client and the local MCP server by tunneling MCP messages over WebSocket and native messaging. We discuss additional production hardening measures in the What’s Next section. The complete source code is available on &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-mcp-bridge-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21026-1.jpg" alt="MCP Bridge Demo extension side panel summarizing a local Excel workbook" width="800" height="2234"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The MCP Bridge Demo extension summarizing a local Excel workbook. The cloud-hosted agent reads the file directly from the user’s machine through the MCP bridge and streams a structured summary back to the side panel&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, anchors an architecture with four components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore runtime:&lt;/strong&gt; Hosts the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands agent&lt;/a&gt; in the cloud. The agent acts as the MCP client, issuing tool discovery and tool invocation requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Browser extension:&lt;/strong&gt; Provides the chat interface and acts as a bidirectional relay, forwarding MCP messages between the AgentCore runtime (over WebSocket) and the MCP Bridge (over native messaging).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP Bridge:&lt;/strong&gt; A &lt;a href="https://gofastmcp.com/v2/servers/proxy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;FastMCP proxy&lt;/a&gt; running on the user’s local machine, spawned by the browser through the native messaging host registration. It translates between the native messaging envelope format and raw MCP JSON-RPC.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP Server:&lt;/strong&gt; A standard MCP server running locally. Because the bridge is co-located, communication uses the stdio transport.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the end-to-end message flow. The user sends a message through the extension, which connects to the AgentCore runtime over a presigned WebSocket. When the Strands agent needs to call a tool, it sends an MCP JSON-RPC request wrapped in a JSON envelope back through the WebSocket to the extension. The extension relays the message as-is to the bridge through native messaging. The bridge unwraps the envelope, extracts the JSON-RPC content, and forwards it to the MCP server over stdio. The response travels the reverse path. The bridge wraps the unmodified MCP server response back into an envelope and relays it through the extension to the AgentCore runtime, where the agent consumes the tool result and continues generation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21026-2.jpg" alt="Architecture diagram showing message flow between the AgentCore runtime, browser extension, MCP Bridge, and local MCP server" width="800" height="493"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;High-level architecture diagram showing all components. The browser extension and MCP Bridge act as relays that wrap and unwrap JSON messages from the AgentCore runtime and JSON-RPC messages from the MCP server&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table shows a single tool call as it travels from the agent to the MCP server, with each hop stripping one layer of wrapping:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hop&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sender → Receiver&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Message&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent -&amp;gt; Extension (WebSocket)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;{“type”: “mcpbridge”, “content”: {“type”: “mcp”, “payload”: “”}, “session_id”: “session_123”}&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Extension -&amp;gt; Bridge (Native Messaging)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;{“type”: “mcp”, “payload”: “”}&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bridge → MCP Server (stdio)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;{“jsonrpc”: “2.0”, “id”: 1, “method”: “tools/call”, “params”: {“name”: “read_sheet”, “arguments”: {“file_path”: “budget.xlsx”}}}&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-does-the-strands-agent-work-in-agentcore-runtime"&gt;How does the Strands agent work in AgentCore runtime&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;WebSocket connection:&lt;/strong&gt; The browser extension connects to the AgentCore runtime over a presigned WebSocket URL. On startup, the side panel sends a presign request through the background script to the native bridge, which uses the user’s local AWS credentials and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/bedrock-agentcore-sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;bedrock-agentcore software development kit (SDK)&lt;/a&gt; to generate a SigV4-signed &lt;code&gt;wss://&lt;/code&gt; URL scoped to the deployed runtime ARN (valid for 5 minutes). The side panel opens a WebSocket to that URL. No credentials ever leave the user’s machine or enter the browser. If the connection drops because of URL expiry or network interruption, the side panel automatically requests a fresh presigned URL after 2 seconds and reconnects, making the expiry window invisible to the user during normal use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP initialization:&lt;/strong&gt; Before discovering tools, the agent performs the standard &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2025-03-26/basic/lifecycle#initialization" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP initialization handshake&lt;/a&gt;. It sends an &lt;code&gt;initialize&lt;/code&gt; request with the protocol version, waits for the server’s capabilities response, and then sends a &lt;code&gt;notifications/initialized&lt;/code&gt; notification. Only after this handshake completes does the server accept &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; requests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool discovery:&lt;/strong&gt; On each user message, the agent calls &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; and receives an array of tool schemas. It wraps each schema into a &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/api/python/strands.types.tools/#agenttool" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands AgentTool&lt;/a&gt; whose &lt;code&gt;stream()&lt;/code&gt; method sends a &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; request through the bridge. Tools added to the MCP server are automatically available on the next request with no agent code changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request-response correlation:&lt;/strong&gt; Each outbound JSON-RPC request from the agent is assigned a unique ID and registered against an &lt;code&gt;asyncio.Future&lt;/code&gt; keyed by &lt;code&gt;(session_id, jsonrpc_id)&lt;/code&gt;. When the response arrives back over the WebSocket, it is matched to the waiting Future and resolved. This allows multiple tool calls to be in flight concurrently without ambiguity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-does-native-messaging-work"&gt;How does native messaging work&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We need the extension to talk to a long-running local process without network permissions or per-message user prompts. Native messaging provides exactly this. Both &lt;a href="https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/develop/concepts/native-messaging" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chrome&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Mozilla/Add-ons/WebExtensions/Native_messaging" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Firefox&lt;/a&gt; support native messaging for their extensions. The browser looks for a manifest file at a well-known location on the user’s machine that specifies which binary to launch. The native messaging manifest file for Chrome on macOS is as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;# Stored at ~/Library/Application\ Support/Google/Chrome/NativeMessagingHosts/com.example.mcp_bridge.json
{
  "name": "com.example.mcp_bridge",
  "description": "MCP Bridge - Routes MCP messages to local servers",
  "path": "/path/to/mcp-bridge-demo/bridge/run_bridge.sh",
  "type": "stdio",
  "allowed_origins": [
    "chrome-extension://&amp;lt;extension_id&amp;gt;/"
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On extension startup, the background script calls &lt;code&gt;chrome.runtime.connectNative("com.example.mcp_bridge")&lt;/code&gt; to launch the native app locally. The &lt;code&gt;run_bridge.sh&lt;/code&gt; script referenced in the manifest activates the Python environment and starts the bridge:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;#!/bin/bash
cd "/path/to/mcp-bridge-demo/bridge"
source .venv/bin/activate
exec python3 bridge.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The native messaging host process stays alive for the lifetime of the connection. Each message is serialized as JSON, UTF-8 encoded, and preceded with a 32-bit message length in little-endian byte order. The maximum size of a single message from the native messaging host is 1 MB (to protect the browser from misbehaving native applications). The maximum size of a message sent to the native messaging host is 64 MiB.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-does-the-mcp-bridge-work"&gt;How does the MCP Bridge work&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP Bridge acts as a protocol translator between two worlds: Chrome’s native messaging protocol on one side and the MCP standard (JSON-RPC 2.0 over stdio) on the other. On the inbound path, it strips the 4-byte length header from stdin, parses the JSON body, and unwraps the envelope to extract the raw JSON-RPC message. On the outbound path, it does the reverse: wraps the JSON-RPC response in an envelope and writes it back with the length header. The JSON-RPC content itself passes through untouched.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Internally, the bridge runs two concurrent loops connected through a FastMCP proxy. The main loop reads messages from the browser, unwraps them, and places the JSON-RPC content onto an input queue. The FastMCP proxy, started once and kept alive for the bridge’s lifetime, picks messages off this queue, forwards them to the MCP server subprocess over its stdin, and places responses from the server’s stdout onto an output queue. A second background loop reads from the output queue, wraps each response back into an envelope, and writes it to stdout for the browser to receive. This two-loop design decouples the browser’s request timing from the MCP server’s processing speed so the bridge does not block waiting for a slow tool to finish before accepting the next request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP server itself is a child process spawned by the bridge on startup, configured through a &lt;code&gt;mcp.json&lt;/code&gt; file. It stays running for the bridge’s lifetime with no per-request process overhead. Adding a new MCP server is a one-line config change. The bridge handles the plumbing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;# mcp.json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "excel": {
      "command": "python3",
      "args": ["excel_server.py"]
    }
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21026-3.jpg" alt="Internal architecture of the MCP Bridge with I/O queues and a FastMCP proxy forwarding to the local MCP server" width="800" height="562"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Internal architecture of the MCP Bridge. The MCP Bridge translates messages from the browser extension into the MCP protocol for the MCP server. I/O queues work with a FastMCP proxy server to forward messages to the locally running MCP server and relay messages back to the browser extension&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following prerequisites are needed to deploy and test the MCP bridge solution. These cover the browser extension, the agent deployed on AgentCore, the MCP Bridge, and a sample Excel MCP server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-account-and-permissions"&gt;AWS account and permissions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS account with Bedrock model access enabled (the code uses Claude Opus 4.7). Model availability varies by Region. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model availability&lt;/a&gt; documentation for the current list.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions for Bedrock AgentCore (&lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore:*&lt;/code&gt;), AWS CloudFormation, IAM role creation, and S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) configured with credentials (&lt;code&gt;aws sts get-caller-identity&lt;/code&gt; to verify).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) bootstrapped in your target Region (&lt;code&gt;cdk bootstrap&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tools-and-software"&gt;Tools and software&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10+.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Node.js 20+ (for the AgentCore command line interface (CLI)).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Google Chrome (Manifest V3 side panel support).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Git.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="install"&gt;Install&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore CLI: &lt;code&gt;npm install -g @aws/agentcore&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS CDK: &lt;code&gt;npm install -g aws-cdk&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="estimated-time-and-cost"&gt;Estimated time and cost&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Setup: ~15 minutes (deploy, install extension, and register bridge).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore runtime: pay-per-invocation (no idle cost).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Bedrock model usage: standard per-token pricing for Claude.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Other components run locally at no additional cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploying-the-solution"&gt;Deploying the solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Clone the repository.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-mcp-bridge-agentcore.git
cd mcp-bridge-demo&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Install Python dependencies.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;chmod +x scripts/setup.sh manifests/install.sh
./scripts/setup.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create and deploy the agent to AgentCore.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npm install -g @aws/agentcore
cd agent
agentcore create --name McpBridgeAgent --defaults
cd McpBridgeAgent
cp ../agent.py app/McpBridgeAgent/main.py
cp ../mcp_bridge_transport.py app/McpBridgeAgent/
agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Note the runtime Amazon Resource Name (ARN) from the output (or run &lt;code&gt;agentcore status&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configure the bridge.&lt;/strong&gt;Edit &lt;code&gt;bridge/bridge_config.json&lt;/code&gt; with your runtime ARN: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "runtime_arn": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:runtime/&amp;lt;your-runtime&amp;gt;",
    "region": "us-east-1",
    "presign_expires": 300
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;The bridge uses your local AWS credentials to generate presigned WebSocket URLs automatically, with no manual token management needed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Load the Chrome extension.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Navigate to &lt;code&gt;chrome://extensions&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Turn on &lt;strong&gt;Developer mode&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Load unpacked&lt;/strong&gt;, and then select the &lt;code&gt;extension/&lt;/code&gt; directory.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Note the extension ID displayed on the card.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Register the native messaging bridge.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./manifests/install.sh &amp;lt;your-extension-id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;This creates a launcher script and registers it with Chrome so the browser can spawn the bridge process.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="testing-the-solution"&gt;Testing the solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Restart the browser and choose the extension icon to open the side panel. The extension automatically requests a presigned URL from the bridge, connects to AgentCore, and discovers available MCP tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 1122px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21026/figure4.gif" alt="" width="1112" height="720"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The browser extension connects to the MCP bridge over native messaging and relays MCP messages between the AgentCore-hosted agent and the side panel, as shown in the extension’s DevTools console&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Additional queries to experiment with:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;"Create a workbook called budget.xlsx with sheets Q1 and Q2"

"Write 'Revenue' in cell A1 of the Q1 sheet in budget.xlsx"

"Read the data from budget.xlsx"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-considerations"&gt;Security considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post prioritizes demonstrating the MCP bridge functionality and therefore limits security measures to the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native messaging origin restriction:&lt;/strong&gt; Chrome checks the calling extension’s ID against the &lt;code&gt;allowed_origins&lt;/code&gt; list in the native messaging manifest and rejects connections from extensions not explicitly listed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Presigned URL expiration:&lt;/strong&gt; WebSocket URLs are SigV4-signed and expire after 5 minutes. Credentials remain on the user’s machine and are not sent to the browser.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Process isolation:&lt;/strong&gt; The extension, bridge, and MCP server each run in separate operating system processes with no shared memory.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The primary exposure surface unique to this architecture is the bridge itself. It accepts instructions from a cloud-hosted agent and runs them locally with the user’s file system permissions. The key principle is that the agent should not have more access than the user explicitly grants, and the user can always see what tools were invoked and with what arguments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a production system, in addition to implementing &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; for content filtering, we recommend the following additional security measures:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What to add&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Authentication&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Require a JSON Web Token (JWT) handshake as the first WebSocket frame. Verify the token (for example, through Amazon Cognito and an HMAC secret in AWS Systems Manager) before accepting messages.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Helps prevent unauthorized use even if a presigned URL is leaked.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Payload signing&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sign every MCP message with Ed25519 (private key on the agent, public key on the bridge). Reject unsigned or tampered payloads.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Makes sure messages were not modified in transit between cloud and local machine.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;File system scoping&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Configure an explicit allowlist of directories the MCP server can access. Reject paths outside the boundary.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Helps prevent a prompt injection from tricking the agent into reading sensitive files (SSH keys, credentials).&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Audit logging&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Log every tool invocation (tool name, arguments, timestamp, result status) to a local file.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Provides traceability for what the agent did on the user’s machine.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you’re done experimenting, remove the deployed resources to avoid ongoing charges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Remove the AgentCore deployment.&lt;/strong&gt; Tear down the CloudFormation stack, IAM roles, and runtime resources. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd agent/McpBridgeAgent
agentcore remove all
agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unregister the native messaging bridge.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;rm ~/Library/Application\ Support/Google/Chrome/NativeMessagingHosts/com.example.mcp_bridge.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Remove the Chrome extension.&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Navigate to &lt;code&gt;chrome://extensions&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Remove&lt;/strong&gt; on the MCP Bridge Demo card.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delete local files (optional).&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;rm -rf mcp-bridge-demo/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our internal production solution includes the capabilities that follow. The MCP Bridge acts as the AgentCore-hosted agent’s window into the user’s local system. Combined with the browser extension, the current architecture can be extended to support more use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="browser-actions"&gt;Browser actions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The browser extension can implement its own browser tools modeled on &lt;a href="https://github.com/microsoft/playwright/tree/main/packages/playwright-core/src/tools/backend" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Playwright MCP’s tool definitions&lt;/a&gt; and expose them to the agent to perform actions such as clicking elements, filling forms, navigating pages, and taking screenshots.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the extension sits as a message relay between the AgentCore agent and the MCP Bridge, it can intercept &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; MCP messages, inject the browser tools into the tool list, and handle their execution locally.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="local-tool-automation"&gt;Local tool automation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bridge speaks standard MCP JSON-RPC over stdio, so MCP servers that use the stdio transport are compatible without modification. Add an entry to &lt;code&gt;mcp.json&lt;/code&gt;. Examples: &lt;a href="https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/servers/tree/main/src/filesystem" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;filesystem access&lt;/a&gt; for sandboxed file read/write, &lt;a href="https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/servers/tree/main/src/git" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Git&lt;/a&gt; for repository operations, &lt;a href="https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/servers/tree/main/src/memory" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;memory&lt;/a&gt; for persistent local knowledge graphs. See &lt;a href="https://github.com/punkpeye/awesome-mcp-servers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awesome-mcp-servers&lt;/a&gt; for a directory of available servers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="packaging-the-bridge-as-a-standalone-binary"&gt;Packaging the bridge as a standalone binary&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production distribution, we use PyInstaller to package the bridge into a standalone binary that bundles the Python runtime, dependencies, and configuration into a single executable. The native messaging manifest points directly at this binary, so users do not need to install Python or manage virtual environments. The bridge works on first extension launch with no extra setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we built an MCP bridge that connects a cloud-hosted Strands agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to local MCP tool servers running on a user’s machine. Using a browser extension as the relay layer and Chrome’s native messaging as the local transport, we tunneled standard MCP JSON-RPC messages between the cloud and the user’s file system without exposing credentials to the browser or modifying the MCP protocol itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this pattern, you can keep your agent centrally deployed and managed while giving it access to tools that must run locally, such as Excel files, Git repositories, or other locally running MCP servers. The architecture is extensible. You can add new tools with a one-line config change, layer in browser actions through the extension, or package the bridge as a standalone binary for frictionless distribution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, deploy the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-mcp-bridge-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt; and experiment with your own MCP servers. For more on the services used in this post, explore the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol specification&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Thanks to Daniel Sheng Sun, Markus Hueck, Nishant Bisen, Shraddha Kabade, and Stacy Kim for their contributions to the internal production system that inspired this post.&lt;/p&gt;
