					<description>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore harness is now generally available. Learn how to add it as an agent step in n8n workflows using a new open-source community node, and build agents with persistent memory, real tools, code execution, and VPC isolation — all from the n8n editor with no infrastructure or agent code.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you build and automate workflows in &lt;a href="https://n8n.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;n8n&lt;/a&gt; without writing much code, you’ve probably used its AI Agent node to add a model call to a workflow. It’s a great start. But a production agent needs more than a single model call: memory that lasts beyond one run, tools it can actually use like a browser or a code sandbox, and room to work through longer tasks. Building that scaffolding yourself is the hard part.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. AgentCore harness, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, is now generally available and provides that scaffolding for you. A new open-source community node brings it into n8n’s visual editor. You get production agents with persistent memory, real tools, and the model provider of your choice, without writing infrastructure or agent code. You’re not tied to one model either. The node works with Amazon Bedrock, OpenAI, Google Gemini, and LiteLLM-supported providers, and you can switch providers between turns of the same conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you will install the node, &lt;a href="https://www.npmjs.com/package/@aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;span class="citation" data-cites="aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore"&gt;@aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and build agents step by step in the n8n editor. You will start with an agent that remembers a conversation, scope memory to individual users, add a code interpreter tool, give the agent skills, and finish by running an agent privately in your own virtual private cloud (VPC). The node is open source under the MIT license, and AgentCore harness is powered by &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt;, the open source agent framework from AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-the-node-does"&gt;What the node does&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An agent is more than a model. The model reasons, but the harness does the work around it: it runs the orchestration loop, calls tools, manages the context window, keeps state across turns, recovers from failures, and isolates each session. Building that layer is where most teams spend their time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore harness gives you that layer as a managed capability. You define an agent in configuration, including the model it uses, the tools it calls, the skills it has access to, and the instructions it follows, and AgentCore assembles and runs the loop for you. Each session runs in its own isolated environment with a filesystem and shell, memory across sessions, and web browsing. When configuration isn’t enough, you can export the harness to Strands code and keep running on the same system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;n8n’s built-in AI Agent node is a good fit for a single model call in a workflow. The community node goes further by exposing the full harness in the n8n editor. It has one operation with a single deciding field, &lt;strong&gt;Harness ARN&lt;/strong&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Leave it blank and the node creates an agent for you on the first run, reuses it on later runs, and updates it when your configuration changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Paste an existing harness ARN and the node invokes that agent directly, so you can use agents you created outside n8n.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The node uses the same AWS credential pattern as the existing &lt;a href="https://docs.n8n.io/integrations/builtin/app-nodes/n8n-nodes-base.awslambda" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;n8n AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.n8n.io/integrations/builtin/app-nodes/n8n-nodes-base.awss3" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) nodes&lt;/a&gt;, so it feels familiar if you already automate AWS services in n8n.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, make sure you have the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;An n8n instance.&lt;/strong&gt; You can run n8n either as a self-hosted install or on n8n Cloud. Both work with this node. If you’re new to n8n, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.n8n.io/deploy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;n8n deployment documentation&lt;/a&gt; to choose an option and set up your instance, then complete the initial sign-in so you land in the editor where you will build the workflows in this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The node installed. &lt;code&gt;@aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore&lt;/code&gt; is a verified community node, so you can find it directly in the n8n nodes panel. In the editor, add a node, search for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and select it — n8n installs it for you. (You can also install it from Settings, Community Nodes, Install by entering &lt;code&gt;@aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore&lt;/code&gt;.) This post uses node version 0.3.&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-1.png" alt="n8n nodes panel with the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore community node in the search results" width="800"&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to AgentCore harness in a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS credentials for the caller (the identity whose keys go in the n8n credential) with the harness caller permissions, plus a separate &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management&lt;/a&gt; (IAM) execution role that the harness assumes at runtime. AWS maintains the least-privilege reference policies in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/harness-security.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore harness security documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;node README&lt;/a&gt; maps each feature to the permissions to add.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; Where you can, use temporary credentials from AWS IAM Identity Center or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Security Token Service&lt;/a&gt; (AWS STS), and follow the principle of least privilege when you configure IAM permissions. Don’t commit credentials to source control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note on cost&lt;/strong&gt;: The AgentCore harness, the managed memory store it provisions, and (if you use it) VPC endpoints are billed AWS resources. Follow the Clean up section at the end to remove what you create for this walkthrough, and refer to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt; for pricing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-credential"&gt;Set up the credential&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You configure the AWS credential once, the same way you would for other AWS nodes in n8n.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In n8n, go to &lt;strong&gt;Credentials&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;New&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for and select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-2.png" alt="n8n new-credential dialog with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API selected from the credential type list" width="800"&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter your &lt;strong&gt;Access Key ID&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;Secret Access Key&lt;/strong&gt;, and a &lt;strong&gt;Session Token&lt;/strong&gt; if you use temporary credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your &lt;strong&gt;Region&lt;/strong&gt; and enter the &lt;strong&gt;Execution Role ARN&lt;/strong&gt; that the harness assumes at runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Test&lt;/strong&gt; to confirm the connection, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Save&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-3.png" alt="Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API credential form in n8n with access key, Region, and execution role ARN, and a successful test" width="800" height="540"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API credential in n8n, showing the access key, Region, and execution role ARN, with a successful connection test&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="build-your-first-agent-and-give-it-memory"&gt;Build your first agent and give it memory&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this walkthrough, you build an agent that remembers details across turns of a conversation. Memory is on by default, so the node provisions a managed memory store for you and there’s nothing extra to configure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add a manual trigger to a new workflow, add the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt; node after it and attach your credential.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Leave &lt;strong&gt;Harness ARN&lt;/strong&gt; blank so the node creates and reuses the agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter an &lt;strong&gt;Agent Name&lt;/strong&gt;, such as &lt;code&gt;travel_concierge&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set &lt;strong&gt;Model Provider&lt;/strong&gt; to &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt; and enter a &lt;strong&gt;Model ID&lt;/strong&gt;, such as a Claude model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a &lt;strong&gt;System Prompt&lt;/strong&gt;, such as &lt;em&gt;You are a warm, concise travel concierge&lt;/em&gt;, and a &lt;strong&gt;Session ID&lt;/strong&gt;, such as &lt;code&gt;demo-travel-session&lt;/code&gt;. Reusing this session ID is what continues the conversation on later runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a &lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt; for the first turn, such as “I love warm beaches and I’m vegetarian. Note that for me.”, and run the node.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first run takes about 30-60 seconds while AWS provisions the agent. The output includes the agent’s response, token usage, and a summary of what the node provisioned, including the memory store it created for you.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-4.png" alt="Turn 1 node output with the agent response and a harness summary confirming managed memory and a provisioned memory ARN" width="800" height="1010"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Turn 1. The node output shows the agent’s response and a harness summary that confirms managed memory with a provisioned memory ARN.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now change only the &lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt; to &lt;em&gt;Suggest one destination and one dish I’d enjoy, based on what you know about me&lt;/em&gt;, keep the same session ID, and run again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-5.png" alt="Turn 2 node output where the agent recalls the warm-beaches and vegetarian preferences from turn 1" width="800" height="1010"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Turn 2. The agent recalls the preferences from turn 1, warm beaches and vegetarian, because the conversation persisted through the same session ID.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The output field &lt;code&gt;sessionSource&lt;/code&gt; reads &lt;code&gt;provided&lt;/code&gt; when you supply a session ID, and the input token count rises on turn 2 because the node loads the prior conversation before the agent reasons. If you leave the session ID blank, each run starts a new conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scope-memory-per-user-with-an-actor-id"&gt;Scope memory per user with an actor ID&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When one agent serves many people, you can keep each person’s memory separate with an &lt;strong&gt;Actor ID&lt;/strong&gt;. Memory is scoped by actor and session, so different actors get isolated histories from the same agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The scoping is a hierarchy: the agent holds the shared configuration, the Actor ID isolates one user’s memory from another’s, and the Session ID isolates individual conversations within an actor. One actor can have many sessions. A different actor with the same session ID still gets its own separate memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use an agent such as &lt;code&gt;team_assistant&lt;/code&gt; with managed memory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Additional Options&lt;/strong&gt;, set &lt;strong&gt;Actor ID&lt;/strong&gt; to a per-user value, such as &lt;code&gt;user-alice&lt;/code&gt;, and set a &lt;strong&gt;Session ID&lt;/strong&gt; for that user.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a first turn, such as &lt;em&gt;Remember my project is codenamed Aurora.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Change the prompt to &lt;em&gt;What’s my project codename?&lt;/em&gt; and run again with the same actor and session.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-6.png" alt="Node configuration panel with Actor ID and Session ID set for a specific user" width="800" height="460"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The node configured with an actor ID and session ID for a specific user.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-7.png" alt="Turn 1 output for actor user-alice storing the project codename preference" width="800" height="1010"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Turn 1 for the actor user-alice, which stores a preference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-8.png" alt="Turn 2 output where the agent returns the stored codename for actor user-alice" width="800" height="1010"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Turn 2 for the same actor. The agent returns the value it stored for this user. A different actor ID keeps its own separate memory.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="add-a-tool-run-code-in-a-sandbox"&gt;Add a tool: run code in a sandbox&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents become far more capable when they can use tools. In this walkthrough, you give the agent a code interpreter that runs code in a sandboxed environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use an agent such as &lt;code&gt;data_analyst&lt;/code&gt; with a Claude model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;System Prompt&lt;/strong&gt;, instruct the agent to write and run code to answer, then report the result.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on &lt;strong&gt;Add Tools, &lt;/strong&gt;then under &lt;strong&gt;Tools&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Tool&lt;/strong&gt; and set &lt;strong&gt;Type&lt;/strong&gt; to &lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Code Interpreter&lt;/strong&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a &lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt; that requires computation, such as “Generate 500 random exam scores between 0 and 100, then report the mean, median, and standard deviation.”, and run the node.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136647" style="width: 874px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136647" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136647" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ml-21089-fig7.png" alt="" width="864" height="458"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136647" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The agent writes and runs code in the sandbox and returns computed results for the mean, median, and standard deviation, rather than estimating them. The harness summary shows one tool configured.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You add other tools the same way, including a cloud browser, AgentCore Gateway (a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore), and remote Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="give-the-agent-skills"&gt;Give the agent skills&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Skills are bundles of instructions and scripts that give an agent domain knowledge on demand. You load them from the AWS curated catalog, a Git repository, Amazon S3, or a filesystem path, and the harness loads them only when the task calls for them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use an agent such as &lt;code&gt;aws_architect&lt;/code&gt; with a Claude model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on &lt;strong&gt;Add Skills, &lt;/strong&gt;then under &lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Skill&lt;/strong&gt; and set the &lt;strong&gt;Source&lt;/strong&gt;. For the curated catalog, choose &lt;strong&gt;AWS Skills&lt;/strong&gt; and enter a glob pattern such as &lt;code&gt;core-skills/*&lt;/code&gt;. You can add more skills, for example a &lt;strong&gt;Git&lt;/strong&gt; source that points to a public repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a &lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt; that benefits from the skill, such as “Outline a serverless image-upload pipeline on AWS”, and run the node.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136648" style="width: 874px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136648" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136648" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/05/ml-21089-figure8.png" alt="" width="864" height="452"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136648" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The agent applies the loaded skills to produce guidance, and the harness summary shows the number of skills configured.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-in-your-vpc"&gt;Run in your VPC&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For agents that need private network access, you can run the harness in your VPC. You set the network configuration on the credential, so every agent that credential provisions runs privately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Edit your &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API&lt;/strong&gt; credential.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set &lt;strong&gt;Network Mode&lt;/strong&gt; to &lt;strong&gt;VPC&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter your &lt;strong&gt;VPC Subnet IDs&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;VPC Security Group IDs&lt;/strong&gt;, then save.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-11.png" alt="Amazon Bedrock AgentCore API credential set to VPC network mode with subnet and security group IDs" width="800" height="634"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The credential configured for VPC mode with subnet and security group IDs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your subnets don’t need internet access. The harness pulls its managed container image from a private Amazon ECR repository in the same Region, so you need VPC endpoints for Amazon ECR and Amazon S3 rather than a NAT gateway. Refer to the AgentCore harness network configuration documentation for the required endpoints and the execution role permissions. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/harness-security.html#harness-network-config" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore harness security documentation&lt;/a&gt; for the required endpoints and the execution role permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In a workflow, use an agent such as &lt;code&gt;private_vpc_agent&lt;/code&gt; with the VPC-enabled credential.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a &lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt; and run the node.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21089-12.png" alt="Node output for a VPC agent with the harness summary showing the network mode is VPC" width="800" height="862"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;The node output for a VPC agent. The harness summary shows the network mode is VPC.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each agent you create is a harness resource in your AWS account, and it can provision a managed memory store. To avoid ongoing charges, delete the agents you no longer need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;List your harnesses with the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) or the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control list-harnesses --region us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the ones you created for this post. For pricing details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-harness --harness-id &amp;lt;harness-id&amp;gt; --region us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you enabled a VPC, you might also want to remove any interface VPC endpoints you created for this walkthrough, because interface endpoints incur charges while they exist.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-to-go-next"&gt;Where to go next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walked through memory, per-user scoping, a code interpreter tool, skills, and VPC networking. The node already supports more of the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore harness feature set. Here’s what to try next, each configured the same way you configured the features above:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your choice of model provider, switchable mid-session.&lt;/strong&gt; Use OpenAI, Google Gemini, or LiteLLM-supported providers alongside Amazon Bedrock. Set the provider and model on the node, and switch between turns of the same session without losing context. Non-Bedrock providers use an API key stored in AgentCore Identity (a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;More tools.&lt;/strong&gt; Add a cloud browser, AgentCore Gateway with optional OAuth outbound authentication, and remote MCP servers alongside the code interpreter.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inline functions.&lt;/strong&gt; Let the agent call back into your n8n workflow for a human-in-the-loop step, then return the result to the agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom containers.&lt;/strong&gt; Bring your own Linux/arm64 container image so the agent runs with your own dependencies.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Filesystem mounts.&lt;/strong&gt; Use managed session storage, or mount Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) or Amazon S3 for data that outlives a session.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OAuth-authenticated invocation.&lt;/strong&gt; Invoke agents protected by an inbound JSON Web Token (JWT) authorizer with a token from your identity provider, set on the AgentCore credential.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Versions and endpoints.&lt;/strong&gt; Every configuration change becomes an immutable version, and named endpoints let you pin staging and production to specific versions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The examples folder in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore/tree/main/examples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; includes importable workflows for many of these. For end-to-end use cases, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore/tree/main/examples/templates" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;examples/templates&lt;/a&gt; folder has fuller workflows that show the agent working alongside other n8n nodes in complete, importable automations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you installed the open-source AgentCore harness community node for n8n and built agents that remember conversations, keep per-user memory separate, run code in a sandbox, use skills, and run privately in a VPC, all from the n8n editor and with no infrastructure or agent code. Because the node works with Amazon Bedrock, OpenAI, Gemini, and LiteLLM providers, you choose the model that fits each task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, add the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore node from the n8n nodes panel (or install &lt;code&gt;@aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore&lt;/code&gt; from Settings &amp;gt; Community Nodes), import an example workflow, and build your first agent. The node is open source under MIT and built on the open source Strands Agents framework, and contributions and feedback are welcome on the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/n8n-nodes-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. To learn more about the underlying capability, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;n8n is a trademark of n8n GmbH. All other trademarks are the property of their respective owners.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Introducing Web Search on Amazon Bedrock for foundation model grounding</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-web-search-on-amazon-bedrock-for-foundation-model-grounding/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Anuj Jauhari]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 04 Aug 2026 18:39:14 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">20c57cb78e0f1ebce99e757dfe0b084d51a3f9e2</guid>

					<description>Today, we are introducing the general availability of Web Search on Amazon Bedrock. It is a server-side built-in tool that grounds model responses in current web knowledge. With Web Search, grounding becomes a native capability of Amazon Bedrock, with no third-party vendors to onboard, no external APIs to orchestrate, and no additional third party vendor security reviews to conduct. In this post, we walk through what Web Search on Amazon Bedrock is, why it matters, how to enable it using the OpenAI Responses API, and how to get started with the tool.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When a foundation model needs to answer a question about last week’s earnings call, yesterday’s regulatory change, or this morning’s weather forecast, it needs knowledge it was never trained on. Grounding the model in current web knowledge closes that gap – whether it’s powering chatbots, coding assistants, CLI tools, or enterprise applications, grounding helps answer questions beyond the model’s training and reduces hallucinations. &amp;nbsp;Traditionally, connecting a model to web knowledge required developers to identify, integrate, and maintain a third-party Web Search provider, a process that delays project timelines and introduces data residency risks and operational overhead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At AWS New York Summit 2026, we announced the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/announcing-web-search-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-ground-your-ai-agents-in-current-accurate-web-knowledge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;general availability of Web Search on AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;. Today, we are extending it further with the general availability of Web Search on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. It is a server-side built-in tool that grounds model responses in current web knowledge. With Web Search, grounding becomes a native capability of Amazon Bedrock, with no third-party vendors to onboard, no external APIs to orchestrate, and no additional third party vendor security reviews to conduct.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through what Web Search on Amazon Bedrock is, why it matters, how to enable it using the OpenAI Responses API, and how to get started with the tool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-web-search-on-bedrock-provides"&gt;What Web Search on Amazon Bedrock provides&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search is designed for Amazon Bedrock model inference, with the following differentiators:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-source grounding approach:&lt;/strong&gt; Web Search is backed by a web index that Amazon operates, spanning billions of documents and refreshed continually. It combines this index with a built-in knowledge graph that anchors the entities in a domain along with the connections between them. When a question is factual in nature; say, who wrote a particular book or what year an event took place; Web Search uses the knowledge graph to answer with strong confidence, rather than leaving the model to infer the answer from extracted page text. That can help cut down on the small factual inaccuracies that tend to slip in whenever an agent assembles an answer from fragments on its own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Context-efficient retrieval.&lt;/strong&gt; Rather than handing the model a raw page and hoping it finds the relevant part, Web Search performs semantic snippet extraction – pulling the passages from each web page that bear on the query and returning them in a form optimized for the model’s context window. The model sees the parts that matter, with fewer tokens spent on boilerplate. Retrieval is fast, so grounded responses can be delivered with minimal latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Single-parameter enablement.&lt;/strong&gt; Web Search becomes a single parameter in your existing OpenAI-compatible API call, removing the need for vendor onboarding, API keys, orchestration layers, and separate SDKs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Helping with enterprise-grade compliance out of the box.&lt;/strong&gt; By default, Web Search on Bedrock offers zero data egress, so your data never leaves your AWS environment. As we introduce new capabilities, some future features may expose data only at your explicit request. For the latest information, please refer to the Amazon Bedrock documentation . Web Search operates entirely within Amazon Bedrock’s infrastructure, supporting customers’ compliance requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/17/ML-21265-1.png" alt="Diagram of the Web Search request lifecycle: the model identifies a knowledge gap, Bedrock formulates a query, retrieves content from the Amazon web index and knowledge graph, and returns a grounded response with citations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When Web Search is enabled in an API call, Bedrock handles the entire search lifecycle server-side. First, the model identifies that a query requires up-to-date web knowledge. Bedrock then formulates a search query, retrieves relevant content from Amazon’s web index and knowledge graph, and injects results – including relevant snippets, source URLs and titles – into the model’s context window. The model reasons over the retrieved content and generates a grounded response with source citations. The API returns the final response with structured citation annotations, including URL and page title for each referenced source. There’s no client-side tool-use loop to build, no external API responses to parse, and no retries or rate limits to manage – a single API call returns a grounded response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-with-the-openai-responses-api"&gt;Getting started with the OpenAI Responses API&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Responses API supports built-in tools natively, so Web Search can be called without defining a function schema or building a client-side loop. Enablement takes three steps: configure AWS credentials, point the OpenAI client at the bedrock-mantle endpoint, and add the Web Search tool to the request. At launch, Web Search is available for OpenAI models served through Amazon Bedrock’s next-generation inference engine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1:&lt;/strong&gt; Configure authentication and permissions. Web Search uses your existing AWS credentials – there are no separate API keys to provision. The environment must have AWS credentials available through the standard credential chain (an IAM role, the AWS CLI profile, or environment variables), which are used to authenticate requests to the bedrock-mantle endpoint. The calling identity needs two sets of permissions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Inference permissions on Amazon Bedrock, so the model call itself succeeds. Attach the AmazonBedrockMantleInferenceAccess managed policy, or grant the specific inference actions your call requires.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Web Search tool permissions, so the model can call the tool during the request. At minimum grant bedrock-websearch:InvokeSearch; add bedrock-websearch:InvokeFetch to let the model read a result’s full page content. Live-web retrieval additionally requires bedrock-websearch:ExternalWebAccess, which is the default request behavior — if your identity doesn’t have it, set external_web_access: false on the tool. If InvokeSearch is denied, Web Search is effectively disabled and the model answers from its training data instead.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Requests to the endpoint are authenticated with an AWS-issued bearer token, which you can mint from your existing AWS credentials using the aws-bedrock-token-generator package. This bearer token isn’t a separate API key; it’s a short-lived (up to 12 hours) credential derived from your existing AWS IAM identity via SigV4, packaged in the format the OpenAI client expects for its api_key parameter. No additional key management is required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start from a standard call. A normal Responses API call, without grounding, looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.4",
    input="What were the key announcements at AWS re:Invent 2025?",
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2:&lt;/strong&gt; Enable Web Search. To ground that same call in web knowledge, add a single tools entry:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
  &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;tools=[{"type": "web_search", "external_web_access": False}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The optional external_web_access field selects where Web Search retrieves from: Amazon’s pre-indexed web corpus, or live content fetched directly from the web. Today only indexed-web retrieval is served; live-web retrieval will be enabled in a future update, and the parameter is already in the API so your code won’t need to change. The default is true, which requires the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-websearch:ExternalWebAccess&lt;/code&gt; permission. The examples below set false, which needs no additional permission.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3:&lt;/strong&gt; Read the grounded response with citations. Putting it together, here’s the complete end-to-end example, including how to extract the source citations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
  &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-python"&gt;from openai import OpenAI
from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token

REGION = "us-east-1"

client = OpenAI(
    base_url=f"https://bedrock-mantle.{REGION}.api.aws/openai/v1",
    api_key=provide_token(region=REGION),
)

response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.4",
    input="What were the key announcements at AWS re:Invent 2025?",
    tools=[{"type": "web_search", "external_web_access": False}],
)

searches = [item for item in response.output if item.type == "web_search_call"]
print(f"Retrieval steps: {len(searches)}")
for call in searches:
    if call.action.type == "search":
        print(f"  search: {call.action.queries}")
    elif call.action.type == "open_page":
        print(f"  open_page: {call.action.url}")

for item in response.output:
    if item.type == "message":
        for content in item.content:
            if content.type == "output_text":
                print(content.text)
                for citation in content.annotations or []:
                    if citation.type == "url_citation":
                        print(f"  [{citation.title}] {citation.url}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The above code produces the following output (abridged):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;Retrieval steps: 2
  search: ['AWS re:Invent 2025 key announcements official AWS blog keynote recap']
  open_page: https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/top-announcements-of-aws-reinvent-2025
The biggest AWS re:Invent 2025 announcements clustered around **AI agents, custom
silicon/infrastructure, and developer productivity**. ...
  [Top announcements of AWS re:Invent 2025 | AWS News Blog] https://aws.amazon.com/...
  [AWS re:Invent 2025: Amazon announces Nova 2, Trainium3, frontier agents] https://...&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this sample, the request includes a Web Search entry in the tools array. Bedrock executes the search server-side and returns the grounded response in a single round-trip – there is no function schema to define and no client-side loop to manage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each citation is a &lt;code&gt;url_citation&lt;/code&gt; object in the message content’s annotations array. Its wire shape:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;{
    "type": "url_citation",
    "start_index": 120,
    "end_index": 303,
    "title": "Top announcements of AWS re:Invent 2025 | AWS News Blog",
    "url": "https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/top-announcements-of-aws-reinvent-2025"
  }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;start_index&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;end_index&lt;/code&gt; are character offsets into output_text, letting you render inline footnotes or highlight the exact span each citation supports.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="auditing-and-observability"&gt;Auditing and observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search is integrated with AWS CloudTrail out of the box. Every call to &lt;code&gt;bedrock-websearch:InvokeSearch&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock-websearch:InvokeFetch&lt;/code&gt; is recorded as a management event, capturing the calling identity, timestamp, action, source identity (including any forward-access-session originator), and the account and Region context of the request. Access-denied outcomes are always logged, and each &lt;code&gt;AccessDeniedException&lt;/code&gt; event includes the specific condition key that caused the denial — which makes IAM misconfigurations easy to diagnose without turning on additional trails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By design, CloudTrail does not record the query text, the URLs returned by search, or the raw page content retrieved by fetch. Query text is treated the same way as an inference prompt and is never exposed in trail events. Combined with in-Region processing and zero data egress, this gives security and compliance teams a full audit trail of who used the tool when, without exposing what end users searched for.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search on Amazon Bedrock removes the undifferentiated heavy lifting of connecting foundation models to up-to-date web knowledge. It delivers context-efficient, multi-source grounded results with low-latency, and simple enablement through a single API parameter – so developers can add web grounding without managing vendors, orchestration, or compliance reviews.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search on Bedrock is generally available in US, with in-region query handling in &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;us-east-2&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;. For pricing details, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;. To get started, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/web-search.html"&gt;Web Search documentation&lt;/a&gt; for complete API references and examples.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Automated web insight extraction with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/automated-web-insight-extraction-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Louisa Liu]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 04 Aug 2026 16:02:21 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">485a2118fbcc1067573c0ef552a78db3ae5bc772</guid>

					<description>Extracting insights from dozens of websites by hand quickly becomes overwhelming. This post shows how to build an automated web insight extraction solution with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Browser, Amazon Bedrock, Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, and AWS Lambda that monitors RSS feeds, renders pages reliably, and makes AI-extracted insights searchable.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Extracting insights from dozens of websites often means manually checking each one, a process that quickly becomes overwhelming. Design teams need to track competitor products, marketing teams want to monitor content trends, and product managers need to stay on top of market intelligence. But doing this manually means someone has to visit sites, copy content, and organize information before real analysis can begin. Rule-based scrapers offer some automation, but they are tightly coupled to page structure. A site redesign or a migration to a JavaScript-rendered frontend can silently break the pipeline for days before the team notices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. The solution in this post uses the AgentCore Browser, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. This fully managed browser service renders JavaScript-heavy pages reliably, so your pipeline is more resilient when websites change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post demonstrates how to deploy an automated insight extraction solution using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Browser, Amazon Bedrock for AI-powered analysis, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/features/serverless/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Serverless&lt;/a&gt; for semantic search, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; for orchestration. You build a system that monitors RSS feeds, retrieves web content using the AgentCore managed browser, extracts insights with AI, and makes everything searchable through a web interface.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-cases"&gt;Use cases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution was built for design and product teams who need to track industry trends, but the architecture applies to broader scenarios:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Competitive intelligence&lt;/strong&gt;: Track competitor blogs, product announcements, and press releases. The AI can identify new features, pricing changes, or strategic shifts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Market research&lt;/strong&gt;: Monitor industry news, analyst reports, and trade publications. Search for emerging trends or technologies relevant to your business.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Content curation&lt;/strong&gt;: Aggregate content from multiple sources and let the AI identify the most relevant pieces for your audience.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compliance monitoring&lt;/strong&gt;: Watch regulatory websites and news sources for changes that might affect your business.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-you-will-learn"&gt;What you will learn&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the end of this post, you will understand how the event-driven architecture separates content collection from AI processing, how the browser automation in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore handles JavaScript-heavy pages, and how vector embeddings in Amazon OpenSearch Serverless power semantic search across your collected insights. The complete implementation is available in this &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-intelligent-insights-extraction" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you are new to the services used in this solution, the following resources provide foundational context:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/browser-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Browser&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/latest/developerguide/serverless.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Serverless developer guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture follows an event-driven pattern that separates content collection from processing. The following diagram illustrates the end-to-end system, organized into three functional layers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/30/ML-20018-1.png" alt="End-to-end architecture of the web insight extraction system across collection, processing, and access layers." width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 1 — Architecture diagram for the automated web insight extraction system&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-the-solution-works"&gt;How the solution works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RSS feed collection:&lt;/strong&gt; An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt; schedule triggers an AWS Lambda function every 15 minutes to check configured RSS feeds for new articles and deduplicate against &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3).&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Browser-based content retrieval:&lt;/strong&gt; For each new article, the Lambda function opens a browser session through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and connects to it using Playwright over the Chrome DevTools Protocol (CDP). Unlike standard HTTP requests, the remote browser renders the full page including JavaScript-heavy content, waits for dynamic elements to load, takes a screenshot, and downloads images. This is the step that makes the rest of the pipeline possible: without reliable page rendering, the AI extraction downstream would receive incomplete or broken content. The artifacts are uploaded to Amazon S3 in a structured format.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Event-driven processing:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon S3 upload events publish messages to an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS)&lt;/a&gt; queue, and a second Lambda function extracts clean text from the raw HTML.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AI-powered insight extraction:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock generates summaries, identifies themes and entities, extracts actionable insights, and creates vector embeddings from the cleaned content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Indexing and semantic search:&lt;/strong&gt; The enriched results are indexed into Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, which supports both keyword and vector search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User and API access:&lt;/strong&gt; End users authenticate through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt; and access a React-based frontend on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;. For programmatic access, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server on Amazon ECS with Fargate exposes the system through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudfront/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudFront&lt;/a&gt;. MCP is an open standard that lets AI assistants and tools connect to external data sources through a unified interface.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-components"&gt;Solution components&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections describe the main components of the solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="content-collection-and-browser-automation"&gt;Content collection and browser automation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The RSS sync Lambda function does more than fetch feeds. It parses each RSS feed, filters for articles published within a configurable time window (24 hours by default), and deduplicates using a URL hash as a unique ID in Amazon S3. For new articles, the function opens a remote browser session through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and connects via Playwright over CDP. This isn’t a headless browser running in Lambda. It is a managed browser service: AgentCore hosts the remote browser, and Playwright controls it through a WebSocket connection. The browser renders the full page, waits for JavaScript to load, and captures the complete rendered output. The function then extracts images from the page, downloads them, and uploads everything to Amazon S3 in a structured format:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;s3://bucket/
└── example.com/
    └── abc123def456/          # URL hash
        ├── article.html       # Full HTML
        ├── screenshot.png     # Page screenshot
        ├── metadata.json      # Article metadata
        └── images/            # Captured images
            ├── image1.jpg
            └── image2.jpg&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The metadata.json file includes the original URL, title, timestamp, and references to downloaded assets. When the metadata file is uploaded to Amazon S3, it triggers the processing pipeline automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This separation means content collection and AI processing scale independently. If you’re monitoring dozens of RSS feeds, the collection Lambda function handles them concurrently while the Amazon SQS queue with a dead-letter queue provides automatic retries for processing failures.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ai-powered-content-analysis"&gt;AI-powered content analysis&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The idea extraction Lambda function preprocesses HTML to reduce token consumption, a step that significantly improves the consistency of AI-generated output.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When it receives a message from Amazon SQS, it pulls the HTML from Amazon S3 and runs it through a preprocessing step. Large HTML files (over 1 MB) get simplified using html-to-text to avoid token limits. Smaller files use Mozilla’s Readability library, which does a better job of extracting the main content and identifying the primary image.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cleaned content then goes to Amazon Bedrock with a customizable prompt. You can use models supported by Amazon Bedrock. For model availability by AWS Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. The prompt asks the AI to extract:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A concise summary.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Key themes and topics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Main entities (people, companies, products).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Relevant categories.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Actionable insights.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda function also generates vector embeddings for semantic search, so you can find related content even when the exact words don’t match. The function writes everything to Amazon OpenSearch Serverless: the original content, AI-extracted metadata, and the embedding vector. The index is configured for both keyword and vector search. A query like “what are emerging design trends” returns relevant results even if those exact words don’t appear in the source content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="responsible-ai-controls"&gt;Responsible AI controls&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because this pipeline sends third-party web content to a foundation model and publishes the AI-generated output to your team, production deployments should include safeguards around the extraction step. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; lets you apply these controls without changing the extraction prompt:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Content filtering blocks harmful or inappropriate material that may appear in scraped web pages before it reaches your searchable index.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Denied topics and word filters keep the extracted insights within the scope your teams expect, which matters when source feeds are outside your control.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Contextual grounding checks validate that generated summaries and insights are grounded in the source article, reducing the risk of hallucinated claims being indexed as facts.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the pipeline ingests content from external websites, treat the scraped text as untrusted input: guardrails act as a control point between raw web content and the insights your organization consumes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="web-interface-and-programmatic-access"&gt;Web interface and programmatic access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The React-based frontend with Amazon Cognito authentication provides a search interface where teams can browse and explore insights. The MCP server provides an API layer for integration with other AI tools, supporting programmatic search and retrieval through Amazon CloudFront. MCP is an open standard that defines how AI assistants discover and call external tools, so other AI agents in your organization can query the insight database directly. Processing components run within an Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC), with Amazon CloudWatch providing observability and AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) enforcing least privilege access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned"&gt;Lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building a real-world insight extraction pipeline involves more than wiring together managed services. A few architectural decisions had outsized impact on cost, reliability, and output quality, and are worth calling out explicitly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Browser sessions are powerful but expensive.&lt;/strong&gt; In our test, each Playwright render through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore takes 10–30 seconds and costs significantly more than a plain HTTP request. URL hashing for deduplication (checking Amazon S3 before triggering a new AgentCore session) is essential to keep costs manageable at scale. The tradeoff is worth it: AgentCore reliably captures pages that would break a standard scraper, which is the foundation the rest of the pipeline depends on.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Raw HTML is a poor input for LLMs.&lt;/strong&gt; Clean HTML before you send it to Amazon Bedrock to reduce token consumption and improve the consistency of AI-extracted output. This preprocessing step is not an optimization. It’s a prerequisite for reliable results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Semantic search broadens what you can find.&lt;/strong&gt; A keyword search for “furniture trends” returns exact matches, while vector search surfaces conceptually related content regardless of terminology. For competitive intelligence or market research, embeddings are what make the search interface genuinely useful.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SQS decoupling is what makes the pipeline reliable.&lt;/strong&gt; Without it, a processing failure means the article is never analyzed. An Amazon SQS dead-letter queue between Amazon S3 and the extraction Lambda provides automatic retries, a small architectural change with significant reliability impact.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Serverless has a real cost floor.&lt;/strong&gt; The service requires minimum capacity units regardless of usage, which suits production workloads with consistent query volume. For lower-volume deployments, Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) with pgvector supports the same vector search capabilities at a lower baseline cost and is worth considering as a starting point. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Serverless pricing&lt;/a&gt; details.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Empty the Amazon S3 content bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run &lt;code&gt;cdk destroy&lt;/code&gt; from the project directory (or delete the stack in the AWS CloudFormation console).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to build an automated insight extraction solution that transforms manual web monitoring into a searchable, AI-enriched knowledge base. The event-driven architecture decouples content collection from processing, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore handles JavaScript-heavy websites, and Amazon OpenSearch Serverless with vector embeddings powers semantic search beyond keyword matching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete source code, deployment guide, and configuration instructions are available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-intelligent-insights-extraction" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

		<title>From weeks to minutes: How Formula 1® uses agentic AI on AWS to accelerate data operations</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/from-weeks-to-minutes-how-formula-1-uses-agentic-ai-on-aws-to-accelerate-data-operations/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Subhro Bose]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 03 Aug 2026 17:24:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Artificial Intelligence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
