		<guid isPermaLink="false">c31d5a216c9f10149d2564008a4c1dd5818f023f</guid>

					<description>Formula 1® partnered with AWS to build the Data Accelerator, using agentic AI on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to transform its MarTech data platform. Learn how F1 cut data source onboarding from up to 8 weeks to about 40 minutes, automated schema evolution, and gained end-to-end observability across its fan-engagement data estate.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Formula 1&lt;sup&gt;®&lt;/sup&gt;&amp;nbsp;(F1) engages an audience of over 800 million fans globally across digital platforms, F1 TV, social media, ticketing, and merchandise year-round. Races happen every two weeks. Fan engagement windows are measured in minutes and commercial decisions need to move at the speed of the grid. Behind the scenes, F1’s marketing technology (MarTech) platform, Customer 360, captures interactions across all of these touchpoints to power personalization, segmentation, and commercial strategy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, the platform faced a significant operational challenge. According to Chris Roberts, Director of IT at Formula 1, “Our MarTech platform is the nervous system of F1’s fan engagement. But every new data source required 6 to 8 weeks of manual engineering. We had an 18-month backlog just to integrate 12 new sources.” The business was generating data faster than the engineering team could wire it up. As a result, Matt Kemp, F1 Head of Data Operations, set to improve efficiencies and data quality. “Manually ingesting data sources is time consuming, creates solution variances, and ultimately results in data integrity issues. I wanted a solution that was repeatable, robust and reliable. AWS worked backwards from our needs to implement an agentic solution that worked end to end, applying business logic at each step.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In early 2026, F1 and AWS worked together to build the Data Accelerator, a solution that uses agentic AI on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to transform F1’s MarTech data platform from a manually maintained system into a self-managed, observable, and unified data estate. In this post, we show how the Data Accelerator reduced data source onboarding from up to 8 weeks to approximately 40 minutes of code generation plus hours of deployment. It also identified and fixed data source anomalies in production, tracked data platform operations and agent lineage in a single window, and opened a gateway for analysts, engineers, and scientists to collaborate. “For the first time, we have end-to-end visibility across the entire MarTech platform with data lineage and root cause analysis, not just dashboards full of alerts,” says Roberts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;F1’s Customer 360 platform ingests data from ticketing partners, streaming integrations, sponsor activation feeds, social media, and merchandise systems. Operating a data estate of this breadth and velocity surfaced three areas of friction the team set out to solve. First, onboarding each new data source was a heavily manual effort: engineers wrote schema mappings, built ingestion pipelines, configured data quality checks, defined General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) classifications, and set governance policies by hand. This process took 6 to 8 weeks per source. Second, the platform had to keep pace with constantly evolving upstream feeds. Providers frequently changed column names, added fields, or restructured and rescheduled payloads without notice. Those changes often surfaced at the worst possible moment, such as mid race-weekend or during a mission-critical campaign launch. Third, visibility was fragmented. Logs were scattered across services with no unified data lineage. When a stakeholder questioned a metric, engineers spent hours manually tracing the issue across Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) paths, Amazon Redshift control tables, Airflow logs, and DBT outputs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Data Accelerator addressed these challenges through five workstreams delivered simultaneously:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agentic data source onboarding using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, hosting agents in its runtime containers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automated schema evolution detection and remediation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Unified data access through Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;End-to-end observability with root cause analysis tool (RCA) and context graph.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automated identification of a failure in observability dashboard and agentic operation if they could be fixed with code changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A sixth workstream optimized the customer identity resolution algorithms that unify fan touchpoints across channels. The following sections describe each workstream in detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agentic-data-source-onboarding"&gt;Agentic data source onboarding&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The centerpiece of the Data Accelerator is a set of platform agents that take a Business Requirements Document (BRD) with limited information about the data source and produce a fully production-ready onboarding pipeline. This includes infrastructure code, data transformations, governance policies, and GDPR classification without a human writing a single line of boilerplate. The agents work in two phases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-1-configuration-generation"&gt;Phase 1: Configuration generation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a new data source needs onboarding, a team member uploads a BRD to an Amazon S3 bucket. The upload triggers an AWS Lambda function, which invokes Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The agent reads the BRD and generates a set of configuration files. It then accesses GitHub through a GitHub App to push these files as a pull request to the standardized Git repository, and accesses Jira through its REST API to create a ticket referencing the PR. All agent conversations and actions are traced in Amazon CloudWatch through built-in AgentCore observability. The assigned engineer reviews, adjusts if necessary, and approves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21186-1-1.png" alt="Phase 1 workflow showing a BRD upload triggering an agent that generates config files and opens a pull request" width="800" height="3555"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Phase 1 workflow: a BRD upload triggers the agent to generate config files and open a pull request&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-2-full-pipeline-generation"&gt;Phase 2: Full pipeline generation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once the configuration files are approved, a human triggers the next stage. The agent takes the approved configuration and generates three separate Pull Requests:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt; application and infrastructure code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;DBT transformation framework.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Governance policies including GDPR tagging.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All three PRs link to a single Jira ticket for traceability. Engineers review each one across the Infrastructure, DBT, and Governance repositories and approve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21186-2-1.png" alt="Phase 2 workflow where the agent generates infrastructure, transformation, and governance pull requests linked to one Jira ticket" width="800" height="621"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Phase 2 workflow: the agent generates infrastructure, transformation, and governance pull requests&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="automated-gdpr-classification"&gt;Automated GDPR classification&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What distinguishes this from a basic code generator is the integrated GDPR classification. The agent proactively analyzes every data column, determines whether it contains personal data, sensitive personal data, or pseudonymized data, and tags it with the appropriate GDPR category. These tags publish directly to the governance registry in SageMaker Unified Studio, giving the compliance team immediate visibility without manual review cycles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="modular-skill-architecture"&gt;Modular skill architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system is not a tightly coupled agent graph. A single agent operates with modular skill definitions, each encapsulating a distinct capability: schema mapping and data type inference, data quality validation, governance enforcement, and sensitive data classification. At runtime, the agent evaluates incoming requirements and activates the relevant skills, composing them through a multi-pass reasoning process. Pass-0 handles token management through scrubbing, Pass-1 summarizes tool outputs, and Pass-2 rolls up an overall assessment, refining accuracy and completeness progressively rather than relying on a one-shot response. New capabilities ship as new skill modules without changing the core agent loop, keeping the architecture maintainable and composable as the platform grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result is onboarding time dropped from 6 to 8 weeks to approximately 40 minutes of code generation plus hours of deployment and review. AI agents now handle 95% of the work autonomously.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="automated-schema-evolution"&gt;Automated schema evolution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Onboarding new data sources is one challenge, but keeping existing integrations healthy is another. Upstream providers frequently modify their data structures, from renaming a column to creating a new field. Previously, the F1 team discovered these changes when a pipeline failed, often during a live race weekend. The same agent architecture that handles onboarding now continuously monitors for upstream schema changes. When a provider modifies their data structure, the agent detects it through event-driven triggers using AWS Lambda and Amazon EventBridge. It assesses the downstream impact, identifying which pipelines are affected, and which consumers depend on the changed fields. It then generates the necessary code updates across all affected repositories and creates a Jira ticket with full context and linked PRs. Engineers receive a notification that explains what changed, describes the impact, and presents a proposed fix for review. End-to-end resolution now takes hours instead of days.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21186-3.png" alt="Schema evolution workflow where an agent detects an upstream change, assesses impact, and generates code updates with a Jira ticket" width="800" height="1424"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Schema evolution agentic workflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="unified-data-access-with-amazon-sagemaker-unified-studio"&gt;Unified data access with Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before the Data Accelerator, working with Customer 360 data required navigating multiple disconnected environments. Data engineers curated pipelines in one account. Data scientists who wanted to model fan behavior needed access to a separate account, and analysts operated in a third world entirely. Nobody shared tooling or context, and getting from a question to an answer took days of coordination before any analysis could begin.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution uses Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio as the foundation for a data mesh framework where a central governance account brokers data discovery and access across multiple producer teams. The key enabler: governance is codified as declarative configuration, not manual console operations. A single data source definition simultaneously publishes data to the catalog and provisions the access control needed for consumers to subscribe. This means agents can safely onboard new data products end-to-end, from storage to catalog to governed access, because the framework enforces security constraints by construction. No human needs to review IAM policies or AWS Lake Formation grants. The platform guarantees correctness structurally. This is what makes the “one front door” possible.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data engineers curate and govern datasets in one place, and data scientists find those same datasets in the same environment: governed, documented, and ready to model. A data scientist building a fan segmentation model or optimizing the customer identity algorithm doesn’t need to know where the data lives, who owns the pipeline, or which S3 prefix to use. They open Unified Studio, find the curated Customer 360 datasets, and start modeling. They get shared notebooks, consistent tooling, and governed access, because declarative governance made safe self-service possible without sacrificing control. The curation and the consumption finally live side by side.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="end-to-end-observability-with-rca-and-context-graph"&gt;End-to-end observability with RCA and context graph&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A data platform is only as trustworthy as the team’s ability to answer one question: is the data correct right now? Before the Data Accelerator, answering that question meant logging into Apache Airflow, checking Amazon S3 paths, querying Amazon Redshift control tables, and reading DBT logs. “Nobody had the full view. When a stakeholder asked, ‘why does this number look wrong?’ the answer was always, ‘give us a few hours.’ The observability dashboard changes that entirely,” adds Roberts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The observability layer presents full data lineage from S3 Raw ingestion through Processed layers into Amazon Redshift DBT stages as a single interactive graph, color-coded for health. Users click on any node to drill down to individual sources and tables, each showing pass/fail status, last run time, and duration. If a pipeline fails, the lineage visualization shows exactly where the break occurred, and which downstream data is affected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Root cause analysis (RCA) is an agentic tool within F1’s platform that reads system logs and identifies failure points across the data estate. On its own, RCA can tell you what failed. We augment the RCA tool by passing through business context and system topology, codified as JSON. A missing file in S3 might be the error, but with the context graph, RCA tells you that the upstream provider rescheduled their delivery window, which is why the file wasn’t there when the pipeline ran. That’s the difference between knowing what failed and understanding why.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the first time, F1 has full lineage, causal root cause analysis, and business context definitions in one place, and the dashboard auto-refreshes every 15 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21186-4-1.png" alt="Data lineage graph with pipeline health color-coded across data sources and processing stages" width="800" height="1486"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Data lineage visualization showing pipeline health across sources and stages&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21186-5-1.png" alt="Observability dashboard showing pipeline failure details including source, status, and timing" width="800" height="1107"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Observability dashboard with failure details&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="customer-identity-resolution"&gt;Customer identity resolution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The final workstream optimized the algorithms that resolve customer identity across F1’s fan touchpoints. A single fan might interact through the app, buy tickets on the website, watch on F1 TV, and engage on social media. Unifying those interactions into a single identity without false merges or missed matches is what makes effective personalization possible within the Fan Personalization Platform (FPP).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;F1 already had a working identity resolution process, but it was slow and struggled to scale with the growing volume of fan interactions across channels. Rather than re-architecting the pipeline or replacing components, the team focused on optimizing the existing resolution algorithm’s computational performance. By profiling execution bottlenecks and tuning the matching logic, the engagement reduced processing time by 50%, while keeping the entire resolution pipeline and its downstream integrations fully intact. No processes were changed, no accuracy trade-offs were made: the same algorithm now runs in half the time at F1’s production scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With faster resolution, F1 can onboard any new data source and gather new customer data in half the existing time. Faster resolution means fresher unified profiles, which in turn means more timely and relevant personalization across every marketing channel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“The whole point is to deliver the right message to the right fan at the right time, whether that’s through email, the F1 app, ticketing, or social. Now that we can onboard sources in hours and resolve identities faster, we can actually deliver the personalized experiences our fans expect across every marketing channel,” says Kemp.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-governance-by-design"&gt;Security and governance by design&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Data Accelerator operates on the principle that AI proposes and humans review. The agents run on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with long-term memory, retaining context across invocations. Development used Kiro for structured spec-driven development and Amazon Bedrock (Claude Sonnet 4.6) as the foundation model. The event-driven backbone uses AWS Lambda for compute, Amazon EventBridge for routing, Amazon Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow (MWAA) for workflow orchestration, and Amazon S3 as the raw data layer. All AI model access is governed through F1’s AI Gateway for unified access control, cost management, and audit logging. But the architecture is only half the story. The security posture is what makes this production-ready.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The security posture includes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Least privilege: fine-grained permissions, short-lived tokens with one-hour expiry, access limited to specific repositories and resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Full audit trail: every action is logged and attributed for compliance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Human review: every generated Pull Request goes through engineer approval.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automated testing: agents generate comprehensive tests for their own changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rollback capabilities: issues surfaced post-merge can be reverted immediately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Network isolation: the entire system runs within private subnets in Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC) with no direct internet access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Encrypted credentials: all secrets stored at rest in AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“What gave us confidence to put agentic AI in our production data pipelines was what we call ‘Human at the helm.’ The agents do the heavy lifting, but humans make the decisions. Every change goes through the same review process our engineers already use, so adoption was immediate.” says Roberts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-impact"&gt;The impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Data Accelerator delivered measurable impact across F1’s MarTech operations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data source onboarding: reduced from 6 to 8 weeks to approximately 40 minutes of code generation plus hours of deployment and review.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Autonomous work: AI agents handle 95% of onboarding tasks without human intervention.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Time-to-value: approximately 99% reduction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Schema evolution: end-to-end resolution in hours instead of days.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Integration backlog: 18-month backlog cleared in weeks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data engineers who previously spent their time writing boilerplate ingestion code and chasing schema breaks now focus on strategic initiatives that advance the business.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implementation velocity: a single developer took the agentic solution from proof of concept to production release in 4 months.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reliability, consistency, and data integrity of the MarTech platform were improved, while the operational overhead was reduced: “The Data Accelerator didn’t just speed things up. It changed how we operate. Our data engineers went from writing boilerplate ingestion code to focusing on strategic initiatives. Issues can be identified and fixed before our end users even notice.” says Kemp.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Data Accelerator’s success comes down to three principles: meeting developers where they already work, keeping them at the helm, and embedding governance like GDPR classification from day one rather than bolting it on after. These principles shaped a solution where F1 partnered with AWS to use agentic AI on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to transform MarTech data operations. By combining automated data source onboarding, schema evolution detection, and unified data access through Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio, F1 reduced onboarding time by approximately 99% and eliminated an 18-month integration backlog in weeks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The approach is deliberately replicable. Any organization dealing with multi-source data onboarding, schema volatility, and governance requirements can apply the same architecture to their own environment. The agents are domain-agnostic, and they know how to onboard, classify, and monitor. The domain is interchangeable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about the AWS services used in this solution:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/unified-studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Automated Reasoning policy refinement in Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/automated-reasoning-policy-refinement-in-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Nafi Diallo]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 03 Aug 2026 16:30:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d495eca7aa31244e52ef472f69e6a18cc949f604</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock now supports automatic Automated Reasoning policy refinement. The refinement engine diagnoses failing tests and proposes formal-logic fixes for rule issues and language issues, and you approve every change before it takes effect. This post walks through both refinement modes with complete API and console workflows.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Refining an Automated Reasoning policy in Amazon Bedrock has been a manual cycle of diagnose, hand-edit, retest, and repeat. Today, we are announcing automatic policy refinement, which automates the diagnose-and-fix work in that cycle. The refinement engine diagnoses failing tests and proposes formal-logic fixes. You approve every change before it takes effect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails-automated-reasoning-checks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automated Reasoning checks&lt;/a&gt; in Amazon Bedrock Guardrails use formal verification to prove answer correctness. On unambiguous translations from natural language to formal logic, they deliver up to 99% verification accuracy, as reported in the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/minimize-ai-hallucinations-and-deliver-up-to-99-verification-accuracy-with-automated-reasoning-checks-now-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GA announcement&lt;/a&gt;. To get started, you build an Automated Reasoning policy from a source document and validate it with test cases. Customers told us that this iterative tuning creates the biggest friction point in policy development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through two new refinement modes: &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; for rule issues, and &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; for language issues. For each mode, we show a complete API workflow (start, poll, retrieve) and a repeatable console workflow for turning failing policies into passing ones.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-automated-reasoning-checks-actually-are"&gt;What Automated Reasoning checks actually are&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automated Reasoning checks translate natural language into formal logic, then apply automated reasoning techniques to produce a finding: &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;SATISFIABLE&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;IMPOSSIBLE&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;. For a full introduction to how policies work, refer to our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/minimize-ai-hallucinations-and-deliver-up-to-99-verification-accuracy-with-automated-reasoning-checks-now-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GA announcement post&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this post, the key concept is the &lt;strong&gt;two-step validation pipeline&lt;/strong&gt;. First, the &lt;strong&gt;translate&lt;/strong&gt; step maps natural-language input/output to variable assignments using the variable descriptions in your policy. Second, the &lt;strong&gt;validate&lt;/strong&gt; step applies your formal rules to those assignments. When a test fails, the root cause lives in one of those two steps, and each refinement mode targets a different one. Figure 1 traces that pipeline end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21259-1.png" alt="How Automated Reasoning checks validate a response at runtime" width="800" height="1802"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: How Automated Reasoning checks validate a response at runtime. Automated Reasoning checks translate natural language into variables using the policy’s variable descriptions, then validate those variables against the policy’s formal rules to return a finding. This two-step pipeline is why refinement has two modes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Testing your policy.&lt;/strong&gt; You validate a policy by attaching tests: each test is input/output text plus the result you expect. Run tests individually or as a batch. Failures tell you exactly where the policy diverges from your intent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-policies-need-refinement-two-failure-modes"&gt;Why policies need refinement: Two failure modes&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Recall the two-step pipeline: &lt;strong&gt;translate&lt;/strong&gt; (natural language to variable assignments) and then &lt;strong&gt;validate&lt;/strong&gt; (formal logic to finding). A failed test means one of these steps produced something you didn’t expect. Automated Reasoning checks surface two distinct failure signals that map cleanly to each step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="failure-mode-1-rule-issues-the-logic-is-wrong"&gt;Failure mode 1: Rule issues (the logic is wrong)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In a rule-issue failure, the translation works correctly: the right variables have the right values, but the validation result doesn’t match your expectation. The problem lives in your rules: a rule is too permissive, too restrictive, or missing entirely. Concretely, you expected &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt; but got &lt;code&gt;SATISFIABLE&lt;/code&gt; because a missing or too-permissive rule lets a bad answer through. Or you expected &lt;code&gt;SATISFIABLE&lt;/code&gt; but got &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt; because an overly strict rule blocks a correct answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mental model:&lt;/strong&gt; The system understood the question perfectly but applied the wrong logic. You need to fix the &lt;em&gt;rules&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="failure-mode-2-translation-ambiguous-the-language-is-wrong"&gt;Failure mode 2: Translation ambiguous (the language is wrong)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a test returns &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;, the validation engine runs and produces different outcomes depending on which interpretation it follows. In some cases, the translation models disagreed on how to map the natural-language input to your policy’s variables, and each competing interpretation led to a different validation result. The finding surfaces two or more options, each with its own translation and conclusion, plus &lt;code&gt;differenceScenarios&lt;/code&gt; showing where the interpretations diverge in practice. Common root causes include overlapping variable definitions (“tenure” compared to “years of service”), vague descriptions, and inconsistent value formats (5 compared to 0.05 for “5%”).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="matching-mode-to-failure"&gt;Matching mode to failure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This table summarizes which refinement mode addresses which failure type:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Failure mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Root cause&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Refinement mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it does&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Rule issues&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Logic is wrong&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Proposes rule or variable additions, edits, or deletions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Translation ambiguous&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Language is ambiguous&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Proposes clearer variable descriptions that collapse multiple interpretations into one&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; when the system cannot determine a single translation. The next two sections walk through each mode in turn: what it does, when to use it, how the review gate works, and how to launch it programmatically. We start with Iterative Refinement because rule-issue failures are the more common case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="iterative-refinement-fixing-the-rules"&gt;Iterative Refinement: Fixing the rules&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When tests fail because the logic is wrong (the translation is clean but the validation result doesn’t match your expectation), the problem lives in your rules. &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;ITERATIVELY_REFINE_POLICY&lt;/code&gt;) automates the diagnose-and-fix cycle so you don’t need to manually trace each rule, hypothesize a correction, and hand-edit formal logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a policy with 10–30 rules. Previously, a fix would take a subject matter expert multiple rounds of manual diagnosis and hand-editing of SMT-LIB formal logic. That work now compresses to a single review-and-approve step, with no formal logic written by hand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Iterative Refinement takes three inputs. The first is your existing policy definition (the current rules, variables, and types). The second is a source document containing the authoritative natural-language text that describes how things &lt;em&gt;should&lt;/em&gt; work. The third input is optional: natural language feedback with explicit instructions describing the change you want.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, the feedback field might contain: &lt;em&gt;“Update the tenure requirement for parental leave from 12 months to 6 months, as specified in section 3 of the revised document.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Given these inputs, the refinement engine analyzes how the current rules diverge from the source document and your feedback. It proposes a set of candidate changes (new rules, edited rules, added variables) that bring the policy in line.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-convergence-loop"&gt;The convergence loop&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Iterative Refinement, as the name suggests, iterates. Behind the scenes, the engine generates a candidate change, simulates its effect on your saved tests, checks whether the previously failing tests now pass, and adjusts if they don’t. This can involve several internal cycles for a single request, especially when a fix in one rule ripples into others. The iteration happens &lt;em&gt;internally&lt;/em&gt;, though: you don’t observe each intermediate attempt, and you don’t need to shepherd it. What you receive is the converged result: a proposed diff that shows exactly which rules changed, which variables changed, and how the change affects every test in your suite.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-review-gate"&gt;The review gate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After convergence, the &lt;strong&gt;Review policy changes&lt;/strong&gt; screen appears.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You then select &lt;strong&gt;Accept changes&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Discard changes&lt;/strong&gt;. Accepting writes the changes to your DRAFT policy. Discarding leaves everything exactly as it was.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites-and-when-to-use"&gt;Prerequisites and when to use&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; requires at least one test attached to your policy. Without a failing test signal, there’s nothing to drive the refinement. Use this mode when the translation is correct (right variables, right values) but the validation result is unexpected. Do not use it when the finding is &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;. That’s a language problem better addressed by &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="through-the-api"&gt;Through the API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Refinement runs as an asynchronous build workflow. Using the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3), the flow has four steps: export the current policy definition, start the workflow, poll for completion, and retrieve the proposed changes. Set &lt;code&gt;buildWorkflowType&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;ITERATIVELY_REFINE_POLICY&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The iterativeRefinementContent block accepts one to five source documents (required) and up to 4,000 characters of optional feedback:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock")

# Export the current policy definition (required input to the workflow).
policy_definition = bedrock.export_automated_reasoning_policy_version(
    policyArn=policy_arn,
)["policyDefinition"]

with open("hr-leave-policy.pdf", "rb") as f:
    source_document = f.read()

response = bedrock.start_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow(
    policyArn=policy_arn,
    buildWorkflowType="ITERATIVELY_REFINE_POLICY",
    sourceContent={
        "policyDefinition": policy_definition,
        "workflowContent": {
            "iterativeRefinementContent": {
                "documents": [
                    {
                        "document": source_document,
                        "documentName": "hr-leave-policy.pdf",
                        "documentContentType": "pdf",
                    }
                ],
                "feedback": "Update the tenure requirement from 12 to 6 months per section 3.",
            }
        },
    },
)

build_workflow_id = response["buildWorkflowId"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The call returns immediately with a &lt;code&gt;buildWorkflowId&lt;/code&gt;, not the proposed changes. The workflow moves from &lt;code&gt;SCHEDULED&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;BUILDING&lt;/code&gt; until it reaches &lt;code&gt;COMPLETED&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;FAILED&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;CANCELLED&lt;/code&gt;. Convergence typically takes one to a few minutes, depending on policy size. Poll &lt;code&gt;get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow&lt;/code&gt; until the status reaches a terminal state. Then retrieve the converged proposal with &lt;code&gt;get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow_result_assets&lt;/code&gt;, requesting the &lt;code&gt;POLICY_DEFINITION&lt;/code&gt; asset to see the updated rules (and &lt;code&gt;BUILD_LOG&lt;/code&gt; for the action log):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import time

while True:
    workflow = bedrock.get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow(
        policyArn=policy_arn,
        buildWorkflowId=build_workflow_id,
    )
    status = workflow["status"]
    if status in ("COMPLETED", "FAILED", "CANCELLED"):
        break
    time.sleep(10)

if status == "COMPLETED":
    assets = bedrock.get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow_result_assets(
        policyArn=policy_arn,
        buildWorkflowId=build_workflow_id,
        assetType="POLICY_DEFINITION",
    )
    proposed_definition = assets["buildWorkflowAssets"]["policyDefinition"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The returned policy definition is the proposed DRAFT, the full new definition. To commit it, call &lt;code&gt;update_automated_reasoning_policy&lt;/code&gt; with this definition. To see what changed, diff it against the policy definition you exported before starting the workflow. The console Review policy changes screen wraps this same start-poll-retrieve sequence, rendering the diff behind the Accept changes and Discard changes buttons.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="ambiguous-variable-refinement-fixing-the-language"&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement: Fixing the language&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Iterative Refinement handles rule issues, but not every failing test is a rule issue. When the translation itself is unstable, no amount of rule-editing will help. You need to fix the language the policy uses to describe its variables. That is what Ambiguous Variable Refinement does. It follows the same asynchronous start-poll-retrieve pattern and lands on the same review-and-accept screen. The difference is in the proposals. They center on variable descriptions and merges, with rule and type updates applied as needed to keep the policy consistent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When tests produce &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt; results (refer to failure mode 2 earlier in this post), competing translations lead to different validation outcomes. Ambiguity can also come from how the validated content itself is phrased. This section focuses on ambiguity in the policy variables.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-it-works-1"&gt;How it works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Translation ambiguity, because of policy variable issues, typically stems from a handful of root causes. Overlapping variables occur when two variables describe the same concept. For example, tenureMonths (“How long the employee has worked in months”) and monthsOfService (“The employee’s months of service”) both capture employment duration. As a result, translation models disagree on which one to use. Incomplete descriptions arise when a variable’s description is too vague to guide translation. Inconsistent value formatting creates ambiguity when the system can’t determine if “5%” should become interestRate = 5 or interestRate = 0.05. Logic baked into variable names creates confusion. A name like timelyReportingNotFeasible already contains a negation, so expressing the positive case requires negating a negative. Translation models often drop one of the two.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These are only the most common patterns. Because detection works by exercising the policy’s variables in translation rather than checking against a fixed list of known problems, variable-level issues that causes translations to disagree can surface.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you run &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;, it pinpoints which variable descriptions or overlapping definitions cause the disagreement, then proposes refined descriptions that collapse multiple interpretations into one precise definition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These refined descriptions incorporate unit conversion rules, synonyms, alternative phrasings, and explicit format guidance. This before/after example illustrates a typical proposal:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After (proposed)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;tenureMonths : “How long the employee has worked in months.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;tenureMonths : “The number of complete months the employee has been continuously employed. When users mention years of service, convert to months (for example, 2 years = 24 months). This variable captures references to employment duration, length of service, time at the company, or seniority.”&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If overlapping variables are detected, a merge may also be proposed: one variable is deleted and rules that reference it are updated to use the surviving variable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-review-gate-1"&gt;The review gate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Just like &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;, you review the proposed changes before anything is applied.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It also displays Test results, where tests that previously returned &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt; now produce a definitive &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;SATISFIABLE&lt;/code&gt; result. You select &lt;strong&gt;Accept changes&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Discard changes&lt;/strong&gt;. No change touches your DRAFT policy until you approve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="when-to-use-it"&gt;When to use it&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; when tests produce &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt; results, or when you inspect a &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt;/&lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt; finding and discover that the translation assigned values to the wrong variables. Do not use it when the translation is correct but the validation result is unexpected. That’s a rule problem for &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="through-the-api-1"&gt;Through the API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; uses the same asynchronous start-poll-retrieve pattern. Set &lt;code&gt;buildWorkflowType&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;RESOLVE_POLICY_AMBIGUITIES&lt;/code&gt;. This mode analyzes your policy’s variables directly and needs neither a source document nor attached tests, so workflowContent can be omitted. The current policy definition is still required in &lt;code&gt;sourceContent&lt;/code&gt;. Export it first with &lt;code&gt;export_automated_reasoning_policy_version&lt;/code&gt; as shown for &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; earlier:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = bedrock.start_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow(
    policyArn=policy_arn,
    buildWorkflowType="RESOLVE_POLICY_AMBIGUITIES",
    sourceContent={
        "policyDefinition": policy_definition,
    },
)

build_workflow_id = response["buildWorkflowId"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As with &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;, the response is a &lt;code&gt;buildWorkflowId&lt;/code&gt;. Poll &lt;code&gt;get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow&lt;/code&gt; until the status reaches a terminal state. Then call &lt;code&gt;get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow_result_assets&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;assetType="POLICY_DEFINITION"&lt;/code&gt; to retrieve the proposed variable descriptions and merges:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;while True:
    workflow = bedrock.get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow(
        policyArn=policy_arn,
        buildWorkflowId=build_workflow_id,
    )
    status = workflow["status"]
    if status in ("COMPLETED", "FAILED", "CANCELLED"):
        break
    time.sleep(10)

if status == "COMPLETED":
    assets = bedrock.get_automated_reasoning_policy_build_workflow_result_assets(
        policyArn=policy_arn,
        buildWorkflowId=build_workflow_id,
        assetType="POLICY_DEFINITION",
    )
    proposed_definition = assets["buildWorkflowAssets"]["policyDefinition"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The changes remain a proposal until you accept them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="you-approve-every-change-the-human-in-the-loop-gate"&gt;You approve every change: The human-in-the-loop gate&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both refinement modes share one non-negotiable property: &lt;strong&gt;no change takes effect until you say so.&lt;/strong&gt; The refinement engine has &lt;em&gt;suggestion authority.&lt;/em&gt; It can analyze, diagnose, and propose. You have &lt;em&gt;commit authority&lt;/em&gt;. You decide what reaches your DRAFT policy and, ultimately, production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-five-step-loop"&gt;The five-step loop&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Regardless of which refinement mode you use, the workflow follows the same five-step pattern. First, test: run your saved tests against the current policy. Second, check: identify which tests don’t match their expected result. Third, propose: the system generates candidate fixes for rules or variable descriptions. Fourth, review: you inspect the proposed diff and its impact on the tests. Fifth, apply: you accept the changes into DRAFT, or reject and nothing changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you accept, re-test to confirm the fix resolved the issue without breaking other tests. This creates a ratchet: each cycle either moves you closer to a passing policy or gives you new diagnostic information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-the-review-screen-shows-you"&gt;What the review screen shows you&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The review screen lets you understand the &lt;em&gt;ramifications&lt;/em&gt; of a change, not just the change itself. It answers two questions at once: what did the engine propose, and how does that proposal affect every test you care about?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The proposal comes in three sections. &lt;strong&gt;Changes to rules&lt;/strong&gt; lists the rules added, edited, or deleted, with the formal-logic expression for each. &lt;strong&gt;Changes to variables&lt;/strong&gt; shows updated variable descriptions and added or deleted variables, with the previous wording alongside the proposed wording so you can compare the two directly. &lt;strong&gt;Changes to custom variable types&lt;/strong&gt; covers changes to the policy’s enumerated types.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Alongside those changes, the screen shows a Test results section that lists the saved test with its previous and new outcome. Each row gives the expected finding and whether the test passed before and after. Choose View findings on a row to see the finding itself. This view is the single most important indicator on the screen. If your failing tests now pass and your passing tests still pass, you can accept with confidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validate-with-the-fidelity-report"&gt;Validate with the Fidelity Report&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After applying changes, generate a &lt;strong&gt;Fidelity Report&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;GENERATE_FIDELITY_REPORT&lt;/code&gt;) to validate that your updated policy still faithfully represents your source document. The report provides three measurements. The &lt;strong&gt;coverage score&lt;/strong&gt; (0.0–1.0) indicates how much of your source document is represented in the policy. The &lt;strong&gt;accuracy score&lt;/strong&gt; (0.0–1.0) indicates how faithfully the rules match the intent of the original document. &lt;strong&gt;Per-rule grounding&lt;/strong&gt; links each rule to the specific source-document statements that support it, with justifications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Compare Fidelity Reports before and after refinement. If your accuracy score drops, the proposed fix may have drifted from your source material, which is a signal to reject or iterate further.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-principle"&gt;The principle&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automatic refinement accelerates the &lt;em&gt;labor&lt;/em&gt; of diagnosing failures and generating candidate fixes. It does not accelerate the &lt;em&gt;authority&lt;/em&gt; to change what your guardrail enforces. Every fix remains a suggestion until you decide to accept it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="steering-the-engine-source-documents-and-custom-feedback"&gt;Steering the engine: Source documents and custom feedback&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can steer both modes by providing context that guides the system toward the right fix faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="source-documents"&gt;Source documents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you launch &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;, you supply a source document representing the ground truth your policy should encode. The console offers three modes for supplying it: &lt;strong&gt;Recently used&lt;/strong&gt; re-selects a previously uploaded doc, &lt;strong&gt;Upload&lt;/strong&gt; takes a new PDF or text file, and &lt;strong&gt;Enter text&lt;/strong&gt; accepts pasted content directly. The clearer and more focused the document, the more precise the proposals.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="custom-feedback-explicit-if-then-guidance"&gt;Custom feedback: Explicit if-then guidance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also provide &lt;strong&gt;natural language feedback&lt;/strong&gt; that tells the system exactly what to fix. Effective feedback is specific and testable. Vague feedback like “Fix the tenure rule” gives the engine too much latitude. Compare it with a specific, testable alternative: “If an employee is full-time and has worked for more than 6 months (not 12), they should be eligible for parental leave.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your feedback acts as a constraint: the system generates changes that satisfy both the source document and your guidance. If the two conflict, the conflict is flagged for your review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can provide both a source document and feedback together. When your document is dense, feedback focuses the system on the specific section that matters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="end-to-end-walkthroughs"&gt;End-to-end walkthroughs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section provides two walkthroughs, one for each refinement mode. Together they give you a repeatable workflow for both failure types.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To use automatic policy refinement with Automated Reasoning checks in Amazon Bedrock, make sure you have met the following prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An active AWS account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirmation of AWS Regions where Automated Reasoning checks is available, and access to Amazon Bedrock in one of them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;IAM permissions to create, view, and refine Automated Reasoning policies and to work with Amazon Bedrock Guardrails.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Automated Reasoning policy in your account, created from a source document that captures the rules you want to enforce. For step-by-step instructions, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/create-automated-reasoning-policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Create your Automated Reasoning policy&lt;/a&gt;. You need this policy to have at least one attached test — Iterative Refinement requires a failing test signal to drive its diagnosis. To add tests, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/test-automated-reasoning-policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Test an Automated Reasoning policy&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="walkthrough-a-fixing-a-rule-issue-with-iterative-refinement"&gt;Walkthrough A: Fixing a rule issue with Iterative Refinement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your HR leave-eligibility policy has a failing test. The assistant reports that a &lt;strong&gt;part-time employee with 8 months of tenure qualifies for parental leave&lt;/strong&gt;. The test expects &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt;, but the policy returns &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-1-validate-the-tests"&gt;Step 1: Validate the tests&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Bedrock console, navigate to &lt;strong&gt;Automated Reasoning&lt;/strong&gt; and open your policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the &lt;strong&gt;Tests&lt;/strong&gt; tab, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Validate all tests&lt;/strong&gt;. Figure 2 shows the results. Three of the four tests pass, and the parental-leave test fails with an unexpected &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt; finding.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21259-2.png" alt="Test results after validation (75% pass rate)." width="800" height="1654"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Tests tab after validation, with three of four tests passing (75%)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-2-inspect-the-failing-finding"&gt;Step 2: Inspect the failing finding&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the failing test’s finding and inspect the translation, shown in Figure 3. The variables are correctly captured: 
  &lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;monthsOfContinuousService = 8.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;employmentStatus = &lt;code&gt;PART_TIME&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21259-3.png" alt="The failing test’s finding and its translation." width="800" height="1704"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The failing test’s finding, with the translation showing correctly captured variables&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The finding is &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt; because the only supporting rule grants eligibility after 6 months of continuous service, and no rule excludes part-time employees. The translation is clean and the logic is wrong, so this is a rule issue.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-3-refine-the-policy"&gt;Step 3: Refine the policy&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Refine policy&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Automatically refine policy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the refinement type to &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Provide a source document using any of the three modes: 
  &lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recently used&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Upload (a new version)&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enter text&lt;/strong&gt; (paste the relevant paragraph).&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Optionally, add custom feedback to focus the fix, for example: “&lt;em&gt;Add a rule that explicitly makes part-time employees ineligible for parental leave.&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When your inputs are ready, choose &lt;strong&gt;Start&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4 shows the completed setup, with the refinement type selected, the source document attached, and the custom feedback entered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136315" style="width: 1440px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136315" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136315" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ml-21259-figure4.png" alt="Figure 4: Iterative Refinement setup with a source document and custom feedback" width="1430" height="540"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136315" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Iterative Refinement setup with a source document and custom feedback&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-4-review-and-accept-the-changes"&gt;Step 4: Review and accept the changes&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When convergence completes, the &lt;strong&gt;Review policy changes&lt;/strong&gt; screen appears. Under&amp;nbsp;&lt;strong&gt;Changes to rules&lt;/strong&gt;, it lists two changes: one deleted rule and one added rule. The engine removed the rule that granted eligibility on continuous service alone: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li style="list-style-type: none"&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;if &lt;code&gt;monthsOfContinuousService&lt;/code&gt; is at least 6, then &lt;code&gt;isEligibleForParentalLeave&lt;/code&gt; is &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;And added a rule that excludes part-time employees:&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li style="list-style-type: none"&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;if &lt;code&gt;employmentStatus&lt;/code&gt; is equal to &lt;code&gt;PART_TIME&lt;/code&gt;, then &lt;code&gt;isEligibleForParentalLeave&lt;/code&gt; is false&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Test results&lt;/strong&gt;, the part-time test moves from &lt;strong&gt;Failed&lt;/strong&gt; to &lt;strong&gt;Passed&lt;/strong&gt;, and the other three tests remain Passed. Figure 5 shows the screen before you accept.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136311" style="width: 798px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136311" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136311" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ml-21259-figure5.png" alt="Figure 5: The Review policy changes screen. Changes to rules lists the deleted tenure-only eligibility rule and the added part-time exclusion, and Test results shows the part-time test moving from Failed to Passed with no regressions." width="788" height="810"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136311" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The Review policy changes screen. Changes to rules lists the deleted tenure-only eligibility rule and the added part-time exclusion, and Test results shows the part-time test moving from Failed to Passed with no regressions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Accept changes&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-5-confirm-the-fix"&gt;Step 5: Confirm the fix&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Return to the &lt;strong&gt;Tests&lt;/strong&gt; tab and choose &lt;strong&gt;Validate all tests&lt;/strong&gt; again.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Figure 6 shows the expected result. The previously failing test now passes, and the pass rate reaches 100%.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136316" style="width: 1440px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136316" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136316" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ml-21259-figure5-1.png" alt="Figure 6: The Tests tab after refinement, with all four tests passing (100%)" width="1430" height="516"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136316" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The Tests tab after refinement, with all four tests passing (100%)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;If tests still fail or regressions appear&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There is no guarantee that a single refinement cycle resolves all failures or avoids regressions. If the previously failing test still fails, or if other tests that previously passed are now failing:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Discard changes&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;on the review screen.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add more specific custom feedback describing what the engine should fix differently. For example, &lt;em&gt;“The rule should exclude part-time employees entirely, not only reduce their eligibility window.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Narrow the source document to the exact section covering the failing rule.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; again with these tighter inputs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the test still won’t pass after two or three rounds, fall back to manual editing. Use the failing finding’s translation and rule trace as a guide to hand-edit the specific rule in the policy editor.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-6-report-and-deploy"&gt;Step 6: Report and deploy&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Generate Fidelity Report&lt;/strong&gt; and compare scores with the previous report.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When satisfied, create a &lt;strong&gt;numbered version&lt;/strong&gt; to deploy.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="walkthrough-b-fixing-a-language-issue-with-ambiguous-variable-refinement"&gt;Walkthrough B: Fixing a language issue with Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You have the same HR policy, but this time a test returns &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;. The finding shows two options:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One interpreted “2 years of service” as tenureMonths = 24.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The other as monthsOfService = 24.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two options reveal overlapping variables. Both tenureMonths and monthsOfService describe employment duration, and the translation models can’t agree on which to use. This overlap is the root cause of the ambiguity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-1-refine-the-policy"&gt;Step 1: Refine the policy&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Refine policy&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;As Figure 7 shows, no source document is required for this mode, so the setup is a single selection.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21259-6.png" alt="Ambiguous Variable Refinement setup." width="800" height="1560"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Ambiguous Variable Refinement setup, with no source document required&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Start&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-2-review-and-accept-the-changes"&gt;Step 2: Review and accept the changes&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Review policy changes&lt;/strong&gt; screen, review the proposed variable merge: 
  &lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;monthsOfService is deleted.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Rules are updated to reference tenureMonths.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;The description of tenureMonths expanded to: 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The number of complete months the employee has been continuously employed. When users mention years of service, convert to months (for example, 2 years = 24 months). This variable captures all references to employment duration, length of service, time at the company, or seniority.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Test results&lt;/strong&gt;, the previously ambiguous test now produces a definitive &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt; result.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Accept changes&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-3-confirm-the-fix"&gt;Step 3: Confirm the fix&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Return to the &lt;strong&gt;Tests&lt;/strong&gt; tab and choose &lt;strong&gt;Validate all tests&lt;/strong&gt; again.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Figure 8 shows the confirmation. The test that previously returned &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt; now produces a definitive &lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt; finding and passes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ML-21259-7.png" alt="Tests tab after the ambiguity refinement is applied." width="800" height="1588"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Tests tab after the ambiguity refinement is applied&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-4-report-and-deploy"&gt;Step 4: Report and deploy&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Generate Fidelity Report&lt;/strong&gt; and compare the scores with the previous report.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When you are satisfied with the results, create a numbered version to deploy.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="note"&gt;Note&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In both refinement modes, there is no guarantee that the test will pass or the proposed changes don’t introduce regressions. If the test is not passing or other passing tests are now failing, you can discard changes and try again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-and-real-world-use-cases"&gt;Best practices and real-world use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The refinement engine works best when you give it a clear signal and a bounded change. The following practices show how, and the use cases show where they pay off.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following practices come from the two most common ways refinement goes sideways. Either you drive it with an ambiguous failure signal, or you let it change more of the policy than you intended.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Read the translation before you pick a mode.&lt;/strong&gt; Open the failing finding and look at the variable assignments first. If the right variables have the right values but the result is wrong, that’s a rule issue for &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;. If the assignments are wrong, or the finding is &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;, that’s a language issue for &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;. Choosing the mode by symptom rather than by inspecting the translation is the most frequent cause of a refinement that “fixes” the wrong thing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope the inputs to bound the change.&lt;/strong&gt; Refine against a source document scoped to a single part of your policy (for example, parental-leave eligibility). Pair it with specific if-then feedback when you need only one rule fixed. Focused inputs produce tighter, more reviewable proposals than pointing the engine at a 40-page handbook with vague guidance like “fix the tenure rule.”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trust Test results over the diff.&lt;/strong&gt; The review screen shows both the changed rules and their effect on every saved test. The Test results panel is the decision-maker: accept when your failing tests flip to passing and your passing tests stay green. A clean-looking diff that regresses a previously passing test is not a fix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compare Fidelity Reports before you version.&lt;/strong&gt; Refinement optimizes to make tests pass. That optimization can pull a rule away from what your source document actually says. After accepting, compare the accuracy score to the pre-refinement run. A drop signals drift, and is a reason to reject and iterate rather than re-run blindly. Once tests pass and the report holds, create a numbered version to promote the change to production.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="real-world-use-cases"&gt;Real-world use cases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These patterns apply across regulated industries. In HR eligibility scenarios, the employee handbook updates annually, and Iterative Refinement ingests the new document and proposes rule changes so the HR team can review without touching formal logic. In financial services, overlapping variables such as debtToIncomeRatio and DTI cause translation ambiguity across customer phrasings, and Ambiguous Variable Refinement consolidates them to support deterministic validation. When clinical protocols are revised, healthcare compliance teams upload the new guideline. Iterative Refinement proposes rule updates, and the team validates the proposal before pushing to production. In manufacturing QA, tolerances tighten over time, and the team refines policies iteratively while maintaining an audit trail through versioned snapshots and Fidelity Reports.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams shipping generative AI into regulated domains have paid a hidden tax: policy maintenance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automatic policy refinement changes the labor equation without changing the authority equation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The rules-vs-language dichotomy.&lt;/strong&gt; When the logic is wrong (rules that are too permissive, too strict, or missing), &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; proposes formal-logic fixes grounded in your source document and feedback. When the language is wrong (overlapping variables, vague descriptions, inconsistent formats), &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; proposes precise variable definitions that collapse competing interpretations into one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The approval gate.&lt;/strong&gt; In both cases, the system suggests and you decide. Every proposed change surfaces on a review screen that shows the diff &lt;em&gt;and&lt;/em&gt; its impact on your saved tests. No change reaches your DRAFT policy, let alone production, without your explicit approval. Automatic in labor. Guided in authority.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="your-next-step"&gt;Your next step&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you don’t yet have an Automated Reasoning policy with attached tests, start by creating a policy from a source document and adding tests that cover your key scenarios. Refer to the &lt;a href="#prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/a&gt; section for links. With a policy and at least one failing test in hand, pick one failing test today and inspect the finding. If the translation is correct but the result is wrong, run &lt;strong&gt;Iterative Refinement&lt;/strong&gt; with a focused source document. If the result is &lt;code&gt;TRANSLATION_AMBIGUOUS&lt;/code&gt;, run &lt;strong&gt;Ambiguous Variable Refinement&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Accept the proposal, re-test, and compare Fidelity Reports before and after. You will have a tighter policy, verified to up to 99% accuracy on unambiguous translations (see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/minimize-ai-hallucinations-and-deliver-up-to-99-verification-accuracy-with-automated-reasoning-checks-now-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GA announcement&lt;/a&gt;), without writing a single line of formal logic by hand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h3&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Get started:&lt;/strong&gt; Explore the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails-automated-reasoning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automated Reasoning checks documentation&lt;/a&gt; for setup guidance, or open your existing policy in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; and select &lt;strong&gt;Refine policy&lt;/strong&gt;. &lt;h3 id="about-the-99-accuracy-figure"&gt;About the 99% accuracy figure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The 99% figure cited in the introduction and conclusion refers to verification accuracy that measures the rate of correct findings (&lt;code&gt;VALID&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;INVALID&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;SATISFIABLE&lt;/code&gt;) given an unambiguous translation from natural language to formal logic. It was first published in the GA announcement, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/minimize-ai-hallucinations-and-deliver-up-to-99-verification-accuracy-with-automated-reasoning-checks-now-available/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Minimize AI hallucinations and deliver up to 99% verification accuracy with Automated Reasoning checks: Now available&lt;/a&gt; (AWS News Blog, August 6, 2025).&lt;/p&gt;
