		<item>
		<title>Announcing the Agentic Catalog Experience in Amazon Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/announcing-the-agentic-catalog-experience-in-amazon-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Srikanth Baheti]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 31 Jul 2026 19:53:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7566a36ce07e1d565d3c4c626b0957bbfa8d654a</guid>

					<description>Amazon Quick introduces the Agentic Catalog Experience, an AI-powered workflow for data curators to discover upstream catalog assets in natural language and auto-create Datasets and Topics with inherited semantics. Now in preview for AWS Glue Data Catalog and Databricks Unity Catalog.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As organizations embrace AI-powered analytics, the value of a natural language (Text2SQL) answer is only as good as the business context behind it. We’re entering a phase where semantic richness (table and column descriptions, and relationships) must flow directly from where it’s authored in upstream data catalogs and semantic tools into the AI products that serve end users. Products like Amazon Quick can no longer operate in isolation. They need to natively consume and reason over the definitions, relationships, and governance metadata that data teams curate in systems like AWS Glue Data Catalog and Databricks Unity Catalog. This shift from siloed metadata to connected, catalog-aware AI is what enables intelligent analytics at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-bridging-the-last-mile"&gt;The challenge: Bridging the last mile&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-investment-is-done"&gt;The investment is done&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enterprise data teams have done the hard work. They have invested heavily in upstream catalog platforms such as AWS Glue, Databricks Unity Catalog, Snowflake Horizon, Collibra, and dbt. On these platforms, they meticulously define table descriptions, column semantics, primary and foreign key relationships, glossary terms, and metric definitions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Yet when it comes to enabling end users (such as sales managers, marketing directors, and finance leads) for production-ready AI and trusted dashboards, a significant gap remains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="three-compounding-challenges"&gt;Three compounding challenges&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When data curators (business intelligence engineers, analytics leads, and senior analysts) need to enable their business users in Amazon Quick, they face three compounding challenges:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limited discoverability&lt;/strong&gt;: With thousands of tables in enterprise catalogs, discovering the right upstream assets that are curated and approved for reporting is a needle-in-a-haystack problem. There’s no way to describe what you need and have the system find it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Semantic fragmentation and manual recreation&lt;/strong&gt;: Rich metadata that already exists upstream (business descriptions on tables and columns, and primary and foreign key relationships) does not flow through. Curators must recreate assets from scratch, redefine descriptions, and reconcile definitions manually. Does “revenue” mean gross or net? Does “active customer” mean a purchase within 30 days or 90 days? These definitions exist upstream but require manual re-entry.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time to insight in weeks, not hours&lt;/strong&gt;: The combination of manual discovery and manual recreation means that the time from data to actionable insights stretches from hours to weeks. Worse, when upstream definitions change, manually created semantics in Quick Datasets become stale, causing semantic drift that erodes trust in AI answers and dashboards over time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-gap"&gt;The gap&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The problem isn’t upstream. The metadata exists. The governance is defined. The relationships are mapped.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The problem is the last mile: translating that rich catalog context into a curated, consumable experience that delivers grounded AI answers and deterministic dashboards end users can trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="introducing-the-agentic-catalog-experience-in-amazon-quick"&gt;Introducing the Agentic Catalog Experience in Amazon Quick&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we’re announcing the Agentic Catalog Experience in Amazon Quick, an AI-powered workflow that helps data curators rapidly define their context boundary, inherit upstream semantics, and enable end users for grounded Q&amp;amp;A and trusted dashboards at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the heart of this experience is the Quick Agent, scoped to discovery, creation, and inheritance tasks within the catalog context. It uses the semantic context from the catalog connection to summarize the entire catalog at a glance, engage the customer in natural language conversation, surface the most relevant tables and relationships based on the customer’s use case, and assess metadata readiness. Then, with a single conversational confirmation, it auto-creates Catalog-Generated Datasets and Topics with targeted metadata inherited from the upstream catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No manual configuration. No context-switching. No weeks of setup.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136414 alignnone" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/AgenticCatalogExperience.png" alt="" width="925" height="1257"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="natural-language-asset-discovery"&gt;Natural language asset discovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instead of scrolling through thousands of tables to find the right ones, curators use natural language. With the Agentic Catalog Experience, curators describe what they need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;Curator: “I’m a Senior Analyst on the Finance team. I need tables for quarterly revenue reporting and cost analysis.”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Quick Agent searches across your entire catalog to surface the most relevant tables instantly, using all available metadata including business descriptions, tags, Gold/Silver/Bronze classifications, quality scores, table health scores, and glossary terms. No more manual browsing. No more guessing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="bulk-agentic-dataset-creation"&gt;Bulk agentic dataset creation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the curator selects their tables, the Quick Agent creates catalog representations (Datasets) at scale in a single guided workflow. Your upstream catalog remains the source of truth because the default creation path is Direct Query. Datasets with inherited semantics are flagged with a clear “Semantics Inherited” badge, and their metadata is read-only. Authors can refresh inherited metadata on demand by choosing the sync button to stay aligned with their catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;Quick Agent: “Creating 6 Catalog-Generated Datasets now: &lt;code&gt;revenue_by_region&lt;/code&gt; created (DirectQuery, read-only metadata), &lt;code&gt;cost_centers&lt;/code&gt; created, and &lt;code&gt;gl_transactions&lt;/code&gt; created.”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="semantic-and-relationship-inheritance"&gt;Semantic and relationship inheritance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Quick Agent carries forward targeted metadata from your catalog into the assets it creates. Today, inheritance is deliberately focused on two key areas to avoid noise and keep Datasets clean:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Table and column definitions to Datasets&lt;/strong&gt;: Business descriptions and column definitions are inherited directly into the created Datasets, so that curators and end users have the semantic context they need.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Primary and foreign key relationships to Topics&lt;/strong&gt;: The Agent detects relationships and uses them to suggest and create multi-dataset constructs (Topics) with star and snowflake schema joins preconfigured.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note&lt;/strong&gt;: While all available metadata (Gold/Silver classifications, quality scores, tags, and health scores) is used during discovery to find the right tables, inheritance into Datasets is intentionally scoped to table and column definitions today. We plan to add more metadata types to Datasets over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;Quick Agent: “I detected 3 relationships between these tables and created a Topic called ‘Finance Revenue Model’ with the star schema joins preconfigured. Table and column definitions have been inherited from the upstream catalog.”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="immediate-consumption"&gt;Immediate consumption&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The curated Datasets and Topics are ready for use immediately:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ask questions&lt;/strong&gt;: Start a Q&amp;amp;A conversation with your new Datasets. The AI agent uses inherited business descriptions, glossary terms, and quality scores to deliver grounded answers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create dashboards&lt;/strong&gt;: Build deterministic visualizations with full semantic context already in place.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Share with end users&lt;/strong&gt;: Add Datasets to a Space and share them with business users for self-service Q&amp;amp;A.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After creation, the metadata tied to these Datasets and Topics feeds into the Amazon Quick semantic store, which powers re-ranking and unified context for AI-powered Q&amp;amp;A. Getting from catalog connection to the first business question takes minutes, not weeks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-consumer-not-catalog"&gt;Architecture: Consumer, not catalog&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A key design principle underpins this experience: Amazon Quick is a consumer of upstream catalog metadata, not a dedicated catalog itself. This means:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No data duplication&lt;/strong&gt;: Catalog-Generated Datasets use DirectQuery. No data is copied or moved.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metadata consumed for context&lt;/strong&gt;: Inherited semantics are read-only in Amazon Quick and flow into the semantic store to power re-ranking and AI answer grounding. Your upstream catalog remains the authoritative source.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual semantic sync&lt;/strong&gt;: Authors can refresh inherited metadata on demand by choosing the sync button. Scheduled automatic sync is on the roadmap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extensibility with transparency&lt;/strong&gt;: Catalog-Generated Datasets show inherited semantics as read-only (marked as catalog representations). If an Author chooses to edit a Dataset, Amazon Quick provides a clear notification that editing creates a custom Dataset and that semantic sync no longer applies. This gives Authors full control while preserving catalog integrity by default.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="supported-catalogs-today"&gt;Supported catalogs today&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Catalog platform&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Glue Data Catalog&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) Role ARN&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Databricks Unity Catalog&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OAuth 2.0 / Personal Access Token&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Support for additional catalog platforms is coming soon.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-gets-inherited"&gt;What gets inherited&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Metadata inheritance is intentionally focused to keep Datasets clean and production-ready:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="into-datasets-table-and-column-definitions"&gt;Into Datasets (table and column definitions)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Table business and technical descriptions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Column descriptions and display names.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data types and nullability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Glossary terms and synonyms.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="into-topics-relationships"&gt;Into Topics (relationships)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Primary and foreign key relationships.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Relationship definitions and cardinality.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Star and snowflake schema models.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-end-user-experience"&gt;The end-user experience&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here’s what this means for the business users downstream:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;A sales manager asks: “What were our Q4 sales by region?”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Behind the scenes, the AI agent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Searches Catalog-Generated Datasets using business descriptions and glossary terms.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Identifies the &lt;code&gt;sales.revenue_by_product&lt;/code&gt; table (Gold, 98 percent quality).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Applies preconfigured joins from the Topic to combine relevant dimensions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Respects personally identifiable information (PII) masking rules from catalog metadata.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Returns a grounded, trusted answer in seconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;No manual dataset configuration required. The curator defined the context boundary once with the Quick Agent, and every end user benefits immediately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="unified-enterprise-context"&gt;Unified enterprise context&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Agentic Catalog Experience doesn’t exist in isolation. Combined with the broader platform capabilities of Amazon Quick (including integration with Slack, Outlook, documents, and knowledge bases), end users get the full enterprise context:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Structured data&lt;/strong&gt; from catalogs through Catalog-Generated Datasets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unstructured context&lt;/strong&gt; from documents, email messages, and conversations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Business rules&lt;/strong&gt; from glossary terms and metric definitions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This unified context enables production-ready AI answers, grounded in your organization’s specific data and semantics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="connecting-to-aws-glue-data-catalog"&gt;Connecting to AWS Glue Data Catalog&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with the Agentic Catalog Experience, create a data source connection to your AWS Glue Data Catalog in Amazon Quick. After you establish the connection, the Quick Agent guides you through discovery, schema exploration, and Topic creation in a single conversational workflow. In this walkthrough, we connect to a Glue Data Catalog and build a Financial Analytics Topic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Amazon Quick, create a new data source. From the list of connection types, select &lt;strong&gt;Glue Data Catalog&lt;/strong&gt; (available in preview), and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Next&lt;/strong&gt;. This connection is for the metadata. With it, Amazon Quick can consume the table and column definitions and the relationships your teams have already curated in AWS Glue.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-1.png" alt="Amazon Quick new data source page with Glue Data Catalog selected from the connection types" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Selecting the Glue Data Catalog connection type in Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Glue Data Catalog connection works together with an Amazon Athena connection. Glue provides the metadata, and Athena provides the query path to the data itself in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3). Create the Athena data source as well, so that Amazon Quick can run queries against the underlying data. After you create both, the Data sources page shows the two entries side by side: the Glue Data Catalog source for the metadata and the Athena source for the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick Data sources page showing the Glue Data Catalog and Amazon Athena connections side by side" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: The Glue Data Catalog and Athena data sources listed together&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open the GDC-Demo data source detail page. Under &lt;strong&gt;Data connections&lt;/strong&gt;, you can see the linked Athena data source that Amazon Quick uses to query the data. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Explore data&lt;/strong&gt; to launch the Quick Agent scoped to this data source.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-3.png" alt="GDC-Demo data source detail page with the linked Athena connection and the Explore data option" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Launching the Quick Agent from the data source detail page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Quick Agent panel opens on the right side of the screen, automatically scoped to the Glue Data Catalog data source. The “Specific data” mode is selected, with “GDC-Demo” pinned as the context boundary. As a result, the Agent surfaces only metadata from this specific catalog connection.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-4.png" alt="Quick Agent panel scoped to the GDC-Demo Glue Data Catalog data source in Specific data mode" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: The Quick Agent scoped to a specific catalog connection&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ask the Agent to explore your catalog. The Agent summarizes the available catalogs and databases at a glance, so you can quickly see what is curated in your Glue Data Catalog. For this post, we use the “fa-demo” database as our example, a Finance Analytics Demo star schema for banking analytics. This walkthrough illustrates how the feature works and is not an exact scenario, so you can apply the same steps to your own catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-5.png" alt="Quick Agent summarizing the catalogs and databases available in the Glue Data Catalog" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: The Agent summarizing available catalogs and databases&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ask the Agent to explore the fa-demo database. The Agent identifies a classic star schema with 7 tables: 2 fact tables (&lt;code&gt;fact_transactions&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;fact_loans&lt;/code&gt;) and 5 dimension tables (&lt;code&gt;dim_account&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dim_date_transactions&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dim_date_loans&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dim_merchant&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;dim_txn_category&lt;/code&gt;). All are stored as external tables in Amazon S3. The Agent recognizes the schema as covering customer account transactions and loan portfolios, with supporting dimensions for merchants, transaction categories, and date hierarchies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-6.png" alt="Quick Agent describing the fa-demo star schema with two fact tables and five dimension tables" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: The Agent identifying the fact and dimension tables in the fa-demo database&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ask the Agent to create a star schema diagram for fa-demo. The Agent analyzes the tables, identifies the primary and foreign key relationships, and presents a complete logical data model with a schema summary. It highlights that &lt;code&gt;dim_account&lt;/code&gt; is the shared conformed dimension connecting both fact tables. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create datasets &amp;amp; Topic&lt;/strong&gt; to let the Agent build everything automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-7.png" alt="Star schema diagram generated by the Quick Agent showing dim_account connecting both fact tables" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: The generated logical data model for the fa-demo schema&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Agent creates a fully configured Topic with all Datasets and relationships in place. In this example, it creates the “Financial Analytics” Topic with all seven Datasets from the fa-demo database and 6 preconfigured star schema joins. Each Dataset carries its inherited business description, and the join relationships between the fact and dimension tables are validated automatically. The Topic is immediately ready for natural language Q&amp;amp;A, so you can ask questions like “What is the total transaction amount by merchant category?” or “Show me delinquent loans by risk rating.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-8.png" alt="Quick Agent showing the Financial Analytics Topic with seven Datasets and six preconfigured joins" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: The fully configured Financial Analytics Topic&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now, let’s see how the Financial Analytics Topic created from the Glue Data Catalog works in action. With the Topic pinned as context, end users can ask questions in plain language and get grounded answers instantly. For example, a user can ask “Total transaction amount by merchant category” and the Agent returns a ranked breakdown with key highlights. The user can then follow up with “Delinquent loans by risk rating” to see a risk-level summary with insights. Because the Datasets and relationships were inherited from the catalog, every answer is backed by the trusted schema, joins, and business definitions defined upstream. This is the power of the Agentic Catalog Experience: curators define the context boundary once, and every end user can explore the data conversationally from there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21554/ML-21554-9.gif" alt="Animation of a user asking questions and the Quick Agent returning ranked answers from the Financial Analytics Topic" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: Asking natural language questions against the Financial Analytics Topic&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="connecting-to-databricks-unity-catalog"&gt;Connecting to Databricks Unity Catalog&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same experience works with Databricks Unity Catalog. Here is a quick example that shows the full flow, from configuring the connection to creating Datasets and a Topic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a Databricks Unity Catalog data source, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Explore data&lt;/strong&gt; to launch the Quick Agent. The Agent summarizes the catalog, and with a single confirmation it creates the Datasets and a Topic with the star schema joins already configured.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21554/ML-21554-10.gif" alt="Animation of creating Databricks Unity Catalog Datasets and a Topic through the Quick Agent" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: Creating Datasets and a Topic from Databricks Unity Catalog&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the Topic is ready, end users can ask complex questions that span multiple related tables. In this example, the Agent answers “Top 5 brands by revenue per region” by joining across the Topic relationships, and returns a grounded, visual result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21554-11.gif" alt="Animation of the Quick Agent answering a multi-table question about top brands by revenue per region" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: Answering a multi-table question across Topic relationships&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-result"&gt;The result&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Curators deliver trusted data, full enterprise context, and production-ready AI answers and dashboards in a fraction of the time. End users get grounded answers they can trust, backed by Gold-standard data with full semantic lineage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;From weeks of manual configuration to minutes of guided conversation.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;That’s the Agentic Catalog Experience in Amazon Quick.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

At AWS, Salim works with customers globally to design and implement AI-powered BI and generative AI capabilities on Amazon Quick. Prior to AWS, he worked as a BI consultant across industry verticals including Automotive, Healthcare, Entertainment, Consumer, Publishing, and Financial Services, delivering business intelligence, data warehousing, data integration, and master data management solutions.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Optimizing production agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/optimizing-production-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-observability/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Joshua Lacy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 31 Jul 2026 15:33:11 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">64799e5f6577ed834ae5241ca5bcee136c4fc073</guid>

					<description>As your AI agents move from prototype to production, the challenge shifts from getting them to work to keeping them fast and efficient. Learn how to use Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability and Amazon CloudWatch to find performance bottlenecks and diagnose memory issues in long-running agent sessions.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As your AI agents move from prototype to production, the challenges shift from getting them to work to keeping them fast and efficient. In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/debugging-production-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-observability/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; of this series, we walked through debugging two common agent failures: infinite loops and tool invocation errors. Those scenarios dealt with agents that were broken. In this post, we tackle a different challenge: agents that work correctly but perform poorly. Slow response times and unbounded memory growth are the most common operational issues that surface after you resolve the initial debugging problems. They don’t trigger error alerts, but they erode user trust and increase costs over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;, you will learn how to identify performance bottlenecks across your agent’s execution path and diagnose memory issues in long-running sessions. You will also implement monitoring practices that catch degradation before users notice it. For additional information and best practices, review &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/insights.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Insights&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You need an AWS account with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore access, CloudWatch Transaction Search enabled, and a deployed agent. See Part 1 for full setup details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-3-performance-bottlenecks"&gt;Scenario 3: Performance bottlenecks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents experience performance bottlenecks when they work correctly but respond too slowly. You expect sub-second responses but experience multi-second delays. Agents complete tasks successfully, but the latency makes them impractical for interactive use cases. This scenario is particularly challenging because &lt;em&gt;slow&lt;/em&gt; is subjective. What’s acceptable for a batch processing agent is unacceptable for a customer service chatbot. You must establish performance budgets for your specific use case, then systematically identify which components violate those budgets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="symptoms-to-watch-for"&gt;Symptoms to watch for&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Performance degradation often manifests gradually. Agents might start with acceptable 2-second response times, but as you add features, integrate more tools, or accumulate more memory, latency creeps to 5 seconds, then 10, then becomes unusable. P95 response times exceed your thresholds, users abandon sessions, but error rates stay low. The agent works correctly but responds too slowly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/30/ML-20467-1.png" alt="CloudWatch session view showing three agent invocations with high 7.5-8.2 second span latency" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 1 — Session details showing multiple traces with consistently high latency. The three invocations took 7.5-8.2 seconds (average span latency), demonstrating a systemic performance bottleneck rather than occasional slowness. This pattern indicates the agent’s architecture needs optimization.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To find bottlenecks, start by identifying high-latency requests. Query CloudWatch for agent invocations that exceed your performance budget:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, RequestId, Latency
| filter Operation like /InvokeAgent/
| filter Latency &amp;gt; 3000
| sort Latency desc
| limit 50&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This query returns agent invocations that took longer than 3 seconds (adjust the threshold based on your requirements), sorted by latency. Pick a representative high-latency request and note its &lt;code&gt;RequestId&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, analyze the request timeline to understand where time is spent:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, Operation, Duration, SpanName
| filter RequestId = "&amp;lt;RequestId&amp;gt;"
| sort @timestamp asc&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The query shows you the sequence of operations within the request and how long each took. Look for operations that consume disproportionate time. Common culprits include memory retrieval operations, tool invocations, token generation, and sequential operations that could run in parallel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/30/ML-20467-2.png" alt="OpenTelemetry trace timeline with 17 spans across three sequential tool-execution cycles" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 2 — OpenTelemetry trace timeline showing 17 spans across three sequential execute_event_loop_cycle operations. The tools (customer_lookup, order_history) execute one after another rather than in parallel, with each cycle waiting for the previous one to complete. This sequential pattern compounds latency across each tool invocation.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check memory retrieval latency specifically:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, MemoryRetrievalLatency, MemoryNamespace
| filter RequestId = "&amp;lt;RequestId&amp;gt;"
| stats avg(MemoryRetrievalLatency), max(MemoryRetrievalLatency) by MemoryNamespace&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Memory retrieval should complete in under 200 milliseconds, representing the point where users begin perceiving noticeable delays in interactive applications. Higher latency suggests inefficient memory organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Examine tool invocation latency to identify slow integrations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, ToolName, ToolLatency
| filter RequestId = "&amp;lt;RequestId&amp;gt;"
| sort ToolLatency desc&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The query identifies which tools contribute most to overall latency. A single slow tool can bottleneck the entire agent workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="root-cause-analysis"&gt;Root cause analysis&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Performance bottlenecks typically stem from three root causes. &lt;em&gt;Slow tool execution&lt;/em&gt; occurs when external tools take seconds to respond because of poor optimization, overload, or network issues. Latencies compound with sequential calls, so a 2-second tool called three times becomes a 6-second bottleneck. &lt;em&gt;Excessive token generation&lt;/em&gt; is a factor because foundation models (FMs) produce tokens sequentially, meaning a 500-token response takes 5x longer than a 100-token one, impacting both latency and cost. Finally, &lt;em&gt;sequential processing&lt;/em&gt;, performing independent operations one at a time instead of in parallel, increases both cost and compute times.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-fix"&gt;The fix&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For slow tool execution, implement caching, connection pooling, and proper database indexing to reduce response times. Set timeout limits and consider faster tool alternatives. If a tool consistently lags, profile it independently. The issue might be network latency, cold starts, or resource contention rather than the tool’s logic itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For memory retrieval, replace single large namespaces with topic-specific partitions, such as preferences, history, and domain knowledge, to reduce search space. Summarize old conversations into compact entries rather than storing them verbatim, and set size limits per namespace, such as 100 preferences, 50 recent messages, or 500 domain facts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For token generation, optimize prompts to encourage brief, direct answers of 2–3 sentences unless more detail is requested. Add explicit length constraints and monitor token usage with alerts for unexpectedly long responses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For sequential processing, run independent tool calls in parallel. Sequential calls totaling 4.5s (2s + 1.5s + 1s) drop to only 2s when parallelized, often cutting latency by 50 percent or more with minimal effort. To verify your optimizations, re-run the latency query from the identifying bottlenecks section. Confirm that P95 response times now fall within your performance budget and that the trace timeline shows parallel execution where expected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scenario-4-memory-issues-in-long-running-sessions"&gt;Scenario 4: Memory issues in long-running sessions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents experience memory issues in long-running sessions when they maintain sessions where memory usage grows unbounded. Agents accumulate context, and eventually, agents hit token limits, lose important context, or exhaust available memory. Sessions fail unexpectedly, and you lose conversation state. This scenario is particularly problematic for agents that support extended workflows, such as customer service sessions, research assistants, or monitoring agents. Without proper memory management, these use cases become impractical.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="symptoms-to-watch-for-1"&gt;Symptoms to watch for&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When abnormally long agent sessions exist, token usage grows linearly with session duration. Memory retrieval latency increases over time as memory stores grow. Sessions terminate unexpectedly with out-of-memory errors or context window exceeded errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/30/ML-20467-3.png" alt="Session details showing 6 traces and 15.7K tokens, with token usage rising each invocation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 3 — Session details for the memorygrowth_Agent showing 6 traces, 15.7K total tokens consumed, and an average trace latency of 3,757 ms within a single session. Token usage grows with each successive invocation, demonstrating unbounded context accumulation. In production sessions spanning hours, this pattern leads to context window exhaustion and unexpected session failures.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To identify long-running sessions with high memory usage:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, SessionId, SessionDuration, MemorySize, TokenUsage
| filter SessionDuration &amp;gt; 3600
| sort MemorySize desc
| limit 20&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The query returns sessions lasting longer than one hour (3600 seconds), sorted by memory size. Pick a session with unusually high memory usage and note its &lt;code&gt;SessionId&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Examine memory extraction patterns to verify consolidation is occurring:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, MemoryExtractionStatus, MemoryExtractionLatency, MemoriesExtracted
| filter SessionId = "&amp;lt;SessionId&amp;gt;"
| sort @timestamp asc&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/30/ML-20467-4.png" alt="CloudWatch Logs Insights showing 209 memory log entries spiking around 18:20 without consolidation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 4 — CloudWatch Logs Insights showing 209 memory-related log entries concentrated around 18:20. The spike in memory operations indicates the agent storing information without consolidation. Each invocation adds new memory entries (through add_conversation_note and add_user_context tools) without pruning or summarizing old data, demonstrating the unbounded growth pattern.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Memory extraction should occur regularly throughout the session. If you see gaps where no extraction happens for extended periods, the agent isn’t consolidating memories properly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check for memory extraction failures:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, ErrorMessage, MemoryExtractionStatus
| filter SessionId = "&amp;lt;SessionId&amp;gt;"
| filter MemoryExtractionStatus = "Failed"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Failed memory extraction stops agents from consolidating context, causing unbounded growth. Common failure reasons include token limits exceeded during summarization, invalid memory formats that can’t be processed, network timeouts when writing to memory storage, and permission errors that block memory updates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Analyze memory namespace organization:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;fields @timestamp, MemoryNamespace, MemoryCount, MemorySize
| filter SessionId = "&amp;lt;SessionId&amp;gt;"
| stats sum(MemoryCount) as TotalMemories, sum(MemorySize) as TotalSize by MemoryNamespace
| sort TotalSize desc&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Memories are distributed across namespaces. Poor namespace organization can lead to inefficient memory retrieval and consolidation. If you see a single namespace containing thousands of memories, that’s a red flag indicating your agent needs &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/organizing-agents-memory-at-scale-namespace-design-patterns-in-agentcore-memory/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;better memory organization&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="root-cause-analysis-1"&gt;Root cause analysis&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Memory issues typically stem from misconfigured settings, filter on inbuilt datetime metadata of the record. For a deeper understanding of how AgentCore memory works, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-memory-building-context-aware-agents/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-fix-1"&gt;The fix&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To resolve memory issues, verify your memory strategies include a consolidation configuration so AgentCore memory merges and summarizes records over time rather than accumulating them indefinitely. Organize records using namespace templates in your strategy definitions to make sure retrieval stays scoped to relevant context. Set &lt;code&gt;eventExpiryDuration&lt;/code&gt; to control how long raw events persist (between 7–365 days). For implementation details, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-memory-building-context-aware-agents/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory implementation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Production best practices&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Having covered the four failure scenarios across both parts, we now turn to production best practices that stop these issues before they occur. The troubleshooting workflows we’ve covered help you respond when things go wrong, but a well-architected observability strategy helps you proactively catch issues. The integration of AgentCore with CloudWatch provides the foundation for this proactive approach, giving you real-time visibility into agent health and performance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Turn on comprehensive instrumentation for production agents, memory systems, and gateways. Configure CloudWatch logs, CloudWatch metrics, and OpenTelemetry traces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configure CloudWatch alarms for critical metrics. Set thresholds for error rates (5 percent), 95th percentile (P95) latency (3 seconds), and token usage per session. Don’t wait for users to report problems. Let CloudWatch alert you when metrics exceed acceptable thresholds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create operational dashboards. Build a primary dashboard showing total invocations (last 24 hours), error rate (current compared to baseline), P50/P95/P99 latency, token usage trends, and active sessions count. Create per-agent dashboards showing agent-specific invocation patterns, tool usage breakdown, memory consumption trends, error types distribution, and cost per session. Review these dashboards daily to spot trends before they become problems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Invest in observability infrastructure before you need it. Build monitoring into your development process from day one. Share dashboards with product managers and stakeholders so everyone understands agent performance. When someone diagnoses a tricky production issue, share the approach with the team to build institutional knowledge about common failure patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To monitor tool accuracy at scale, you can use Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluators.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Evaluators&lt;/a&gt; to continuously and automatically assess agent behavior. Instead of manually reviewing traces after failures occur, Evaluators inspect agent sessions in real time, scoring them against predefined quality criteria. Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/insights.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Insights (preview)&lt;/a&gt; builds off Evaluators and provides triage analysis. Insights can provide failure analysis, user intent extraction, and execution summary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After testing the optimization techniques in this post, clean up resources to avoid unnecessary charges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For CloudWatch resources, delete test CloudWatch dashboards created during debugging, remove CloudWatch alarms set up for testing purposes, and consider archiving or deleting old CloudWatch log groups if no longer needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For AgentCore resources, if you created test agents specifically for this tutorial, delete them through the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;. Remove any test memory namespaces created during memory optimization testing. Delete any temporary tool integrations used for performance testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For cost optimization, review your Amazon CloudWatch Logs retention settings and adjust based on your compliance requirements. Consider using CloudWatch Logs data protection to cut storage costs for older logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To delete CloudWatch log groups:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws logs delete-log-group --log-group-name /aws/bedrock-agentcore/your-agent-name&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To remove CloudWatch alarms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws cloudwatch delete-alarms --alarm-names your-alarm-name&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Performance bottlenecks and memory issues represent the most common operational challenges for production agents beyond the debugging scenarios covered in Part 1. With systematic diagnosis using CloudWatch traces and metrics, you can identify whether latency stems from slow tools, inefficient memory retrieval, excessive token generation, or sequential processing. For long-running sessions, monitoring memory growth patterns and implementing consolidation strategies keeps agents stable over extended interactions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ready to put these techniques into practice? Start by turning on AgentCore Observability for your production agents if you haven’t already. This one-time setup provides the foundation for everything we’ve covered. Set up CloudWatch alarms for critical metrics. Create troubleshooting runbooks for your specific agents and workflows, documenting the queries you run, the thresholds that indicate problems, and the fixes you implement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Practice debugging in non-production environments. Deliberately introduce failures and practice diagnosing them using the workflows we’ve covered. Build muscle memory for troubleshooting before you need it in production. Share this knowledge with your team to help confirm everyone who operates your agents understands these debugging techniques and knows how to use the observability features of AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Production agents often fail for preventable reasons. With the right observability tools, systematic troubleshooting approaches, and a culture that treats every issue as a chance to improve, you can catch problems early, resolve them quickly, and build agents that earn lasting trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="learn-more"&gt;Learn more&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information about AgentCore and observability features, visit the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-configure.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with AgentCore, visit the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;. For additional CloudWatch monitoring best practices, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CloudWatch User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Deploying Kimi K3 on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod and Amazon EKS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploying-kimi-k3-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-and-amazon-eks/</link>
					
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vivek Gangasani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 30 Jul 2026 17:22:32 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Lakehouse]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">31e07eff62e76a2062e28f358c55660a2c45d6f3</guid>

					<description>This post walks through deploying Kimi K3 on AWS using two approaches: Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, and&amp;nbsp; Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) cluster.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Open weight models have become powerful enough to handle complex tasks such as multi-step agentic workflows, advanced reasoning, and long-horizon coding. However, as these models grow in capability, they also grow in size and hosting multi-trillion parameter architectures requires purpose-built infrastructure, high-end GPU compute, and optimized serving frameworks. On July 27, 2026, Moonshot AI released Kimi K3, a 2.8 trillion parameter Mixture of Experts (MoE) model that represents the &lt;a href="https://platform.kimi.ai/docs/guide/kimi-k3-quickstart" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;first open-weight system&lt;/a&gt; to reach the 3 trillion parameter class. Kimi K3 delivers &lt;a href="https://artificialanalysis.ai/models/kimi-k2" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;frontier-level intelligence&lt;/a&gt; while making its weights publicly available, so that organizations can self-host one of the most capable models in existence on their own infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walks through deploying Kimi K3 on AWS using two approaches: Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, and Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;About Kimi K3&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kimi K3 is built on a differentiated architecture featuring Kimi Delta Attention (KDA), Gated Multi Head Latent Attention (MLA), and a Stable LatentMoE framework. The model distributes its 2.8 trillion parameters across 896 specialist experts, activating only 16 per token. This means approximately 104 billion parameters are active during any single forward pass, yielding a &lt;a href="https://platform.kimi.ai/docs/guide/kimi-k3-quickstart" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;2.5x improvement&lt;/a&gt; in scaling efficiency over its predecessor, Kimi K2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table class="styled-table" border="1px" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Total Parameters&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;2.8 Trillion&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Active Parameters per Token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;104 Billion&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Architecture&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Mixture of Experts (MoE)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Expert Count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;896 (16 activated per token)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Context Window&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;1 Million Tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Modality&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Native Multimodal (Text + Vision)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;Release Date&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td style="padding: 10px;border: 1px solid #dddddd"&gt;July 27, 2026&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kimi K3 excels at long-horizon coding tasks, agentic workflows, and complex reasoning. It supports native tool calling, structured output, and an always-on thinking mode for multi-step problem solving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model availability and format&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The open weights for Kimi K3 are available on Hugging Face under the model identifier moonshotai/Kimi-K3. The weights are distributed in &lt;strong&gt;MXFP4&lt;/strong&gt; (Microscaling Floating Point 4-bit) format, which provides an effective balance between model quality and memory efficiency for large-scale inference deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Given the model’s architecture and size, serving Kimi K3 requires a vLLM day-0 inference container for Kimi K3. At the time of writing, vllm commits for Kimi K3 are in &lt;code&gt;vllm/vllm-openai:kimi-k3&lt;/code&gt;. We expect these to be merged to the main vllm container in the upcoming releases. vLLM provides native support for MoE architectures, tensor parallelism, and the MXFP4 quantization format, making it the recommended serving engine for this model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Infrastructure requirements&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying a model of this scale requires substantial GPU compute. Kimi K3 requires a &lt;strong&gt;p6-b300&lt;/strong&gt; instance (&lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;), which provides 8 NVIDIA B300 Blackwell Ultra GPUs with high-bandwidth interconnects necessary for efficient tensor-parallel inference across the full expert pool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS offers two primary mechanisms to procure this capacity:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flexible Training Plans (for SageMaker HyperPod): &lt;/strong&gt;Provide committed capacity reservations that can be allocated to your HyperPod cluster, so that GPU resources are available for sustained inference workloads.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capacity Blocks:&lt;/strong&gt; Allow you to reserve EC2 GPU instances for a defined period, providing guaranteed access to p6-b300 capacity without long-term commitments. &lt;strong&gt;Amazon EKS &lt;/strong&gt;workloads consume these reservations by targeting the reserved capacity.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option 1: Deploying on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod with the Inference Operator provides the simplest path to deploying Kimi K3. The Inference Operator is installed automatically as part of cluster creation, and it abstracts away the complexity of container orchestration, model loading, and endpoint management.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying the model, complete the following two prerequisite steps to set up your infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Create a SageMaker HyperPod cluster with EKS orchestration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying the model, you must provision a HyperPod cluster. Navigate to the Amazon SageMaker AI console and follow the cluster creation workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the SageMaker AI console and select &lt;strong&gt;HyperPod Clusters &amp;gt; Cluster Management &amp;gt; Create HyperPod cluster&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Orchestrated by Amazon EKS&lt;/strong&gt; from the list.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;Quick setup&lt;/strong&gt; to provision a cluster with default networking, storage, and IAM resources, or choose &lt;strong&gt;Custom setup&lt;/strong&gt; to integrate with existing VPC, subnets, and security groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Orchestration&lt;/strong&gt;, either create a new EKS cluster or attach an existing one. Verify that the &lt;strong&gt;Use default Helm charts and add-ons&lt;/strong&gt; option is selected so that the Inference Operator and other required operators are installed automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Instance groups&lt;/strong&gt;, add a worker group configured with the &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance type.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review the configuration and choose &lt;strong&gt;Submit&lt;/strong&gt; to begin provisioning.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the complete walkthrough, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-operate-console-ui-create-cluster.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Creating a SageMaker HyperPod cluster with Amazon EKS orchestration&lt;/a&gt; documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Procure capacity using a Flexible Training Plan&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance type requires reserved capacity. A Flexible Training Plan provides a committed capacity reservation for your Blackwell GPU nodes, guaranteeing that p6-b300 instances are available to your cluster without contention from the general on-demand pool. Go to SageMaker Console, select a FTP block based on your timeline and instance count. To create or attach a training plan:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the instance group configuration, choose &lt;strong&gt;Training plan&lt;/strong&gt; as the capacity source.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select an existing plan that covers &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; capacity, or create a new reservation specifying the instance count and duration that you want.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the &lt;strong&gt;Target Availability Zone&lt;/strong&gt; to match the zone where your training plan capacity is allocated.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once the cluster reaches an Active state with healthy p6-b300 nodes, you are ready to deploy the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deploying the model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy Kimi K3 on HyperPod, apply the following &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; manifest to your cluster:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;apiVersion: inference.sagemaker.aws.amazon.com/v1
kind: InferenceEndpointConfig
metadata:
  name: kimik3
spec:
  modelName: Kimi-K3
  instanceType: ml.p6-b300.48xlarge
  invocationEndpoint: v1/chat/completions
  replicas: 1
  modelSourceConfig:
    huggingFaceModel:
      modelId: moonshotai/Kimi-K3
    modelSourceType: huggingface
  worker:
    image: vllm/vllm-openai:kimi-k3
    modelInvocationPort:
      containerPort: 8000
      name: http
    modelVolumeMount:
      mountPath: /opt/ml/model
      name: model-weights
    resources:
      limits:
        nvidia.com/gpu: 8
      requests:
        nvidia.com/gpu: 8
    args:
      - "--model"
      - "moonshotai/Kimi-K3"
      - "--trust-remote-code"
      - "--load-format"
      - "fastsafetensors"
      - "--enable-prefix-caching"
      - "--enable-auto-tool-choice"
      - "--tool-call-parser"
      - "kimi_k3"
      - "--reasoning-parser"
      - "kimi_k3"
      - "--served-model-name"
      - "Kimi-K3"
      - "--moe-backend"
      - "auto"
      - "--tensor-parallel-size"
      - "8"
      - "--no-enable-flashinfer-autotune"
    environmentVariables:
      - name: "VLLM_ENABLE_K3_LATENT_MOE_TAIL_FUSION"
        value: "1"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This yaml is also provided in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/SageMakerHyperpod/kimi-k3/kimi-k3.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. Apply this configuration with:&lt;code&gt;kubectl apply -f kimi-k3.yaml&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Inference Operator handles model download from Hugging Face, container scheduling, health checks, and endpoint readiness. Once the endpoint transitions to a ready state, it exposes an OpenAI compatible API at the configured invocation path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option 2: Deploying on Amazon EKS&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For teams that prefer to manage their own Kubernetes infrastructure, you can deploy Kimi K3 on a standalone Amazon EKS cluster and procure GPU capacity through &lt;strong&gt;EC2 Capacity Blocks&lt;/strong&gt;, which allow you to reserve p6-b300 instances for a defined duration without long-term commitments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key deployment steps&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/ai-on-eks-charts/blob/main/charts/inference-charts/values-kimi-k3-vllm-b300.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;AI on EKS&lt;/a&gt; project provides an inference-ready cluster recipe that automates the end-to-end provisioning. At a high level, the deployment involves the following stages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Provision the EKS cluster&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the provided Terraform modules to create a GPU-optimized EKS cluster. This includes VPC networking, managed node groups, and the necessary IAM roles and policies for GPU workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Reserve GPU capacity with Capacity Blocks&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a Capacity Block reservation for &lt;code&gt;p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances in your target Availability Zone. Capacity Blocks guarantee that the requested GPU nodes will be available for the reserved time window. Once the reservation becomes active, the instances join your EKS cluster as worker nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Install GPU drivers and device plugin&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The recipe installs the NVIDIA device plugin and GPU drivers on the node group, so that Kubernetes can discover and schedule against the available GPUs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;4. Deploy the vLLM inference server&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Helm chart or Kubernetes manifest deploys the vLLM container with Kimi K3 specific arguments, including tensor-parallel size of 8, the MXFP4 load format, MoE backend configuration. The model identifier points to the Hugging Face repository, or as an alternative, you can sync the model weights to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) for faster model loading. The serving arguments mirror those shown in the HyperPod configuration above.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;5. Expose the inference endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Kubernetes Service (type LoadBalancer or via an Ingress controller) exposes the vLLM server on port 8000, providing the OpenAI compatible &lt;code&gt;/v1/chat/completions&lt;/code&gt; endpoint to your applications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;6. Validate&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Confirm the deployment by sending a test request to the endpoint and verifying a successful model response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the full deployment walkthrough, including Terraform modules, Helm values, and step-by-step instructions, refer to the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/ai-on-eks/tree/main/infra/solutions/inference-ready-cluster/recipes/kimi-k3" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;AI on EKS Kimi K3 recipe&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Invoking the endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once deployed, the Kimi K3 endpoint exposes an OpenAI compatible chat completions API. You can invoke it using the OpenAI Python SDK or a simple curl command.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Using the OpenAI Python SDK&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-powershell"&gt;from openai import OpenAI
client = OpenAI(
    base_url="http://&amp;lt;ENDPOINT_URL&amp;gt;:8000/v1",
    api_key="not-needed"
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-css"&gt;response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="Kimi-K3",
    messages=[
        {"role": "system", "content": "You are a helpful assistant."},
        {"role": "user", "content": "Explain the benefits of mixture of experts architectures."}
    ],
    temperature=0.7,
    max_tokens=1024
)
print(response.choices[0].message.content)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Using curl&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-css"&gt;curl -X POST http://&amp;lt;ENDPOINT_URL&amp;gt;:8000/v1/chat/completions \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "model": "Kimi-K3",
    "messages": [
      {"role": "system", "content": "You are a helpful assistant."},
      {"role": "user", "content": "Explain the benefits of mixture of experts architectures."}
    ],
    "temperature": 0.7,
    "max_tokens": 1024
  }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;ENDPOINT_URL&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with the service endpoint exposed by your HyperPod Inference Operator or EKS ingress configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Clean up&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created during this walkthrough when you no longer need them.For SageMaker HyperPod deployments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; by running &lt;code&gt;kubectl delete -f kimi-k3.yaml&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the SageMaker AI console, navigate to HyperPod Clusters, select your cluster, and choose Delete.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Release or cancel your Flexible Training Plan reservation if it is no longer needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Amazon EKS deployments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the vLLM deployment and associated Kubernetes services.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Terminate the GPU node group or delete the EKS cluster using Terraform (&lt;code&gt;terraform destroy&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Release your Capacity Block reservation if it has not yet expired.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For pricing details on p6-b300 instances and Capacity Block reservations, refer to the Amazon EC2 pricing page.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conclusion&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kimi K3 represents a new frontier in open-weight model capabilities, and AWS provides the infrastructure and managed services to deploy it at scale. Whether you choose the streamlined HyperPod Inference Operator path or the flexibility of a self-managed EKS cluster, the combination of p6-b300 GPU instances, vLLM serving, and MXFP4 quantized weights delivers a deployment with built-in health checks, auto-recovery, and endpoint readiness verification for the world’s largest open model.To get started, here are some links&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/SageMakerHyperpod/kimi-k3/kimi-k3.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;SageMaker HyperPod Kimi K3 Example&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/ai-on-eks/tree/main/infra/solutions/inference-ready-cluster/recipes/kimi-k3" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;AI on EKS Kimi K3 Recipe&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-operate-console-ui-create-cluster.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Creating a SageMaker HyperPod Cluster (AWS Documentation)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/moonshotai/Kimi-K3" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Kimi K3 on Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;vLLM Documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt;
