			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing explicit prompt caching for OpenAI GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-explicit-prompt-caching-for-openai-gpt-5-6-models-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Melanie Li]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 30 Jul 2026 16:02:32 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9078a506990d3c974fff12a1937a20b081010c09</guid>

					<description>OpenAI GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna are now generally available on Amazon Bedrock, along with explicit prompt caching that gives you precise control over which parts of your prompt are cached and reused. Learn how to get started, set up explicit caching, and migrate existing GPT workloads to reduce inference cost.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;This post is co-written with Chris Dickens from OpenAI.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;OpenAI GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna are &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/openai-gpt-5-6-sol-terra-and-luna-are-now-generally-available-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;now generally available on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. With GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock, you get the newest generation of OpenAI frontier models with pay-per-token pricing, AWS security and governance controls, and usage that counts toward your existing AWS commitments. The family covers three capability tiers: GPT-5.6 Sol for the most complex reasoning and agentic coding work, GPT-5.6 Terra for balanced everyday production workloads, and GPT-5.6 Luna for fast, high-volume tasks such as classification and summarization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Alongside the new models, GPT-5.6 introduces &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;explicit prompt caching&lt;/a&gt; on Amazon Bedrock, a new capability that gives you precise control over which portions of your prompt are cached and reused across requests. Cached input is billed at a 90 percent discount (see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;) and stays available for reuse for 30 minutes. You get the most value from this in agentic workflows, where system instructions, tool definitions, and reference documents repeat across many calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we introduce explicit prompt caching for GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock, a new capability that gives you control over which parts of your prompt are cached and reused across requests. We show how it differs from implicit caching and when to use each mode, how to set it up and verify that it is working, and how it performs in an agentic workflow. We also cover how to migrate workloads from earlier GPT models onto GPT-5.6, whether they run on Amazon Bedrock today or on another platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started-with-gpt-5.6-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Get started with GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 models are served through the OpenAI-compatible Responses API on the Amazon Bedrock &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="set-up-the-client"&gt;Set up the client&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The recommended way to authenticate is with short-term bearer tokens generated from your AWS credentials. Install the token generator alongside the OpenAI SDK:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;pip install openai aws-bedrock-token-generator&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then create a client. The token generator uses the standard AWS credential chain (IAM roles, environment variables, or your CLI profile), so your code stays free of long-lived secrets:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from openai import OpenAI
from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token

REGION = "us-east-2"

client = OpenAI(
    base_url=f"https://bedrock-mantle.{REGION}.api.aws/openai/v1",
    api_key=provide_token(region=REGION),  # short-term token from your AWS credentials
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="make-your-first-request"&gt;Make your first request&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 models use the Responses API. A minimal request looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    instructions="You are a concise technical assistant.",
    input="What is Amazon Bedrock?",
    max_output_tokens=500,
)
print(response.output_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The three model IDs are &lt;code&gt;openai.gpt-5.6-sol&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;openai.gpt-5.6-terra&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;openai.gpt-5.6-luna&lt;/code&gt;. GPT-5.6 Sol is available in US East (N. Virginia) and US East (Ohio). Terra and Luna are additionally available in US West (Oregon). For model availability by Region, refer to Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="control-reasoning-effort"&gt;Control reasoning effort&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 supports reasoning effort levels &lt;code&gt;none&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;, with &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; as the default. Higher levels allocate more reasoning to harder problems, and &lt;code&gt;none&lt;/code&gt; gives you the lowest-latency path for straightforward tasks:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-luna",
    input="Classify this ticket as bug, feature request, or question: 'App crashes on login.'",
    reasoning={"effort": "none"},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A practical guideline when upgrading from GPT-5.5 or GPT-5.4: start with the effort level you use today, then test one level lower. GPT-5.6 is more token efficient, and many workloads maintain quality at a lower effort setting. Sampling parameters (&lt;code&gt;temperature&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;top_p&lt;/code&gt;) are supported when &lt;code&gt;reasoning.effort&lt;/code&gt; is set to &lt;code&gt;none&lt;/code&gt;. At other effort levels, the model’s reasoning process controls output variance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="stream-responses"&gt;Stream responses&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Streaming works through the SDK’s standard interface and delivers output as typed events:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;with client.responses.stream(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    input="Write a haiku about distributed systems.",
) as stream:
    for event in stream:
        if event.type == "response.output_text.delta":
            print(event.delta, end="", flush=True)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="call-tools-and-get-structured-output"&gt;Call tools and get structured output&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Function calling and strict JSON schema output both work as they do with OpenAI’s native API. Tool definitions use the Responses API’s flat format:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    input="What's the weather in Seattle?",
    tools=[{
        "type": "function",
        "name": "get_weather",
        "description": "Get current weather for a city",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {"city": {"type": "string"}},
            "required": ["city"],
        },
    }],
)
for item in response.output:
    if item.type == "function_call":
        print(item.name, item.arguments)  # get_weather {"city":"Seattle"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For structured output, pass a strict JSON schema and the model returns valid JSON that conforms to it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-luna",
    input="Extract: 'Order #1234 shipped to Berlin on May 2.'",
    text={
        "format": {
            "type": "json_schema",
            "name": "order",
            "strict": True,
            "schema": {
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "order_id": {"type": "string"},
                    "destination": {"type": "string"},
                },
                "required": ["order_id", "destination"],
                "additionalProperties": False,
            },
        }
    },
)
print(response.output_text)  # {"destination":"Berlin","order_id":"1234"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-prompt-caching-for-gpt-5.6-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Use prompt caching for GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 models support prompt caching through the Responses API, with two modes: implicit, where Amazon Bedrock places cache breakpoints for you automatically, and explicit, where you mark the cache boundary yourself for precise control. This section covers both, along with how to verify caching behavior and choose the right mode for your workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prompt caching lets Amazon Bedrock skip recomputing portions of your prompt that repeat across requests. On GPT-5.6, cache reads are billed at a 90 percent discount compared to uncached input tokens, and cache writes are billed at 1.25 times the uncached input rate, so the feature is designed for write-once, read-many patterns. Because a read saves far more than a write adds, a workload lowers its net input cost once cache reads make up roughly 20 percent of the tokens that flow through the cache. Implicit moves you toward that with no work on your part, because Amazon Bedrock places the breakpoint and tunes it for you. In explicit mode, marking the stable prefix yourself typically pushes the hit rate higher still, because later requests read the prefix back while it stays cached. For current rates, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="start-with-implicit-prompt-caching"&gt;Start with implicit prompt caching&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On GPT-5.6, prompt caching is on by default in implicit mode. When you send a request without any caching parameters, Amazon Bedrock automatically places a cache breakpoint and looks for a reusable prefix. A stable prefix of at least 1,024 tokens can be cached and reused with no change to your code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Implicit caching: no caching parameters needed
response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    instructions=SYSTEM_INSTRUCTIONS,  # stable prefix, &amp;gt;= 1,024 tokens
    input=user_question,
)
details = response.usage.input_tokens_details
print(f"cached: {details.cached_tokens}, written: {details.cache_write_tokens}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Implicit caching means your existing code starts earning cache reads from day one, with no analysis of your prompt structure required. Because Bedrock chooses the breakpoint for you, the first request writes the prefix to the cache and later requests read it back. For workloads where the content after your stable prefix changes on every request, such as chat assistants and agentic tool loops, you get more predictable results by marking the prefix yourself with an explicit breakpoint. The following section describes this approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="set-up-explicit-prompt-caching"&gt;Set up explicit prompt caching&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Explicit mode gives you direct control over the cache boundary. You mark the end of your reusable prefix, and cached prefixes stay available for reuse for at least 30 minutes, long enough to cover the burst of calls a single agent run generates. Three request parameters control caching on GPT-5.6:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_breakpoint&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: placed on a content block, marks the exact end of a reusable prompt prefix. Everything up to and including the marked block is cached, and everything after it can change freely while the cached prefix stays valid. Each cached prefix must contain at least 1,024 tokens, and you can set up to 4 breakpoints per request on &lt;code&gt;input_text&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;input_image&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;input_file&lt;/code&gt; blocks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: a stable ID that routes requests to the same cache. Set it consistently across all requests in a session or application so repeat requests find the cached prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_options&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: controls the caching mode (implicit or explicit, covered in the next section) and the TTL.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here is a complete example: a support assistant with a long system instruction that is identical on every call, followed by a user question that changes every time. The breakpoint goes at the end of the static content:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    prompt_cache_key="support-app:kb-v1",  # same key across all requests
    input=[
        {
            "type": "message",
            "role": "developer",
            "content": [{
                "type": "input_text",
                "text": SYSTEM_INSTRUCTIONS,  # long, static: guidelines, KB excerpts (&amp;gt;= 1,024 tokens)
                "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"},
            }],
        },
        {
            "type": "message",
            "role": "user",
            "content": [{
                "type": "input_text",
                "text": user_question,  # changes on every request
            }],
        },
    ],
    extra_body={"prompt_cache_options": {"mode": "explicit"}},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="verify-caching-behavior"&gt;Verify caching behavior&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every response reports exactly what the cache did, in the &lt;code&gt;usage.input_tokens_details&lt;/code&gt; object. This is the place to verify your setup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: input tokens read from cache, billed at the 90 percent discount.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: input tokens written to cache, billed at 1.25 times the uncached rate.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;details = response.usage.input_tokens_details
print(f"cached: {details.cached_tokens}, written: {details.cache_write_tokens}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Note: Tokens served from the cache within the 30-minute window are excluded from charges on subsequent calls. The following explanation covers this in detail.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;input_tokens = cached_tokens + cache_write_tokens + non-cached remainder&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; is the full input count for the request. &lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/code&gt; report how much of that input came from the cache, and the rest is the new content you send each time, such as the user question after your breakpoint. Because the cached and written counts are part of &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; rather than added on top of it, a token served from the cache is counted once and billed once at the cache-read rate. You are never charged twice for the same token. In the following example, the requests reuse the same 3,626-token system prefix under one &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt;. The user question changes each time, so the non-cached remainder of &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; changes with it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;input_tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New (non-cached) input&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;First (cold)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,682&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,626&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;56&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Second&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,671&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,626&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;45&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Third&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,662&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,626&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;36&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first request writes the 3,626-token prefix to the cache. From the second request onward, that prefix is read back at the cache-read discount while only the new user input (45 and 36 tokens here) is processed at the standard rate. &lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt; stays high, &lt;code&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/code&gt; drops to zero, and &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; moves only by the size of the changing user question.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These two fields arrive with every response. The most direct verification workflow is to log them from your application alongside the request ID and prompt_cache_key. Then confirm the write-once, read-many pattern in your logs before production traffic depends on it. For aggregate monitoring, the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-mantle&lt;/code&gt; endpoint publishes inference and token metrics to Amazon CloudWatch under the &lt;code&gt;AWS/BedrockMantle&lt;/code&gt; namespace (see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/monitoring-mantle-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Monitor bedrock-mantle inference using CloudWatch metrics&lt;/a&gt;). This tracks overall input and output token volume by account, project, and model. The per-request cache breakdown comes from the usage object in each response, which makes application-level logging of these two fields the foundation of your caching observability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-caching-in-an-agentic-loop"&gt;Use caching in an agentic loop&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agentic workloads are where explicit caching shines. In a tool-calling loop, the system prompt and tool definitions repeat on every turn while the conversation grows at the end, a perfect fit for a breakpoint after the static content. This example builds an incident-response assistant that checks service health and recent deployments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

SYSTEM_PROMPT = "..."  # long runbook and instructions (&amp;gt;= 1,024 tokens)
MAX_TURNS = 10
TOOLS = [
    {"type": "function", "name": "get_service_health",
     "description": "Get health status for a microservice",
     "parameters": {"type": "object",
                    "properties": {"service": {"type": "string"}},
                    "required": ["service"]}},
    {"type": "function", "name": "get_recent_deploys",
     "description": "List deployments in the last N hours",
     "parameters": {"type": "object",
                    "properties": {"hours": {"type": "integer"}},
                    "required": ["hours"]}},
]
conversation = [
    {"type": "message", "role": "developer",
     "content": [{"type": "input_text",
                  "text": SYSTEM_PROMPT,
                  "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"}}]},
    {"type": "message", "role": "user",
     "content": [{"type": "input_text",
                  "text": "Is checkout-service healthy? If not, was there a recent deploy?"}]},
]
for turn in range(MAX_TURNS):
    response = client.responses.create(
        model="openai.gpt-5.6-sol",
        prompt_cache_key="incident-agent:session-42",
        input=conversation,
        tools=TOOLS,
        extra_body={"prompt_cache_options": {"mode": "explicit", "ttl": "30m"}},
    )
    tool_calls = [o for o in response.output if o.type == "function_call"]
    if not tool_calls:
        print(response.output_text)
        break
    for call in tool_calls:
        # execute_tool is your implementation: call the real system
        # and return a JSON-serializable result
        result = execute_tool(call.name, json.loads(call.arguments))
        conversation.append({"type": "function_call", "call_id": call.call_id,
                             "name": call.name, "arguments": call.arguments})
        conversation.append({"type": "function_call_output", "call_id": call.call_id,
                             "output": json.dumps(result)})&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model drives this loop: it decides which tools to call, and the loop ends when the model returns a final response instead of another tool call. This tool loop is the minimal form of an agent, the same loop that agent frameworks manage for you. The first turn writes the system prompt prefix to the cache. Every later turn in the run reads that prefix from the cache while the model processes the newly appended tool calls and results, and the 30-minute TTL comfortably covers multi-minute agent sessions. Logging the two usage fields per turn shows the pattern clearly: a single cache write on the opening turn, followed by cache reads on each turn after it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choose-between-implicit-and-explicit-caching"&gt;Choose between implicit and explicit caching&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 supports two caching modes, controlled by &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_options.mode&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Implicit (the default).&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock automatically places a cache breakpoint on the latest message and attempts to maximize your cache hit rate. Any explicit breakpoints you add are honored as well. This mode delivers caching benefits with your existing request format automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Explicit.&lt;/strong&gt; Setting “prompt_cache_options”: {“mode”: “explicit”} gives your breakpoints full control: only the breakpoints you place are used for cache reads and writes.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Explicit mode puts the cache boundary under your control, and that control matters most when your prompts combine a stable prefix with content that changes on every request. In implicit mode, Bedrock places the breakpoint for you, and the number of cache writes before reads begin can vary from request to request. With one explicit breakpoint placed at the end of the static content, the behavior is deterministic: the first request writes the prefix once, and every following request reads it from the cache, because the changing content sits safely after the breakpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your workload&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prompts repeat exactly (repeated document analysis, batch scoring with a fixed prompt)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Implicit (default) works with no request changes. Explicit gives the same result with deterministic control&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Stable prefix plus changing suffix (chat assistants, RAG, agentic tool loops)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Explicit, with one breakpoint after the static content&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multiple sections that change at different frequencies (tools, system prompt, long documents, conversation)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Explicit, with up to 4 breakpoints for granular control&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you do not want prompt caching on GPT-5.6+, you can use explicit cache mode without providing explicit breakpoints. This opts you out of prompt cache billing behavior (and does not incur cache-write charges), but parts of the prompts may still be cached on Bedrock systems to give you lower latency. Whichever mode you choose, keep monitoring the two usage fields in production. A healthy caching pattern shows meaningful cache reads following each write. If you see high cache writes with few reads in implicit mode, consider switching to explicit mode by adding an explicit breakpoint at the end of your static content so the cached prefix is deterministic. In explicit mode, move your breakpoint earlier so it covers only content that stays constant between calls. Confirm that &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt; is set consistently across the requests that should share a cache.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="migrate-to-gpt-5.6-from-earlier-gpt-models"&gt;Migrate to GPT-5.6 from earlier GPT models&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6’s combination of stronger performance, better token efficiency, and controllable caching makes it a compelling upgrade target. The migration effort depends on where your workload runs today. We cover the three common starting points, from smallest change to largest.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="starting-point-1-gpt-5.5-or-gpt-5.4-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Starting point 1: GPT-5.5 or GPT-5.4 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already run OpenAI models on Amazon Bedrock, the upgrade is a model ID change. The endpoint, authentication, and Responses API request shape stay identical:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",  # was: openai.gpt-5.5
    instructions=SYSTEM_PROMPT,
    input=user_input,
    max_output_tokens=1000,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two things to fold into the upgrade:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Adopt explicit cache breakpoints.&lt;/strong&gt; On GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.4, prompt caching is automatic: the system caches eligible prefixes of 1,024 tokens or longer without additional parameters, and cache writes are free of charge. GPT-5.6 moves to the controllable model described above, including the 1.25 times cache-write rate, which applies to implicit and explicit writes alike. Plan the two changes together: when you switch the model ID, place an explicit breakpoint after your static content at the same time. That single addition establishes the write-once, read-many pattern for prompts whose ending changes on each request. It extends your cache lifetime as well, because GPT-5.6 cached prefixes remain available for at least 30 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Revisit your reasoning effort.&lt;/strong&gt; GPT-5.6 adds the xhigh effort level and delivers more capability per token across the range. Start with your current GPT-5.5 effort level and test one level lower. Many workloads keep their quality bar at lower cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="starting-point-2-responses-api-on-another-platform"&gt;Starting point 2: Responses API on another platform&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your application already uses the OpenAI Responses API on another platform, migrating to Amazon Bedrock requires three changes: the base URL, the authentication, and the model ID. Your request and response handling code stays the same:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from openai import OpenAI
from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token

REGION = "us-east-2"

client = OpenAI(
    base_url=f"https://bedrock-mantle.{REGION}.api.aws/openai/v1",  # 1. endpoint
    api_key=provide_token(region=REGION),  # 2. auth
)

response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",  # 3. model ID
    instructions=SYSTEM_PROMPT,
    input=user_input,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The authentication change is also a security upgrade: aws-bedrock-token-generator derives short-term tokens from your AWS credentials, so your application uses IAM roles end to end. You keep the same identity, policy, and audit model (AWS CloudTrail) as the rest of your AWS workloads. For production, wrap token generation in a refresh routine, since tokens are short-lived by design. Because this migration typically also moves you up a model generation, fold in the two changes from starting point 1: place an explicit cache breakpoint after your static content, and test one reasoning effort level below your current setting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="starting-point-3-chat-completions-api-on-another-platform"&gt;Starting point 3: Chat Completions API on another platform&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock is served through the Responses API, so workloads written against Chat Completions port their request and response handling as part of the migration. The mapping is mechanical:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chat Completions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Responses API&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;messages=[{role, content}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;instructions="..."&lt;/code&gt; (system) plus &lt;code&gt;input="..."&lt;/code&gt; or message list&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;max_completion_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;max_output_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;response.choices[0].message.content&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;response.output_text&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;tools=[{"type": "function", "function": {...}}]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;tools=[{"type": "function", "name": ..., ...}]&lt;/code&gt; (flat)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;choices[0].message.tool_calls[i].id&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;output[i].call_id&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{"role": "tool", "tool_call_id": ...}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{"type": "function_call_output", "call_id": ...}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;response.usage.prompt_tokens&lt;/code&gt; / &lt;code&gt;completion_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;response.usage.input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; / &lt;code&gt;output_tokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;stream=True&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;delta.content&lt;/code&gt; chunks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;stream=True&lt;/code&gt; with typed events (&lt;code&gt;response.output_text.delta&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before and after, side by side:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Before: Chat Completions on another platform
response = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="gpt-5.5",
    messages=[
        {"role": "system", "content": "You are a concise assistant."},
        {"role": "user", "content": "What is Amazon Bedrock?"},
    ],
    max_completion_tokens=200,
)
print(response.choices[0].message.content)

# After: Responses API on Amazon Bedrock
response = client.responses.create(
    model="openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    instructions="You are a concise assistant.",
    input="What is Amazon Bedrock?",
    max_output_tokens=200,
)
print(response.output_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Responses API also brings additional capabilities, including server-managed conversation state, typed streaming events, and the explicit prompt caching covered in this post, so the port is an investment that pays beyond the migration itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validate-and-roll-out"&gt;Validate and roll out&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whatever your starting point, treat the migration as a structured engineering project:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inventory first.&lt;/strong&gt; Catalog the models, APIs (Responses or Chat Completions), tools, streaming usage, and state management your application relies on. This determines which starting point above applies and scopes the work.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build a golden prompt set.&lt;/strong&gt; Collect representative prompts and expected behaviors from production traffic, and replay them against GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock side by side with your current setup. Evaluate success rate, output quality, token consumption, and latency on the tasks that matter to your application. Amazon Bedrock evaluation tooling, or your existing evaluation harness both work.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Establish your caching baseline.&lt;/strong&gt; As part of the same replay, log cached_tokens and cache_write_tokens and confirm your breakpoint placement produces the write-once, read-many pattern before production traffic depends on it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ramp gradually.&lt;/strong&gt; Route a small percentage of traffic to the new model, compare KPIs against your baseline, and expand as confidence grows, keeping the old configuration available for instant rollback until the migration is complete.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna bring OpenAI’s newest frontier models to Amazon Bedrock. With explicit prompt caching you have a precision tool for the workloads that dominate modern large language model (LLM) spend: agents and AI workloads that resend the same instructions, tools, and references on every call. You need one breakpoint after your static content, a consistent cache key, and two usage fields to monitor. With those in place, you can convert that repeated context from full-price input into discounted cache reads. The same OpenAI SDK compatibility that makes caching straightforward to adopt also makes the migration itself a small, well-defined change from wherever your workload runs today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/openai-gpt-5-6-sol-terra-and-luna-are-now-generally-available-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GPT-5.6 launch announcement&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching documentation&lt;/a&gt; for full details on supported parameters and models. For pricing details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;. To monitor your workloads in production, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/monitoring-mantle-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Monitor bedrock-mantle inference using CloudWatch metrics&lt;/a&gt;. Try GPT-5.6 with your own prompts in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt;, and share your feedback on &lt;a href="https://repost.aws/tags/TAQeKlaPaNRQ2tWB6P7KrMag/amazon-bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS re:Post&lt;/a&gt; for Amazon Bedrock or through your usual AWS Support contacts.&lt;/p&gt;

		<guid isPermaLink="false">031afb634b787c3d2c724961d61c58a27d1fbc12</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization optimizes your prompts for up to 5 models at once and compares original versus optimized performance across quality, latency, and cost. Migrate to a new model or improve your current one in minutes instead of weeks.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;h2 id="the-problem-prompt-engineering-at-scale-is-still-painful"&gt;The problem: Prompt engineering at scale is still painful&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Migrating prompts to new models on Amazon Bedrock, or optimizing them for your current model, is still one of the most manual parts of building a generative AI application. Say you have built a deployed generative AI application. It works. Your prompts are tuned, your outputs are consistent, and your users are happy. Then a new model becomes available on Amazon Bedrock that is faster, cheaper, and more capable, and you must decide whether to migrate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The decision should be straightforward, but it rarely is.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customers spend days to weeks optimizing prompts and re-evaluating responses when they migrate to a new model. The same effort applies when they try to improve performance on their current model. The cycle looks the same every time. Rewrite the prompt, run it against test cases, compare results, tweak, and repeat. Now multiply that work by every prompt template in production and every model candidate worth evaluating. The result is a problem that scales with your ambition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, Amazon Bedrock introduces &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-how.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a tool that optimizes prompts for up to 5 models on Bedrock while comparing original and optimized performance. In this post, we show you how to use Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization to migrate and optimize prompts across multiple models in a single job. This approach replaces days of manual iteration with a guided, metrics-driven workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-how-this-breaks-the-generative-ai-development-lifecycle"&gt;The challenge: How this breaks the generative AI&amp;nbsp; software development lifecycle&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prompt migration and optimization sit at a critical chokepoint in the generative AI development lifecycle. When this step is slow or manual, you feel it in 4 areas:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model lock-in.&lt;/strong&gt; Teams avoid migration because the time and effort of re-tuning is too high, even when newer models would reduce latency and inference cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Underperformance.&lt;/strong&gt; When you write prompts for one model, you don’t use the full capability of another.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regression blindness.&lt;/strong&gt; Without systematic evaluation against ground truth, you can’t distinguish “different” from “worse.” Teams ship degraded outputs without knowing it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Slow iteration cycles.&lt;/strong&gt; Every prompt change requires manual A/B testing, which keeps engineers in evaluation loops instead of building features.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You need a prompt optimizer with built-in evaluations that test multiple models at once. It should also let you guide how prompts and responses change, grounding the optimization in real use cases and data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-architecture-metric-driven-feedback-loop"&gt;The architecture: Metric-driven feedback loop&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-how.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization&lt;/a&gt; works backward from your evaluation metric. Instead of rewriting prompts heuristically, the system operates in a reinforcement learning-style feedback loop, without changing model weights:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You provide a prompt template, example user inputs (text or multimodal), optional ground truth answers, and an evaluation metric.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The optimizer sends your template and inputs to your chosen inference models. It evaluates responses against your metric and then rewrites the prompt.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The loop repeats (evaluate, rewrite, evaluate), iteratively optimizing your prompt against your evaluation metric.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The output is the original and optimized prompts. For each, you get evaluation scores, time to first token (TTFT) per sample, and inference cost estimates at on-demand pricing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture is model-agnostic. You can use your choice of model on Amazon Bedrock. A single job can target up to 5 models at once. You get an optimized prompt for each candidate and a direct comparison of quality, latency, and cost, all from one submission.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how these pieces fit together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-20950-1.png" alt="Process diagram of Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization: the prompt template, input samples, and ground truth flow into the inference models (up to 5 targets). Responses are scored by an evaluation step against your metric, and a prompt rewriter loops back to the models until the metric converges." width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What is TTFT?&lt;/strong&gt; TTFT (time-to-first-token) measures how quickly the model starts responding: the time from sending a request to receiving the first token. It’s a useful proxy for perceived latency, like a chatbot beginning to type.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TTFT captures when the response starts, so pair it with output-token counts for the full latency picture. Since it also reflects real-time infrastructure conditions, read single-run values as a directional signal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization optimizes for your quality metric (the score) and reports TTFT and cost alongside every result. This gives you a complete view of quality, latency, and cost to choose the right prompt with confidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-amazon-bedrock-advanced-prompt-optimization"&gt;Solution: Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock introduces &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-how.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a tool that optimizes prompts for up to 5 models on Bedrock while comparing original and optimized performance. You can use it in two ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model migration.&lt;/strong&gt; Select your current model as a baseline plus up to 4 candidates. You get optimized prompts for each and a side-by-side comparison.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Same-model improvement.&lt;/strong&gt; Select only your current model. You get a better prompt without changing anything else.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="three-evaluation-modes"&gt;Three evaluation modes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You choose one evaluation method per prompt template, and each method serves a different use case. The following table summarizes when to use each one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you provide&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda function&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Concrete metrics (accuracy, F1, ROUGE, JSON match, and so on)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An AWS Lambda function with &lt;code&gt;compute_score&lt;/code&gt; logic&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LLM-as-a-Judge&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Open-ended tasks (summarization, reasoning, generation)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A rubric prompt and a judge model selection&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Steering criteria&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Brand voice, format, safety constraints&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 5 natural language criteria&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you omit all evaluation fields, the system uses a built-in default that combines accuracy, answer completeness, and writing style scoring. We recommend you define your own evaluation metric for the best results. If you have more than one objective to optimize for, you can create a composite metric with weights for each of your criteria in the scoring algorithm. Your metric output must be a single number.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multimodal-support"&gt;Multimodal support&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization supports multimodal inputs: PNG, JPG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, and PDF files referenced through Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) URIs. You can optimize prompts for document analysis, image classification, visual question answering, and other tasks that combine text with visual input.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-console"&gt;Getting started: Console&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section walks you through the console flow step by step: you create a job, select models, upload your input dataset, submit the optimization, and read the results. The Advanced Prompt Optimization landing page lists your jobs and their status and provides a button to start a new one, as shown in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-20950-2.png" alt="Advanced Prompt Optimization landing page in the Amazon Bedrock console, showing the list of optimization jobs and their status." width="800" height="1360"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization landing page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-create-a-prompt-optimization-job"&gt;Step 1: Create a prompt optimization job&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the Amazon Bedrock console, in the left navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;Advanced Prompt Optimization&lt;/strong&gt;. On the landing page, choose &lt;strong&gt;Create prompt optimization&lt;/strong&gt;. The next screenshot shows the creation page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-20950-3.png" alt="Create prompt optimization page, where you select inference models, upload the input dataset, and set the Amazon S3 output location." width="800" height="1187"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Step 1: Create a prompt optimization job&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-select-inference-models"&gt;Step 2: Select inference models&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Model selection&lt;/strong&gt; section, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add model&lt;/strong&gt;. Select your current model as a baseline. To add candidates, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add model&lt;/strong&gt; again and select up to 4 additional models. If you are not migrating, select only your current model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-20950-4.png" alt="Model selection in the Create prompt optimization page: choose up to 5 target inference models, your current model as a baseline plus the candidates you want to evaluate." width="800" height="1726"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Step 2 Select Inference Models&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-prepare-your-input-dataset-jsonl"&gt;Step 3: Prepare your input dataset (JSONL)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your input file uses JSONL format, with one JSON object per line, and each line is one prompt template to optimize. For full details, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-input.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;technical documentation&lt;/a&gt;. The following snippet shows the core schema.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "version": "bedrock-2026-05-14",
    "templateId": "unique-id-for-this-template",
    "promptTemplate": "Your prompt with {{variableName}} placeholders",
    "evaluationSamples": [
        {
            "inputVariables": [
                {"variableName": "value"}
            ],
            "referenceResponse": "Expected output (optional but recommended)"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then add one of the evaluation method fields. For more details and overall guidance, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-evaluation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;technical documentation&lt;/a&gt;. The following snippet shows the AWS Lambda metric option.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "evaluationMetricLambdaArn": "arn:aws:lambda:us-west-2:111122223333:function:my-metric",
    "customEvaluationMetricLabel": "my_score_name"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following snippet shows the LLM-as-a-Judge option.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "customLLMJConfig": {
        "customLLMJPrompt": "Your rubric. Use {{prompt}}, {{response}}, {{referenceResponse}} as placeholders.",
        "customLLMJModelId": "us.anthropic.claude-opus-4-6-v1"
    },
    "customEvaluationMetricLabel": "my_judge_metric"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authoring your judge rubric.&lt;/strong&gt; In your &lt;code&gt;customLLMJPrompt&lt;/code&gt;, reference 3 placeholders with double curly braces: &lt;code&gt;{{prompt}}&lt;/code&gt; (the fully rendered prompt, which is the prompt template combined with the evaluation sample), &lt;code&gt;{{response}}&lt;/code&gt; (the model output), and &lt;code&gt;{{referenceResponse}}&lt;/code&gt; (the ground truth). Advanced Prompt Optimization substitutes these values for each candidate and sample before sending the rubric to the judge model. For details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-evaluation.html#advanced-prompt-optimization-evaluation-llmj" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use an LLM as a judge&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following snippet shows the steering criteria option.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "steeringCriteria": [
        "be concise",
        "use professional tone",
        "avoid speculation"
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-upload-and-submit"&gt;Step 4: Upload and submit&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Prompt templates source&lt;/strong&gt; section, choose &lt;strong&gt;Upload files directly from your local machine&lt;/strong&gt; to upload your JSONL file. Alternatively, choose &lt;strong&gt;Import prompt templates from Amazon S3&lt;/strong&gt; and enter the S3 URI. In the &lt;strong&gt;S3 Output location&lt;/strong&gt; section, enter your S3 output path. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create optimization&lt;/strong&gt; to submit the job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-interpret-results"&gt;Step 5: Interpret results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The results page shows 4 things: evaluation scores before and after optimization (per model), the optimized prompts ready to copy and deploy, TTFT per sample across models, and cost estimates based on on-demand inference pricing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshots show the actual results of the examples we ran for this post: NESTFUL and XSum. We submitted them through the API and SDK (see the next section) using the datasets from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/tree/main/advanced-prompt-optimization" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;tutorial GitHub repo&lt;/a&gt;. Each job used 5 evaluation samples. The console renders the same results whether the job was created from the console or the API. You can use the repo as a reference for the expected output format and reproduce these views in your own account. Your results will vary based on your dataset, prompt complexity, and model choice. The summary view gives you the headline before and after scores. The following screenshot shows the NESTFUL run (function calling, Amazon Nova 2 Lite), where accuracy increased from 0.267 to 0.647.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;

 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;NESTFUL: Summary Table Optimization Results&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The details view shows the original and optimized prompt side by side, along with per-sample metrics and the full token and latency breakdown, as shown in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h2 id="getting-started-api-sdk-boto3"&gt;Getting started: API / SDK (boto3)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also drive the whole workflow with boto3. The following code creates a job that targets two candidate models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock", region_name="us-west-2")

response = bedrock.create_advanced_prompt_optimization_job(
    jobName="my-migration-job",
    modelConfigurations=[
        {"modelId": "us.amazon.nova-2-lite-v1:0"},              # candidate 1
        {"modelId": "us.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0"}, # candidate 2
    ],
    inputConfig={
        "s3Uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/inputs/prompts.jsonl"
    },
    outputConfig={
        "s3Uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/outputs/migration-job/"
    },
)

job_arn = response["jobArn"]
print(f"Job submitted: {job_arn}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="poll-for-completion"&gt;Poll for completion&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A job runs asynchronously, so poll its status until it reaches a terminal state. The following code checks every 60 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import time

while True:
    info = bedrock.get_advanced_prompt_optimization_job(jobIdentifier=job_arn)
    status = info["jobStatus"]
    print(f"Status: {status}")

    if status in ("Completed", "Failed", "Stopped"):
        break
    time.sleep(60)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="download-results"&gt;Download results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the job completes, it automatically writes a JSONL results file to the Amazon S3 output location that you selected when you started the job. The following code uses boto3 to download it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Results land at: s3://output-uri/job-id/advanced_prompt_optimization_results.jsonl
s3 = boto3.client("s3")
job_id = job_arn.rsplit("/", 1)[-1]
result_key = f"outputs/migration-job/{job_id}/advanced_prompt_optimization_results.jsonl"
s3.download_file("amzn-s3-demo-bucket", result_key, "results.jsonl")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="full-example-steering-criteria-with-multimodal-input"&gt;Full example: Steering criteria with multimodal input&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example builds a steering-criteria dataset that pairs a question with a PDF document, writes it to JSONL, and uploads it to Amazon S3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

# Build your input dataset
record = {
    "version": "bedrock-2026-05-14",
    "templateId": "document-qa-v1",
    "promptTemplate": "Answer the question based on the document.\n\nQuestion: {{question}}",
    "steeringCriteria": [
        "State the answer first, then provide supporting evidence from the document.",
        "Use exact figures from the document. Do not round or approximate.",
        "Keep the response under 100 words."
    ],
    "evaluationSamples": [
        {
            "inputVariables": [
                {"question": "What was the total revenue in Q4?"}
            ],
            "inputVariablesMultimodal": [
                {
                    "financial_doc": {
                        "type": "PDF",
                        "s3Uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/assets/q4_report.pdf"
                    }
                }
            ],
            "referenceResponse": "Total Q4 revenue was $4.2 billion, a 12% increase year-over-year."
        }
    ]
}

# Write JSONL (one record per line)
with open("input.jsonl", "w") as f:
    f.write(json.dumps(record) + "\n")

# Upload to Amazon S3 and submit
s3.upload_file("input.jsonl", "amzn-s3-demo-bucket", "inputs/prompts.jsonl")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-considerations"&gt;Key considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you run a job, it helps to know the service limits and a few practices that keep optimization on track. The following table lists the per-job limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limit&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Templates per job&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;10&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Evaluation samples per template&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Models per job&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Text variables per template&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;20&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multimodal files per sample&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Steering criteria per template&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few practices matter more than the limits. Choose a single evaluation method per template, because combining &lt;code&gt;steeringCriteria&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;customLLMJConfig&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;evaluationMetricLambdaArn&lt;/code&gt; is not supported. When you write templates, use double curly braces (&lt;code&gt;{{variable}}&lt;/code&gt;) rather than single braces. Give each key in &lt;code&gt;inputVariables&lt;/code&gt; its own object, so &lt;code&gt;[{"var1": "val"}, {"var2": "val"}]&lt;/code&gt; rather than &lt;code&gt;[{"var1": "val", "var2": "val"}]&lt;/code&gt;. Finally, mix easy and hard examples in your dataset. An all-easy set won’t push the optimizer, and an all-hard set leaves it nothing to learn from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you use LLM-as-a-Judge, 3 judge models are currently available: &lt;code&gt;anthropic.claude-opus-4-6-v1&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6&lt;/code&gt;. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-quotas.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported Regions, models, and quotas&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-metrics"&gt;Results and metrics&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following numbers come from 4 real Advanced Prompt Optimization jobs that we ran for this post in the AWS US East (N. Virginia) Region, each optimizing over 5 evaluation samples. The runs use small sample sets to illustrate the workflow rather than to establish a formal benchmark. Treat the figures as illustrative of the kinds of gains the optimizer surfaces, not as published benchmark results. You can reproduce them with the datasets in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/tree/main/advanced-prompt-optimization" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;tutorial GitHub repo&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A note on naming.&lt;/strong&gt; XSum, NESTFUL, MM-VQA, and IFBench are the &lt;em&gt;example datasets&lt;/em&gt; from the tutorial repo. They are not modes you choose. What you actually pick is the evaluation method (Lambda metric, LLM-as-a-Judge, or steering criteria), described in &lt;a href="#three-evaluation-modes"&gt;Three evaluation modes&lt;/a&gt;. You choose one per prompt template, based on your use case. These 4 datasets simply exercise all 3 methods so you can see each one in action.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;

&lt;h3 id="understanding-the-cost-and-latency-trade-offs"&gt;Understanding the cost and latency trade-offs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Optimized prompts don’t just change quality. They change what you pay and how long you wait. Here’s how to read the numbers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you optimize a prompt, the token counts shift, and that is what drives cost. Bedrock bills per input and output token, with output tokens priced higher than input tokens. The clearest way to read these results is to compare input and output token counts before and after. The 4 runs land in different places, and you stay in control of where.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The optimizer maximizes your quality metric (the score) and reports tokens, TTFT, and cost so you have the full picture for each result. You can also shape the output directly: add a goal to your evaluation criteria that rewards shorter responses, and the optimizer favors prompts that produce them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;XSum delivered a 35% quality gain. The steering criteria (“exactly one sentence, 25 words or fewer”) produced much tighter answers, cutting output tokens from 51 to 28. The optimized prompt carries that guidance, so input tokens grew from 720 to 1,601. You spend more on input to get a higher-quality, more concise answer, a strong trade when short, accurate responses are the goal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NESTFUL invested tokens for accuracy. The function-calling prompt grew from about 1.3K to about 3.8K input tokens as it added explicit decomposition rules and worked examples, and accuracy more than doubled (+143%). You spend more per call for a substantial quality improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MM-VQA produced the largest quality gain for the smallest cost change. ROUGE-L rose from 0.339 to 0.777 (+129%) on document VQA, while input tokens grew only about 7% (30,998 to 33,057) and output tokens edged down from 142 to 136. Here the optimizer sharpened the prompt rather than lengthening it, so a large quality gain came with only a small cost change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;IFBench more than doubled quality. Constraint adherence climbed from 0.413 to 0.890 (+115%) on this LLM-as-a-Judge task. The optimized prompt spends more tokens because it spells out the constraints the model must satisfy. That makes it the most token-intensive of the 4 and the clearest case of investing tokens for quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern across all 4 runs is consistent: optimization delivered large quality gains, and every optimized prompt used more total tokens. The increase ranged from about 7% more (MM-VQA) to several times more (IFBench). When output shrinks, as in XSum and MM-VQA, it softens the increase. The optimizer hands you the exact token counts and scores, so you can run the cost calculation for your own traffic and choose the trade-off that fits your workload. That quantified decision is the real advantage over tuning prompts by hand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reading the cost column.&lt;/strong&gt; Bedrock bills per input and output token, and output tokens are generally more expensive than input tokens. A higher score that adds input tokens can be a great trade for a low-volume, high-stakes task, and a high-traffic endpoint benefits from keeping token growth in check. Because output tokens carry the higher price, you can add reducing output tokens as a goal in your evaluation criteria to keep inference cost down while you optimize for quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-the-results-tell-you"&gt;What the results tell you&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Compared to manual prompt tuning, Advanced Prompt Optimization gives you more than a higher score. Every run produces a quantified, comparable result across quality, latency, and cost in a single job. This turns a prompt that “feels better” into a change you can defend in a design review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few pitfalls are worth watching for as you read your own results:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Do the real cost calculation&lt;/strong&gt;. A higher score that adds input tokens can still be the right call. Because output tokens cost more than input tokens, shorter optimized responses recover part of the added input cost. Compute the actual input-plus-output cost for your request volume so you weigh the quality gain against the true per-call cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Treat TTFT as directional&lt;/strong&gt;. TTFT on Bedrock reflects real-time network conditions, capacity, and time of day, and a longer input prompt tends to raise it. Use the reported TTFT as a rough signal, and confirm latency for your workload with your own repeated measurements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choose your metric deliberately.&lt;/strong&gt; The optimizer maximizes exactly what you measure, so define a metric that reflects real success. Give it signal to climb by mixing easy and hard examples. Cover as much of your expected production distribution as possible in your evaluation dataset so the optimized prompt generalizes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use the right placeholders in LLM-as-a-Judge rubrics.&lt;/strong&gt; Reference &lt;code&gt;{{prompt}}&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;{{response}}&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;{{referenceResponse}}&lt;/code&gt; with double curly braces so the optimizer substitutes the right values and the judge scores each sample accurately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match the evaluation method to your goal.&lt;/strong&gt; Steering criteria guide the rewriter and feed a holistic score, which fits qualitative goals like tone and format. When you need a hard threshold, such as accuracy of 0.9 or higher, choose a Lambda metric for a precise, deterministic score.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; the results presented in this post are single-run results on tutorial datasets (5 samples each). Your results will vary with data distribution, prompt complexity, and model choice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Bedrock Advanced Prompt Optimization, you remove the manual bottleneck from prompt migration and optimization. Instead of days of trial and error, you submit a job, define your success criteria, and get back optimized prompts with quantified improvements across multiple models at once.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow stays the same across use cases. You might migrate to a newer model for cost savings, evaluate a multimodal model for document understanding, or extract more performance from your current setup. In each case you provide your template, your data, and your metric, and let the optimizer close the gap.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-how.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How Advanced Prompt Optimization works&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/reference/bedrock/create-advanced-prompt-optimization-job.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;API reference: CreateAdvancedPromptOptimizationJob&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-bedrock-samples/tree/main/advanced-prompt-optimization" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub: tutorial notebooks for the three evaluation modes&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/advanced-prompt-optimization-input.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Documentation: prepare your input dataset&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;
