			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Authenticate with Private Key JWT using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/authenticate-with-private-key-jwt-using-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-identity/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Swara Gandhi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 29 Jul 2026 16:20:28 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Agents]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Artificial Intelligence]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2f268e77e09a1b42f31a12d09b4d92e8a7e6ec6c</guid>

					<description>This post explains how Private Key JWT client authentication works in AgentCore Identity and reviews the supported grant flows. We then walk through creating an AWS KMS signing key, registering its public key with your identity provider, configuring a credential provider on the AWS Management Console, and reviewing example AWS CloudTrail events that record your agent’s access.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt; now supports Private Key JWT client authentication for agents. With Private Key JWT client authentication, your agents can authenticate to a downstream identity provider’s token endpoint using a signed JSON Web Token (JWT) client assertion instead of a shared OAuth 2.0 client secret. You can register a public key with your identity provider, while the corresponding private key stays in an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Key Management Service&lt;/a&gt; (AWS KMS). To authenticate, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt; uses AWS KMS to sign the assertion and sends the signed assertion to the identity provider, which verifies it using the public key you registered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explains how Private Key JWT client authentication works in AgentCore Identity and reviews the supported grant flows. We then walk through creating an AWS KMS signing key, registering its public key with your identity provider, configuring a credential provider on the AWS Management Console, and reviewing example AWS CloudTrail events that record your agent’s access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-does-it-work"&gt;How does it work?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example illustrates the request flow. Consider a customer-support agent that needs to read a customer’s order history from an internal orders API protected by your identity provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21480-1.png" alt="Request flow showing the agent calling AgentCore Identity, which signs a JWT with KMS, posts the assertion to the identity provider, and returns an access token to the agent" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 1 – Example request flow for a machine-to-machine token request, from the agent’s call through to the downstream API&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the request flow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent calls &lt;code&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/code&gt; on AgentCore Identity to request a token for the orders API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity reads the client ID, KMS key ARN, and signing algorithm from your credential provider. It builds a short-lived JWT client assertion with the required payload claims (plus any additional header or payload claims you configured, such as a key identifier or a certificate thumbprint), and calls &lt;code&gt;kms:Sign&lt;/code&gt; against your KMS asymmetric signing key using the signing algorithm you configured (RS256, PS256, or ES256).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS KMS signs the assertion and returns the signature to AgentCore Identity. The private key never leaves KMS.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity posts the signed assertion to your identity provider’s token endpoint with &lt;code&gt;grant_type=client_credentials&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;client_assertion_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:client-assertion-type:jwt-bearer&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The identity provider verifies the signature against the public key you registered and returns an access token to AgentCore Identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity returns the access token to your agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent calls the orders API with the access token.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The orders API returns the customer’s order history.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="overview-of-supported-grant-flows"&gt;Overview of supported grant flows&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Private Key JWT authentication works for the three grant flows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Machine-to-machine (M2M)&lt;/strong&gt;: the agent acts as itself. There is no human user in the picture. The agent needs to access a resource, for example a background job syncing data, or a service that any customer-support agent can read regardless of who triggered it. The token represents the application/agent identity. Uses the client_credentials grant. The token’s subject is the client itself.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;On-behalf-of (OBO)&lt;/strong&gt;: the agent acts for a specific user, using that user’s existing token. A user has already signed in somewhere and there is an inbound user token. The agent needs to call a downstream API as that user, so their permissions and identity carry through. AgentCore Identity exchanges the inbound user token for a downstream token that represents the user, while still authenticating itself with the client assertion. You can use &lt;a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc8693" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RFC 8693&lt;/a&gt; token exchange or the &lt;a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7523" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RFC 7523 JWT&lt;/a&gt; authorization grant, depending on the identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User-delegated access&lt;/strong&gt;: the agent acts for a user, but the user grants consent interactively first. There is no pre-existing token to exchange. Instead, the user goes through an interactive login/consent (the authorization code / three-legged OAuth flow), approving what the agent can do. After consent, the agent gets a token representing the user. Uses the authorization code grant.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post assumes that you have&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/console/home/?nc2=h_si&amp;amp;src=header-signin" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Management Console access&lt;/a&gt; to &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/kms" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KMS&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A tenant on your identity provider, where you can register a public key for an application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Discovery URL and Client ID that clients use to discover and integrate with your identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the signing algorithm your identity provider requires for Private Key JWT client authentication. Confirm the same algorithm and key spec is supported by &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/private-key-jwt.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;both AWS KMS and AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permissions for each part of the flow: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Create and configure the KMS key – &lt;code&gt;kms:CreateKey&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;kms:PutKeyPolicy&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Export the public key to register with your identity provider – &lt;code&gt;kms:GetPublicKey&lt;/code&gt;. (If your identity provider generates the key pair and gives you the private key material instead, you also need &lt;code&gt;kms:GetParametersForImport&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;kms:ImportKeyMaterial&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/importing-keys-conceptual.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;import it into KMS&lt;/a&gt;.)&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Create the credential provider – &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore-control:CreateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections show how to configure Private Key JWT as the client authentication method using the AWS Management Console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-create-the-kms-signing-key-and-register-the-public-key"&gt;Step 1: Create the KMS signing key and register the public key&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by creating an asymmetric KMS key that AgentCore Identity will use to sign the JWT client assertion. In this example, we create the key in KMS and export the corresponding public key to the Identity provider. However, if your identity provider generates the key pair and gives you the private key material instead, you can import it to KMS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/kms" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS KMS console&lt;/a&gt; in the same AWS Region as your credential provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Customer managed keys, then choose Create key.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Key type, choose Asymmetric.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Key usage, choose Sign and verify.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Key spec, choose a spec compatible with your signing algorithm. This example uses ECC_NIST_P256 with the ES256 signing algorithm.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Next, enter an Alias, and proceed through the key administrator and key usage permission steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the Edit key policy step, add the following statement to the key policy to grant AgentCore Identity permission to use the key, replacing &lt;code&gt;111122223333&lt;/code&gt; with your AWS account ID and &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with AgentCore region in use. The &lt;code&gt;kms:ViaService&lt;/code&gt; condition verifies the key can only be used when the request originates through AgentCore Identity: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Id": "BedrockAgentCoreIdentityPrivateKeyJwtAccess",
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {
        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root"
      },
      "Action": [
        "kms:Sign",
        "kms:DescribeKey"
      ],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "aws:ResourceAccount": "${aws:PrincipalAccount}"
        },
        "StringLike": {
          "kms:ViaService": "bedrock-agentcore-identity.&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;.amazonaws.com"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Finish. On the key’s detail page, note the key ARN. You provide it when you create the credential provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Next, export the public key and register it with your identity provider: 
  &lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;On the key’s detail page, choose the Public key tab and download the public key. KMS provides a DER-encoded public key.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Convert the public key into the format your provider requires (for example, an X.509 certificate for Microsoft Entra ID or a JSON Web Key for Okta) and register it on your application.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-add-an-oauth-client"&gt;Step 2: Add an OAuth client&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the left navigation pane, under Build, choose Identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Outbound Auth section, choose Add Outbound Auth, and then choose Add OAuth client.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21480-2.png" alt="AgentCore Identity console showing the Add Outbound Auth menu with the Add OAuth client option" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 2 – Adding an OAuth client from the AgentCore Identity console&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-choose-private-key-jwt-as-the-client-authentication-method"&gt;Step 3: Choose Private key JWT as the client authentication method&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the Add OAuth Client page, in the Provider configurations section:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Configuration type, choose Discovery URL so AgentCore Identity can retrieve your provider’s configuration automatically. (Choose Manual config if your provider does not publish a discovery endpoint.)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Client authentication method, choose Private key JWT.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21480-3.png" alt="Provider configurations section showing Private key JWT selected as the client authentication method" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 3 – Selecting Private key JWT as the client authentication method&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-provide-the-discovery-url-client-id-kms-key-and-signing-algorithm"&gt;Step 4: Provide the discovery URL, client ID, KMS key and signing algorithm&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Discovery URL, enter the URL where your provider publishes its OpenID Connect configuration (ending in &lt;code&gt;.well-known/openid-configuration&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Client ID, enter the client identifier registered at your identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For KMS key, choose the ARN of your asymmetric KMS signing key. The key must have been created with &lt;code&gt;SIGN_VERIFY&lt;/code&gt; as usage and be in the same Region for it to show up in the list.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note: You can also use a key from another account in the same Region by entering that key’s ARN. Cross-account access requires extra permissions in both accounts. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/key-policy-modifying-external-accounts.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Allowing users in other accounts to use a KMS key.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Signing algorithm, choose the algorithm your identity provider requires for Private Key JWT. It must be compatible with your KMS key spec:&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21480-4.png" alt="Console fields for discovery URL, client ID, KMS key ARN, and signing algorithm selection" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 4 – Providing the discovery URL, client ID, asymmetric KMS key and signing algorithm&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-optional-add-claims-then-create-the-client"&gt;Step 5: (Optional) Add claims, then create the client&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your identity provider requires additional claims in the JWT client assertion, add them here:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under Header claims – optional, choose Add header claim to add provider-specific header claims, such as a key identifier. The reserved keys &lt;code&gt;alg&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;typ&lt;/code&gt; cannot be set.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under Payload claims – optional, choose Add payload claim to add provider-specific payload claims. The reserved keys &lt;code&gt;iss&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sub&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;jti&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;exp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;iat&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;nbf&lt;/code&gt; cannot be set.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Add OAuth Client.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify that the credential provider was created by checking that it appears in the Outbound Auth list.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/28/ML-21480-5.png" alt="Console showing optional header claims and payload claims sections with the Add OAuth Client button" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 5 – Adding optional header and payload claims before creating the client&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-cloudtrail-logs-for-the-agent-access"&gt;Example CloudTrail logs for the agent access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an agent uses the credential provider to fetch a token, the operations are recorded on AWS CloudTrail so you can audit each access. You will see event names such as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GetWorkloadAccessToken&lt;/strong&gt; (event source &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;) – the agent obtains its workload identity token. The &lt;code&gt;requestParameters&lt;/code&gt; record the &lt;code&gt;workloadName&lt;/code&gt;, the &lt;code&gt;resources&lt;/code&gt; block identifies the workload identity, and the returned token is redacted: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
   "eventSource":"bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com",
   "eventName":"GetWorkloadAccessToken",
   "requestParameters":{
      "workloadName":"my-agent-workload"
   },
   "responseElements":{
      "workloadAccessToken":"HIDDEN_DUE_TO_SECURITY_REASONS"
   },
   "resources":[
      {
         "accountId":"111122223333",
         "type":"AWS::BedrockAgentCore::WorkloadIdentity",
         "ARN":"arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:111122223333:workload-identity-directory/default/workload-identity/my-agent-workload"
      }
   ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GetResourceOauth2Token&lt;/strong&gt; (event source &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;) – the agent requests an access token for the downstream resource. The &lt;code&gt;requestParameters&lt;/code&gt; show which credential provider, scopes, and grant flow were used, and the inbound token is redacted. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
   "eventSource":"bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com",
   "eventName":"GetResourceOauth2Token",
   "requestParameters":{
      "workloadIdentityToken":"HIDDEN_DUE_TO_SECURITY_REASONS",
      "resourceCredentialProviderName":"my-private-key-jwt-provider",
      "scopes":[
         "https://graph.microsoft.com/.default"
      ],
      "oauth2Flow":"M2M"
   },
   "resources":[
      {
         "accountId":"111122223333",
         "type":"AWS::BedrockAgentCore::OAuth2CredentialProvider",
         "ARN":"arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:111122223333:token-vault/default/oauth2credentialprovider/my-private-key-jwt-provider"
      }
   ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sign&lt;/strong&gt; (event source &lt;code&gt;kms.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;) – AgentCore Identity signs the JWT client assertion with your KMS key. This is the event that shows Private Key JWT working: &lt;code&gt;userIdentity.invokedBy&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sourceIPAddress&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;userAgent&lt;/code&gt; are all &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;, confirming that AgentCore Identity called &lt;code&gt;kms:Sign&lt;/code&gt; on your behalf. Note that the &lt;code&gt;Sign&lt;/code&gt; event records the KMS signing algorithm name, not the JWT algorithm name you configured. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
   "eventSource":"kms.amazonaws.com",
   "eventName":"Sign",
   "userIdentity":{
      "invokedBy":"bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com"
   },
   "sourceIPAddress":"bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com",
   "userAgent":"bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com",
   "requestParameters":{
      "signingAlgorithm":"RSASSA_PKCS1_V1_5_SHA_256",
      "keyId":"arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:111122223333:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab",
      "messageType":"DIGEST"
   },
   "responseElements":null,
   "readOnly":true,
   "managementEvent":true,
   "resources":[
      {
         "accountId":"111122223333",
         "type":"AWS::KMS::Key",
         "ARN":"arn:aws:kms:us-east-1:111122223333:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab"
      }
   ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid incurring ongoing charges and to remove resources you no longer need, clean up the credential provider and the KMS signing key you created in this walkthrough.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="remove-the-oauth-credential-provider"&gt;Remove the OAuth credential provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the left navigation pane, under Build, choose Identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Outbound Auth section, locate the credential provider you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select the provider, choose Delete, and confirm the deletion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="schedule-deletion-of-the-kms-key"&gt;Schedule deletion of the KMS key&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deleting a KMS key is irreversible and permanent. Once the key is deleted, any data or signatures that depend on it can no longer be produced, so KMS requires you to schedule deletion with a waiting period rather than deleting immediately. Before you schedule deletion, confirm the key is no longer referenced by any credential provider or identity provider registration. If you are unsure whether the key is still in use, consider &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/enabling-keys.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;disabling the key instead&lt;/a&gt;, which stops it from being used while keeping it recoverable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/kms" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS KMS console&lt;/a&gt; in the Region where you created the key.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Customer managed keys, then select the asymmetric signing key you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Key actions, then choose Schedule key deletion.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a waiting period between 7 and 30 days. During this window the key is disabled but can still be recovered by canceling the scheduled deletion.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that you want to schedule the key for deletion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the waiting period elapses, KMS permanently deletes the key. Remember to also remove or rotate the corresponding public key you registered with your identity provider, so it no longer trusts the retired key.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By using Private Key JWT client authentication in AgentCore Identity, you can give your agents a secret-less, auditable way to authenticate to identity providers. The signing key stays on AWS KMS, every signing operation is recorded on AWS CloudTrail, and the same credential provider extends across M2M, on-behalf-of, and user-delegated flows. For end-to-end samples, including Entra and Okta identity-provider registration and both M2M and OBO flows, see the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore samples on &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/05-certificate-based-auth" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Generate Autonomous Business Insights with AI Agent and MCP Servers</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/generate-autonomous-business-insights-with-ai-agent-and-mcp-servers/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sudhanshu Hate]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 29 Jul 2026 15:34:18 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">5399570beb6fc51047d9138d06247cfdb4cf8d63</guid>

					<description>Learn how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore delivers autonomous, cross-system business intelligence through configuration rather than custom code. Using pre-built MCP server connectors, fine-grained access control, and persistent memory, enterprises can query multiple data sources with natural language while enforcing role-based boundaries automatically.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p id="a-monday-morning-problem"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A Monday morning problem&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sarah Chen manages 12 assembly lines and 2,000 machines. Before her 10 AM production review, she needs one answer: &lt;em&gt;Which lines need attention this week?&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Simple question. Painful journey.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;She starts in the IoT dashboard. Line 4’s motor temperature is running 12°C above baseline — has been for three days. Calibration drift or bearing failure? The dashboard shows signals, not causes. So she pivots to the ERP system for maintenance history. Machine 42 had a bearing replaced eight months ago. Warranty says 12 months, but the operating hours log — buried in a separate historian database — shows it’s been running at 130% rated capacity since January.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That context exists nowhere in the IoT dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;She pulls 30-day OEE trends. Line 4’s availability dropped from 94% to 87%. Line 9’s throughput also dipped 6%. Related? They share a coolant loop, but that relationship isn’t modeled in any system. She checks defect rates. Line 4’s scrap jumped 2.3% last Tuesday. Line 9 shows nothing — yet. Is the coolant theory wrong, or is Line 9 just lagging?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;She emails three supervisors. By 11:15 AM, she finally has a confident answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two floors down, Line Supervisor Raj Patel is doing the same exercise for Line 7 — except he can’t access the analytics dashboard. He’s working from a PDF export that’s three days stale. Maintenance Technician Priya Nair just wants to know if the vibration she flagged on Machine 42 last week has gotten worse. The data exists. She doesn’t have credentials to see it. She asks Raj. Raj asks Sarah’s analyst. A CSV arrives four hours later. By then, Priya has moved on to her next work order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three people. Three different access levels. Five disconnected systems. The same underlying data. Hours of manual stitching, context-switching, and waiting—for answers that should take seconds. The data existed but it just couldn’t speak in one voice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-data-rich-insight-poor"&gt;The problem: Data-rich, insight-poor&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The average enterprise runs five to eight operational and analytical systems daily. Each has its own login, its own query interface, and its own access model. When a manager needs a cross-system view — the kind that actually drives decisions — a human being becomes the integration layer. Traditional BI dashboards show what happened yesterday. Single AI assistants can answer isolated questions. But neither can autonomously coordinate across your entire technology stack to deliver contextual, actionable intelligence exactly when needed. Managing complex ETL pipelines, fragmented data stores and access patterns, and multiple AI assistants and agents lead to additional challenges for end users. Custom multi-agent frameworks can theoretically span the full stack, but building one from scratch requires months of engineering: custom connectors, session isolation, memory infrastructure, security code, scaling logic, and orchestration logic — all before the first business question gets answered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Businesses should not need an engineering team just to ask questions about their own data. And the real cost is not engineering time — it is the decisions made on stale, partial information while the right answer was sitting in a system nobody had time to check.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-configuration-not-code"&gt;The solution: Configuration, not code&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore flips the model. Instead of building an autonomous intelligence system from the ground up, you configure one. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore handles orchestration, security, memory, and scaling. You bring your data sources and your business rules. The result is a system where Sarah, Raj, and Priya can each ask natural language questions and get synthesized and personalized answers from across the entire technology stack — without knowing which system provided which piece of the answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model is three steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Connect your existing systems using pre-built MCP server connectors — no custom integration code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define who can see what using plain-English policy rules that Amazon Bedrock AgentCore translates into enforcement logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ask questions in natural language and let Amazon Bedrock AgentCore orchestrate the rest.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture is organized into five layers, each building on the one below. The layered structure is intentional: it separates user-facing intelligence from orchestration, from tool execution, from data access — keeping each layer independently scalable and replaceable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users at the top, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore as the center of gravity, pre-built MCP server connectors in the middle, and the data infrastructure at the foundation. Every user interaction flows through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore — through authentication, policy, memory, and the Agent — before reaching data sources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/16/ML-19801-1.png" alt="Layered architecture diagram showing users at top, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore in the middle with identity, policy, memory, and gateway components, MCP server connectors below, and data infrastructure at the foundation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 1 — Autonomous BI architecture using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture follows a layered design, where a user queries in natural language through Amazon Bedrock for intent classification and response synthesis, then into Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, where the agent orchestrates execution across isolated run-times, a gateway, identity and policy enforcement, persistent memory, and a governed tool registry. Before selecting and routing to data connectors, the agent looks up the Semantic Layer powered by SageMaker Data Catalog, which exposes the right data sources — enabling the agent to understand what data exists across the enterprise and where it resides without hard-coded logic. Equipped with this metadata, the agent routes the query to the appropriate pre-built MCP server connector (equipment, IoT telemetry, supply chain, analytics, or custom low-code), which in turn pulls data from the underlying infrastructure — SageMaker Lakehouse, Redshift, S3 Tables, OpenSearch or Aurora. This separation of metadata discovery from data retrieval means new sources can be on-boarded by registering them in the Data Catalog rather than writing custom integration code, keeping the system extensible through configuration alone. The complete working implementation is available on &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-autonomous-business-insights-with-ai-agent-and-mcp-servers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each layer of the architecture corresponds to a distinct set of capabilities. Below is a guided tour through each component, grounded in what Sarah, Raj, and Priya actually experience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-infrastructure"&gt;Data infrastructure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The data foundation is built on Amazon SageMaker Lakehouse — an open lakehouse architecture fully compatible with Apache Iceberg that unifies data across Amazon S3 data lakes (including S3 Tables), Amazon Redshift data warehouses, and operational data stores connected through zero-ETL integrations. The agent accesses this unified data through a combination of pre-built and custom MCP servers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pre-built MCP servers (ready to configure):&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Redshift MCP server — warehouse queries for supply chain inventory and OEE analytics AWS S3 Tables MCP server — Iceberg-format historical data in S3 Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL MCP server — operational data for equipment registry and maintenance history&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom MCP servers (for services without a pre-built option):&lt;/strong&gt; Custom MCP servers (for services without a pre-built option)- IoT Telemetry MCP server — queries Amazon Timestream for real-time sensor time-series (vibration, temperature, pressure). Built with FastMCP framework. Quality Analytics MCP server — queries Amazon OpenSearch Serverless for defect pattern matching and semantic search across quality inspection documents. Semantic Layer MCP server — queries SageMaker Data Catalog metadata to help the agent discover which data sources are relevant before calling them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building a custom MCP server for a service like Timestream requires only defining the tool interface and delegating to the AWS SDK:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from mcp.server import FastMCP
mcp = FastMCP("IoT Telemetry Server", port=8002)

@mcp.tool(description="Get sensor readings for a specific machine")
def get_sensor_readings(machine_id: int, metric: str = "temperature", days: int = 7) -&amp;gt; str:
    # Validate input, query Timestream via boto3, return JSON
    ...&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once registered, a custom MCP server behaves identically to a pre-built one — the agent discovers its tools through the same MCP protocol and calls them the same way. This means enterprises can start with pre-built connectors for common services and add custom servers incrementally for specialized or legacy systems, without changing the agent or the orchestration logic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-servers-pre-built-connectors-to-your-data"&gt;MCP servers: Pre-built connectors to your data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that defines how AI agents discover and invoke tools. In this architecture, each domain of your business — equipment status, IoT telemetry, supply chain, quality management — is represented by a dedicated MCP server. Each server exposes a set of typed tools: &lt;code&gt;get_equipment_status&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;detect_anomaly&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;check_parts_inventory&lt;/code&gt;. The agent calls these tools, the way a developer calls an API. What makes this practical for enterprises is that you do not build these servers from scratch. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore provides pre-built MCP connectors for common systems: Amazon Redshift, Amazon Aurora, Amazon OpenSearch, and third-party systems through OAuth. For proprietary or custom systems, low-code MCP server templates on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime reduce the effort to configuration rather than development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Connectivity options span the full spectrum of enterprise systems:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Semantic store through Amazon SageMaker Catalog for right data source identification, selection and querying.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Relational databases: Amazon Aurora, RDS. Which bring in the operational data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Streaming services like Amazon MSK for real-time IoT sensor feeds and event streams to SageMaker Lakehouse.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Vector stores: Amazon OpenSearch Service for supporting semantic search, real-time vector embedding with Amazon MSK, and hybrid keyword-vector queries on unstructured data in Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data Warehouses and Lakehouses with Zero ETL features to replicate data from various data stores to Amazon S3 Tables/Amazon Redshift in SageMaker Lakehouse. Thus storing multi-year historical records.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Third-party SaaS: REST or GraphQL APIs via the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway OAuth integration (for example, Salesforce, SAP, ServiceNow).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;MCP servers can run in stateless mode (default, each call is independent) or stateful mode for multi-step operations that need to maintain intermediate results within a session.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp-servers-advantages-and-trade-offs"&gt;MCP servers: Advantages and trade-offs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Advantages&lt;/strong&gt; Loose coupling — each MCP server is deployed independently. Updating the CRM connector does not touch the IoT connector. Domain encapsulation means each server owns its schema and authentication context. Pre-built connectors for AWS-native services mean most enterprises can connect their core data sources within hours. The open MCP standard avoids vendor lock-in — MCP-compatible servers work with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trade-offs&lt;/strong&gt; Custom or legacy systems without a pre-built connector require a low-code template configuration, which adds a one-time setup step. Stateful MCP servers introduce session affinity requirements that need careful planning for horizontally scaled deployments. Each additional MCP server is one more endpoint to monitor and version. Consider using gateway to connect to MCP servers with or without pre-built connectors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-gateway-unified-entry-point-and-intelligent-routing"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway: Unified entry point and intelligent routing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway is the single entry point through which every agent-to-tool call flows. When a new MCP server is registered, the Gateway performs a protocol handshake and indexes its available tools — their names, input schemas, and descriptions. The agent does not need to know the address of a MCP server. It calls tools by name through the Gateway, which routes the request to the appropriate server. The Gateway implements a three-tier caching strategy designed to reduce latency for repeat queries. Organization-scoped cache holds reference data that all users share — equipment catalog records, product lists, threshold values — and refreshes daily. User-scoped cache holds recent query results that are personal to a specific user and respects their access context. The Policy engine determines which responses can be shared across users and which must remain isolated. For Precision Manufacturing, this means that a Plant Manager’s query about overall fleet status can be partially served from cache, while Priya’s query about Machine 42 is scoped to her.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-runtime-isolated-execution-for-every-user"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime: Isolated execution for every user&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime deploys agents to Firecracker microVM environments delivering isolation and security — the same technology that powers AWS Lambda and AWS Fargate — with no servers to manage and automatic scaling. When Sarah submits her query, Runtime spins up a dedicated microVM. Raj’s session runs in a separate microVM. Priya’s in a third. No shared filesystem, no shared memory, no shared networking. When the session ends, the microVM is destroyed. Multi-tenancy is a runtime attribute, not a software convention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In our implementation&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;deploy/agentcore/deploy_all.py&lt;/code&gt; provisions the full stack — Cognito identity, Gateway with Lambda tool targets, Cedar policies, and request/response interceptors. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway then handles session isolation, identity propagation, and policy enforcement automatically, with no changes to the agent code itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-identity-authentication-that-propagates"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity: Authentication that propagates&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity provides robust identity and access management so that agents can access resources or tools either on behalf of users or themselves, with pre-authorized user consent, minimizing the need for custom access controls and identity infrastructure development. Identity integrates with your existing providers (Okta, IAM, Cognito, OAuth 2.0 systems) and propagates user context through the entire call chain using the &lt;code&gt;Mcp-Session-Id&lt;/code&gt; header. Two flows are supported: agent-level access (service-to-service) and user-delegated access (agent acts on behalf of a specific user with their scoped token). In practice for Precision Manufacturing: – Sarah’s token (from Cognito) grants access to all three plants, all 12 lines. – Raj’s token scopes to Plant 2, Line 7 only. – Priya’s token scopes to Machine 41-45. These restrictions come from the IdP. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore propagates them through the call chain and enforces them automatically. If Raj’s scope changes in Cognito, the system reflects it immediately without code changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In our implementation&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;src/identity/models.py&lt;/code&gt; defines three user identities with explicit scopes. In production, these come from Cognito claims that AgentCore propagates automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-policy-authorization-in-plain-english"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Policy: Authorization in plain English&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore integrates with Gateway to intercept every tool call in real time, verifying agents stay within defined boundaries. Teams create policies using natural language that automatically convert to Cedar — the AWS open-source policy language — with automated reasoning that validates policies for completeness before deployment. Policy enforcement happens at the Gateway level, intercepting every tool call before execution. Rules operate across multiple dimensions: user role, geographic scope, data classification, time of day, and specific tool parameters. A policy might read: “Line supervisors can call &lt;code&gt;get_equipment_status&lt;/code&gt; only for lines within their assigned plant.” When Raj asks about Line 4, the Gateway evaluates his identity against this rule and returns a deny decision before the MCP server is ever called. Every decision is logged to AWS CloudTrail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In our implementation:&lt;/strong&gt; Cedar policies deployed to the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway enforce fine-grained access at the infrastructure level — before Lambda tool targets execute. When Raj queries Line 4, the Gateway’s Policy Engine evaluates his JWT claims against the Cedar rules and returns a deny. The MCP server is not contacted. For local development without a deployed Gateway, &lt;code&gt;src/identity/gateway_hook.py&lt;/code&gt; simulates this enforcement using Strands Agents’ &lt;code&gt;BeforeToolCallEvent&lt;/code&gt; hook.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sequence-diagrams-access-control-flow"&gt;Sequence diagrams: Access control flow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagrams illustrate the end-to-end access control flow implemented by Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway. They show how user identity (through JWT claims from Cognito) flows through the REQUEST Interceptor, gets evaluated by the Cedar Policy Engine, and determines whether an MCP tool is invoked or the request is denied — all before data leaves the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="diagram-1-end-to-end-access-control-sequence"&gt;Diagram 1: End-to-end access control sequence&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This UML-style sequence diagram shows the complete lifecycle of a request, from user authentication through Cognito, to the agent’s LLM reasoning, through the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway’s enrichment and authorization layers, and finally to tool execution or denial.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key observations:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Authentication: Cognito issues a JWT with custom claims (role, line_scope, plant_scope, equipment_scope) that encode the user’s data boundaries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enrichment: The REQUEST Interceptor decodes the JWT and injects &lt;code&gt;user_context&lt;/code&gt; into the tool call arguments — the agent/LLM does not see or control this step.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Authorization: Cedar evaluates the enriched request against policies. A &lt;code&gt;permit_all&lt;/code&gt; baseline allows access, while &lt;code&gt;forbid_*&lt;/code&gt; rules check whether the requested parameter falls within the user’s scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Execution vs Denial: If Cedar allows, the Gateway forwards to the Lambda tool target. If Cedar denies, the MCP server is not invoked — avoiding data exposure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Synthesis: The agent receives either tool results or a deny message, and composes a natural-language response. On denial, it explains the boundary and suggests alternatives within scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/16/ML-19801-2.png" alt="End-to-end access control sequence diagram" width="800"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 2 — Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway fine-grained access control sequence diagram&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="three-personas-same-interface-different-access"&gt;Three personas — same interface, different access&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This comparison diagram demonstrates how the same agent interface produces different outcomes for different users. Each row represents a persona with a horizontal left-to-right flow through the system components. &lt;strong&gt;Personas:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sarah Chen (Plant Manager) — Full access. Queries are not denied because her role has the ‘has_full_access’ attribute, bypassing all forbid rules.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Raj Patel (Line Supervisor) — Scoped to Line 7, Plant 2. Attempts to access Line 4 data are denied at the Cedar layer. The Lambda tool is not invoked.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Priya Nair (Maintenance Technician) — Scoped to Machines 41-45. Attempts to access Machine 72 are denied. The agent suggests checking Machine 42 instead.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore components highlighted:&lt;/strong&gt; The purple-shaded region in the diagram encloses the two Amazon Bedrock AgentCore-managed components: the Gateway (with its REQUEST Interceptor) and the Cedar Policy Engine. These operate server-side, external to the agent process, making sure that neither the LLM nor application code can bypass authorization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-136028" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/27/ml-19801-fig3.png" alt="" width="1384" height="1035"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 3 — Same agent, same interface, different data access (Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway enforces Cedar policies at the parameter level)&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-security-properties-illustrated"&gt;Key security properties illustrated&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deterministic Authorization:&lt;/strong&gt; Cedar evaluation is pure logic — same input produces the same output. The LLM cannot influence the policy decision regardless of prompt injection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fail-Secure:&lt;/strong&gt; If the interceptor crashes, context is not injected. Cedar has nothing to permit, so the result is DENY (deny-by-default).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP Server Isolation:&lt;/strong&gt; Denied requests do not reach the MCP server. The tool Lambda is not invoked. Data processing does not occur. Compute is not wasted.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Graceful Degradation:&lt;/strong&gt; The agent receives the deny message as a tool result and intelligently responds: explains what it CAN access, suggests alternatives, and does NOT retry the denied call.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-memory-context-that-persists"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory: Context that persists&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory enables agents to learn and adapt from experiences, building knowledge over time, with support for episodic memory that creates more human-like interactions. Memory operates at two levels: Short-term memory captures turn-by-turn context within a single session. When Priya asks “Show me vibration trends on Machine 42,” then follows with “Compare that to last week,” the second question is understood in context without repeating the machine ID. Long-term memory persists selected insights across sessions. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore automatically extracts and stores preferences, recurring query patterns, and session summaries organized into namespaces:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;User-scoped: Priya’s preferred units, her assigned equipment list.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Team-scoped: The maintenance team’s standard anomaly thresholds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Organization-scoped: Equipment catalog, site codes, product definitions. Retrieval passes through Policy — even if an insight exists in long-term memory, it will not surface to a user who lacks access to the underlying data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In our implementation:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;src/memory/manager.py&lt;/code&gt; provides two memory tiers — &lt;code&gt;SessionMemory&lt;/code&gt; for turn-by-turn context within a conversation, and &lt;code&gt;MemoryManager&lt;/code&gt; for long-term insights organized into user-scoped and team-scoped namespaces. Priya’s long-term memory retains last week’s Machine 42 vibration baseline (3.8 mm/s), so when she asks “has it gotten worse?”, the agent compares against that stored reading without repeating context. In production, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory persists these insights across sessions and enforces policy on retrieval — a user only sees memories derived from data they’re authorized to access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-registry-governed-tool-discovery"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Registry: Governed tool discovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock Agent Registry enables organizations to discover, share, and reuse agents, tools, and agent skills across the organization through a governed catalog with publish-and-approve workflows. New MCP servers enter the Registry in draft state. Administrators review and approve them. Once approved, the agent automatically discovers the new tools on the next invocation — no redeployment needed. The Registry also enables versioning: an updated connector can be staged alongside the current version, tested, and promoted without disrupting live sessions. This governance model means the system grows incrementally. New data sources, new tools, and new connectors are added through a structured publish-and-approve workflow rather than code changes to the orchestration layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In our implementation:&lt;/strong&gt; The Semantic Layer MCP server (&lt;code&gt;src/servers/semantic_layer_server.py&lt;/code&gt;) maintains a registry of data sources — each entry declares its available tools, glossary terms, data origin, and refresh frequency. The agent calls &lt;code&gt;discover_data_sources&lt;/code&gt; first, which keyword-matches against the catalog and returns ranked recommendations. Onboarding a new data source means adding one entry to the &lt;code&gt;DATA_SOURCES&lt;/code&gt; registry — no agent logic changes, no redeployment of other servers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="three-users-one-system-three-different-views"&gt;Three users, one system, three different views&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The real test of any multi-tenant system is not whether it works for one user — it is whether it works correctly for many users simultaneously, with each seeing exactly what they should and nothing more. Here is how the same Amazon Bedrock AgentCore deployment serves Sarah, Raj, and Priya differently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sarah-asks-which-assembly-lines-need-attention-this-week"&gt;Sarah asks: “Which assembly lines need attention this week?”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime creates a microVM for Sarah’s session. The Gateway authenticates her through Okta — role: Plant Manager, scope: all associated assembly lines. The agent classifies the intent as multi-source and operational. It calls the Equipment MCP Server (&lt;code&gt;get_equipment_status&lt;/code&gt;, all plants) and the IoT Telemetry MCP Server (&lt;code&gt;detect_anomaly&lt;/code&gt;, all lines) in parallel. Policy evaluates each call: Sarah’s role permits all of them. Memory recalls that Sarah prefers severity-ranked output. The synthesized response — a ranked list of assembly lines with supporting evidence from both equipment and sensor data — arrives in seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="raj-asks-whats-the-current-status-of-line-7"&gt;Raj asks: “What’s the current status of Line 7?”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Raj’s session runs in a separate microVM. His Okta token scopes him to Plant 2, Line 7 only. The agent routes the same tool calls to the Equipment and IoT MCP servers — but Policy intercepts the calls and enforces the scope restriction. Line 7 data returns. Lines 1 through 6 and 8 through 12 do not. The Gateway’s user-scoped cache serves part of this response from Raj’s recent query history, reducing latency. Raj sees a precise, line-specific status report — not because the system filtered a global result, but because the data was not retrieved for lines outside his scope.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="priya-asks-has-the-vibration-on-machine-42-gotten-worse-since-last-week"&gt;Priya asks: “Has the vibration on Machine 42 gotten worse since last week?”&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Priya is scoped to her assigned equipment. The agent calls the IoT Telemetry MCP Server with &lt;code&gt;machine_id=42&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;metric=vibration&lt;/code&gt;. Policy confirms Machine 42 is in Priya’s assignment list. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory surfaces a summary from her session last week when she asked about the same machine, enabling a week-over-week comparison without Priya having to specify the timeframe. The response includes the trend, the delta, and a maintenance recommendation — all in plain language. Throughout all three interactions: three isolated microVMs, three distinct policy evaluations, three personalized memory contexts. Users can’t see each other’s data. No configuration was needed to achieve this separation — it is the default behavior of the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-makes-this-architecture-different"&gt;What makes this architecture different&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Several aspects of this approach are worth calling out explicitly, because they represent meaningful departures from how enterprise AI systems have traditionally been built.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configuration is the interface for the business.&lt;/strong&gt; A business analyst can define a new access policy in plain English. A data engineer can register a new MCP connector through the Registry UI. Neither requires changes to the orchestration layer or a deployment cycle. The system grows through configuration, not code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The MCP open standard helps prevent lock-in.&lt;/strong&gt; Because MCP servers follow an open protocol, MCP-compatible tools or services can be registered with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore — including open-source connectors, third-party integrations, and future AWS-native services. The orchestration layer does not care where a tool comes from, only that it follows the protocol.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The Registry compounds growth.&lt;/strong&gt; Adding a new MCP server connector does not require changes to the agent, updates to routing logic, or redeployment of existing components. Each new connector increases the system’s intelligence automatically. Engineering effort invested in new connectors benefits everyone who uses the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The system gets smarter over time.&amp;nbsp;&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory accumulates user preferences, recurring patterns, and session summaries. Combined with Gateway caching that recognizes repeated query shapes, response times improve as the system learns what each user typically needs and where to find it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security is a design principle, not an afterthought.&lt;/strong&gt; In most custom multi-agent implementations, data isolation is enforced at the application layer through query filters and role checks scattered across the code base. In Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, isolation is enforced at the infrastructure layer — by the microVM boundary and by the Policy engine intercepting every tool call. A misconfigured agent can’t accidentally return data it should not.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="looking-ahead-extensibility"&gt;Looking ahead: Extensibility&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture described here is a foundation with several natural extension points.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Agent-to-Agent (A2A) protocol, an emerging open standard, will allow Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agents to collaborate with agents running on other infrastructure or in other organizations — enabling supply chain intelligence that spans multiple enterprises without exposing underlying data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Multi-modal interaction is a near-term addition. A voice interface on the factory floor — where Priya cannot type a query while operating equipment — connects to the same Amazon Bedrock AgentCore backend. The answer comes back as spoken language through the same MCP toolchain.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Proactive agents extend the model beyond query-response. Rather than waiting for Sarah to ask which lines need attention, an Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agent monitors incoming IoT data continuously and surfaces an alert when a threshold is crossed — before the 10 AM meeting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;As the Registry accumulates routing history, the agent can learn which tools and data sources each user typically needs and pre-fetch relevant context — reducing time-to-insight further as the system matures.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="summary"&gt;Summary&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It is now 9:58 AM on that same Monday. Sarah types a question into the chat interface on her laptop: “Which assembly lines need attention this week?” By 10:00 AM, she has a ranked list with root-cause context drawn from equipment records, live sensor data, and maintenance history. The production review starts on time. Line 9 is addressed before the shift supervisor even sits down. Raj gets his Line 7 status in the same session, without visibility into Sarah’s view. Priya gets her Machine 42 trend comparison, with the memory of last week’s baseline already loaded. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore — through the combination of MCP server connectors, Runtime isolation, Gateway routing, Identity propagation, Policy enforcement, Memory persistence, and Registry governance — makes this possible as a configured system rather than a custom-built one. The business describes what it wants. The system handles how it works. The future of enterprise intelligence is conversational, autonomous, secure, and available today on AWS. For teams ready to move from data-rich to insight-ready, this is where it starts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="code-samples"&gt;Code samples&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full implementation with Amazon Bedrock Agent Core Cedar policies, Gateway interceptors, MCP tool servers, and Strands agent code is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-autonomous-business-insights-with-ai-agent-and-mcp-servers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample-autonomous-business-insights&lt;/a&gt; repository. You can explore the architecture, run it locally, or adapt it for your own fine-grained access control needs.&lt;/p&gt;
